## Ancient Near East & Egypt (34)

**1. The Fertile Crescent & Mesopotamia's rivers** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Tigris-Euphrates farming birthed first cities · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 2; AP World Unit 0*  
> Celebrate ordinary ingenuity as wonder (moral_vision §2): the unglamorous breakthrough of irrigation and the plow let a few farmers feed a whole city, which is what made cities possible at all. Lead with the strange concrete image (a desert fed by two rivers; the silt left by the flood) and INVERT cool facts into the stem with a knowable answer (§19) — not 'how many rivers' rote. Present on its own terms; this is the literal headwater of human civilization, no condescension toward the ancients (§4).

**2. Sumer and the first city-states** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Ur, Uruk; world's first cities/government · *src: AP World Unit 1; World History Encyclopedia*  
> Wonder Pattern: the answer should be the NAMED thing (Uruk, the walls Gilgamesh supposedly built, the en/lugal kings) or the VIVID first — humanity's FIRST city, first division of labor, first kings. Present Sumer fully on its own terms as a genuine human achievement (moral_vision §4: non-Western/pre-Western civilizations are substantive, not exotic foils). This is where 'the city' itself is invented — frame it with the awe that deserves.

**3. Cuneiform, the first writing** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high  
Wedge marks on clay tablets in Sumer · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 2; AP World*  
> Beauty of mechanism + surprise reversal (moral_vision §2): the first writing wasn't poetry or scripture — it was receipts (beer rations, grain tallies). That reversal is the hook. Wonder Pattern: the answer is the concrete NAMED thing (the wedge/stylus, the clay tablet, the accounting origin), never a 'what is cuneiform' definition lookup (§6 anti-rote). This is the moment record-keeping — and therefore history itself — begins; celebrate it as such.

**4. Ziggurats** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
Stepped temple-mountains of Sumerian cities · *src: AP World; World History textbooks*  
> OBJECT-tier wonder: the stepped temple-mountain, each city's artificial holy mountain rising from the flat plain. Concrete image over abstraction (§5). Treat the religion on its own terms — no smug 'primitive belief' framing (§4); the ziggurat is sacred architecture, the ancestor of the monumental-temple instinct. Keep it to the most picturable specific (the form, Etemenanki at Babylon as the likely Tower-of-Babel echo), not a dry feature list.

**5. The Epic of Gilgamesh** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high  
World's oldest story; king seeks immortality · *src: AP World Unit 1; cultural-literacy canon*  
> This is cultural-literacy canon — the OLDEST surviving story, and the kid should own its named pieces (Enkidu, the search for immortality, the snake stealing the plant, the flood survivor Utnapishtim). Wonder Pattern: answer is the NAMED thing or VIVID action. Note the Flood parallel to Genesis as a fascinating fact stated plainly — NOT as a smug 'the Bible just copied it' reveal (§4 no smug atheist voice, §6 no TIL-secretly-bad framing). Present both stories straight; the resonance is the wonder.

**6. Sumerian inventions (wheel, plow, sail)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Wheel, plow, sail, bronze, math · *src: AP World; World History Encyclopedia*  
> Ordinary ingenuity celebrated (moral_vision §2): real people made real things work, and we STILL use them. Surprise-reversal hook available — the wheel debuted not on carts but on the potter's wheel, or as toys, before transport. INVERT the cool fact into the stem (§19). Wonder Pattern: the answer is the specific NAMED invention, never 'how many inventions did Sumer give us' counting-rote (§6).

**7. The Sumerian base-60 number system** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
60-minute hour, 360-degree circle origin · *src: World History textbooks; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Pure wonder-reversal (moral_vision §2, §6): every time the kid looks at a clock or a protractor, they are using Sumer. Put the everyday object in the STEM ('why does an hour have 60 minutes and a circle 360 degrees?') and let the answer be the Sumerian base-60 origin (§19 invert). This is the kind of 'makes the world feel larger' fact the bank exists for. High delight-per-character.

**8. Sargon of Akkad's empire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
World's first empire-builder conquers Sumer · *src: AP World; World History Volume 1 (OpenStax)*  
> Wonder Pattern at the high tiers: the FIRST empire in human history, and the answer should carry the vivid specific — Sargon the cupbearer who seized the throne, the legend of being set adrift on the river as a baby (a Moses parallel worth noting plainly, not as a gotcha). Story-led opening with the named man and the decisive moment (framework: named person OR specific moment in every stem). Honest about empire as conquest — no romanticizing, no condemning; state what happened (§4).

**9. Hammurabi's Code** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
282 written laws; eye-for-an-eye justice · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World Unit 1*  
> VISION-CENTRAL: the rule of law is part of the Western inheritance the bank celebrates (moral_vision §3.4, framework pillar 1). Hammurabi is the earliest famous written law code — the deep root of 'the law is written down, public, and applies to all.' BUT teach it honestly: its justice was explicitly CLASS-BASED (a noble's eye cost more than a commoner's), and 'eye for an eye' (lex talionis) is a real, nameable principle — set this up as a deliberate contrast to the later Hebrew/Decalogue tradition (§4 honest comparison is not condescension). Answer = the named principle or the vivid specific law (the carved stele, Shamash handing Hammurabi the law), never 'how many laws' rote.

**10. Babylon and the Hanging Gardens** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Nebuchadnezzar's city; a Wonder of World · *src: World History textbooks; Seven Wonders canon*  
> Seven Wonders canon — pure picturable wonder. Wonder Pattern: the NAMED thing (the Hanging Gardens, the Ishtar Gate's blue glazed lions, Nebuchadnezzar). Build in the genuine surprise-reversal (§6): the Hanging Gardens are the one Wonder no archaeologist has ever found — possibly legend, possibly at Nineveh instead — and THAT mystery is the hook, not a flaw to hide. State the Babylonian Captivity of the Hebrews as plain connected fact where relevant (links to the monotheism strand).

**11. The Assyrian war machine** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Iron weapons, siege engines, brutal empire · *src: AP World; Lumen World Civilization*  
> HONEST PRE-MODERN BRUTALITY (moral_vision §4, framework pillar 5 + §5): the Assyrians are the bank's chance to state ancient terror plainly without sensationalism — they pioneered psychological warfare and BOASTED of it in their own royal inscriptions (flaying captives, impaling, deporting whole peoples like the Ten Lost Tribes of Israel). State it as fact, no 'savages' framing, no theatrical reveal — the record is the argument. Pair the brutality with the real innovation honestly (siege engineering, the first true iron army, Ashurbanipal's great library at Nineveh — they preserved Gilgamesh for us). Answer = named specific (Nineveh, Sennacherib, the library, the named tactic).

**12. The Phoenician alphabet** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Trader-sailors invent the letter alphabet · *src: Core Knowledge; Biblical Archaeology Society*  
> VISION-RESONANT: a direct ancestor of the very letters the kid is reading the question in (moral_vision §3.3 Western inheritance; framework pillar 1). Surprise-reversal hook (§6): the alphabet was invented by MERCHANTS who needed fast records, not by priests or poets — practicality drove one of history's greatest gifts. Wonder Pattern: answer is the concrete NAMED thing (the alphabet itself, the handful of letters replacing thousands of signs, the path Phoenician -> Greek -> Latin). Strong 'makes the world larger' fact.

**13. Phoenician sea traders and Tyre's purple dye** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Mediterranean merchants; royal purple from snails · *src: World History Edu; Biblical Archaeology Society*  
> Wonder-reversal gem (moral_vision §2, §19): 'purple = royalty' has a concrete, gross, fascinating origin — thousands of murex sea-snails crushed for a single robe's worth of Tyrian purple, costlier than gold. Put the everyday phrase in the stem, let the snail be the answer. Also honors ordinary ingenuity (the greatest sailors of the ancient world, founders of Carthage). Keep the named specifics (Tyre, murex, Carthage) as the answers, not generic 'they traded a lot.'

**14. The Hittites and ironworking** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Anatolian empire; early iron, chariot warfare · *src: World History textbooks; AP World*  
> Wonder Pattern at high tier: the NAMED specific — the Hittites and the secret of iron, and especially the Treaty of Kadesh with Ramesses II (the oldest surviving peace treaty in the world, a copy of which hangs at the UN). That 'first peace treaty in history' fact is the memorable hook. Story-led on the Kadesh moment (named figures, decisive clash). Present this lesser-known empire substantively on its own terms (§4), not as filler.

**15. The ancient Hebrews and monotheism** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
First lasting belief in one God · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; World History textbooks*  
> VISION-SUPREME for this strand. moral_vision §3.4 names JERUSALEM — covenantal ethics and the dignity of the individual person (imago Dei) — as one of the four headwaters of the Western tradition (Athens/Jerusalem/Rome/Canterbury), and §1 puts it in the master lineage. This is arguably the single most consequential idea on the whole strand: the first lasting monotheism, and with it the radical claim that every person bears the image of God (which the bank elsewhere credits as underwriting universal human dignity, individual rights, and the abolition of slavery). Treat the tradition straight and on its own terms — NO smug atheist 'people used to believe' voice AND no smug believer 'and that's the true God' advertising (§4 both banned). NO attribution-rote ('who wrote the Torah'); tell the STORY (the covenant, the Exodus, the Decalogue as a law tradition, Babylonian exile and return). Answer = the NAMED idea or vivid specific. Should be DECOMPOSED into multiple cells given its weight — see injected topics.

**16. Cyrus the Great and the Persian Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Largest ancient empire; freed the Hebrews · *src: AP World; World History textbooks*  
> VISION-MANDATED: Cyrus freeing the Hebrews from Babylonian captivity to rebuild the Temple is a named, load-bearing event in the Western/Jerusalem inheritance (moral_vision §3.4) and a striking early instance of imperial tolerance. The Cyrus Cylinder is sometimes called the first 'human-rights'-flavored decree — present this as the genuinely interesting artifact it is WITHOUT anachronistically overselling it as a modern bill of rights (honest, not preachy, §4). Wonder Pattern: answer = the named man, the largest empire yet built, or the specific deed (freeing the exiles). Story-led on Cyrus's character and the decisive act.

**17. The Persian Royal Road and administration** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
1,500-mile road; satraps govern provinces · *src: AP World; World History Volume 1*  
> Ordinary ingenuity + governance-as-engineering (moral_vision §2). Wonder hook available via the famous Herodotus line about the royal couriers ('Neither snow nor rain nor heat nor gloom of night' — the unofficial motto echoed by the US Postal Service) — put the modern echo in the stem, let the Persian road/relay be the answer (§19). Define 'satrap' inline on first use (framework T4: specialist terms glossed). Answer = the named specific (the Royal Road, satraps, the relay couriers), not abstract 'good administration.'

**18. The Nile River and Egyptian geography** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Annual flood feeds the desert kingdom · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; AP World*  
> Beauty of mechanism as wonder (moral_vision §2): WHY Egypt existed — the Nile flooded on a near-perfect annual schedule, laying down black silt (the Egyptians called their land Kemet, 'the Black Land') in a thin green ribbon through absolute desert. The 'gift of the Nile' (Herodotus) phrasing is the hook. Concrete image over abstraction (§5). INVERT the cool specific into the stem rather than asking 'what river' rote (§6, §19). Present Egypt fully on its own terms as a magnificent civilization (§4).

**19. The pharaohs and Egyptian god-kings** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high  
Divine rulers of a unified Egypt · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; AP World*  
> Take the theology seriously on its own terms — the pharaoh as living god (Horus on earth, becoming Osiris in death) is a genuine belief system, not 'they were tricked into worshipping a man' (moral_vision §4 no smug voice, no condescension toward the past). Wonder Pattern: the answer is the NAMED thing (the double crown uniting Upper and Lower Egypt, the title pharaoh = 'great house,' the god-king concept). Story-led with named pharaohs at the higher tiers. Honest about absolute power without editorializing.

**20. Hieroglyphics** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Egypt's sacred picture-writing on stone · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Cultural-literacy canon, picturable wonder. Wonder Pattern: the answer is the concrete NAMED thing (hieroglyphs = 'sacred carvings,' the cartouche oval that ringed a royal name, papyrus and the scribe's status). Surprise-reversal available (§6): the script was fully readable for millennia, then NOBODY on earth could read it for ~1,400 years until the Rosetta Stone — set up that mystery. Avoid 'what is a hieroglyph' definition-rote; lead with the scene (a scribe, a temple wall) and ask for the vivid specific.

**21. The Rosetta Stone** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Key that decoded hieroglyphics; Champollion · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; major encyclopedias*  
> Courage/genius in pursuit of truth (moral_vision §2, framework 'drama of the great minds' pillar): the SAME decree carved in three scripts let Champollion crack a code silent for 1,400 years — he reportedly ran through the streets shouting 'Je tiens l'affaire!' before collapsing. Story-led on the decipherment moment. Wonder Pattern: answer = the named key/insight (three scripts, the trilingual stone, Champollion's breakthrough), NOT a 'who decoded it' bare-attribution stem (§6) — make the move the cracking of the code, with the name in context. The Greek third script was the bridge — that's the satisfying mechanism.

**22. The Great Pyramids of Giza** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high  
Khufu's tomb; last standing Ancient Wonder · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; Seven Wonders canon*  
> Flagship wonder of the whole strand — the ONLY surviving Ancient Wonder, the tallest human-made structure on Earth for ~3,800 years. Wonder Pattern: the answer is the NAMED/vivid specific (Khufu/Cheops, the Great Pyramid, ~2.3 million blocks, the near-perfect cardinal alignment). INVERT the staggering numbers into the stem with a knowable answer (§19) — a SINGLE awe-number in a stem is fine, but don't make 'how many blocks' the test. Honor the engineering as genuine human achievement; treat the 'aliens built it' trope as the answerable myth it is, not by sneering but by celebrating the real ingenuity.

**23. The Great Sphinx** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low  
Giant lion-bodied, human-faced guardian statue · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3*  
> OBJECT-tier picturable wonder (moral_vision Wonder Pattern): the lion's body with a human (pharaoh's) face, carved from a single limestone outcrop, guarding Giza. Concrete image (§5). Genuine mystery hooks available (the missing nose, the riddle of who it portrays and exactly when) — present as open questions, the curiosity-over-completion virtue (§2), not manufactured drama. Keep the answer to the named/concrete specific.

**24. Mummification and the afterlife** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Preserving bodies for eternal life · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; AP World*  
> Take the belief seriously (moral_vision §4): mummification was sophisticated applied chemistry in service of a coherent theology of eternal life — not 'creepy ancient superstition.' Wonder Pattern: the answer is the vivid NAMED specific (natron salt drying the body, canopic jars holding the organs, the brain pulled out through the nose and discarded while the heart was kept for judgment). Strong gross-but-true delight factor — these are exactly the details kids remember. INVERT a striking specific into the stem (§19); avoid 'what is mummification' rote.

**25. Egyptian gods and the Book of the Dead** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Ra, Osiris, Anubis; weighing of heart · *src: AP World Unit 1; World History textbooks*  
> Mythology told straight and on its own terms (moral_vision §4, parallels the bank's theology-strand respect for Greek/Norse myth). Wonder Pattern: the answer is the NAMED thing or VIVID image — the weighing of the heart against the feather of Maat (Ma'at), Anubis the jackal-headed embalmer, Osiris murdered and reassembled, Ra sailing the sun across the sky, the monster Ammit waiting to devour the unworthy heart. These named figures and the heart-weighing scene are premium memorable content. No smug 'primitive myth' framing; present it as the rich imaginative system it is.

**26. Tutankhamun and his tomb** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Boy-king; Carter's 1922 golden discovery · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Drama of discovery (moral_vision §2, framework 'iconic moments' pillar): Howard Carter peering in by candlelight, asked 'Can you see anything?', answering 'Yes — wonderful things.' That line is the hook. Story-led on the 1922 moment. Wonder Pattern: answer = the named/vivid specific (the intact tomb, the solid-gold death mask, the boy-king dead at ~18). Note the 'curse of the pharaohs' as the press legend it was — present plainly, neither endorsed nor sneered at. Tut matters precisely because he was MINOR yet his was the one tomb found unlooted — that reversal is worth surfacing.

**27. Hatshepsut, the female pharaoh** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Woman who ruled Egypt as king · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 3*  
> Genuine wonder, handled WITHOUT modern grievance framing (moral_vision §6 no advocacy/anachronism; §4 honest, not preachy): Hatshepsut took the FULL kingship — including the ceremonial false beard and male regalia — and ran a prosperous, peaceful reign (the Punt trade expedition, her great mortuary temple). The fascinating dark twist is that her successor Thutmose III later tried to erase her name from the monuments — a real, datable act, presented as fact, not as a feminist morality tale. Wonder Pattern: answer = the named/vivid specific (the false beard, the erasure, Punt). State it straight and let the kid be amazed.

**28. Ramesses the Great** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Long-reigning warrior pharaoh; Abu Simbel, Kadesh · *src: World History textbooks; AP World*  
> Story-led on the named figure and decisive moment (framework requirement). Wonder Pattern: the answer is the vivid NAMED specific — the four colossal seated statues at Abu Simbel, the ~66-year reign, the Battle of Kadesh (which he boastfully carved as a great victory though it was really a draw — an honest, fun media-spin fact to surface, §4). Kadesh links to the Hittite topic via the first peace treaty. Note plainly that he is the pharaoh many scholars associate with the Exodus narrative — stated as the connection it is, no doctrinal claim either way (§4 both-voices-banned).

**29. Cleopatra and the end of ancient Egypt** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high  
Last pharaoh; Caesar, Antony, Rome · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; AP World*  
> Bridge to the Rome strand — celebrate the genuine wonder while correcting the pop-culture myth (moral_vision §6 surprise-reversal): Cleopatra was GREEK (a Ptolemy, descended from Alexander's general), reportedly spoke ~9 languages, and was the first of her dynasty to bother learning Egyptian — her power was political brilliance, not just seduction. Story-led on the named figures and the fall (Caesar, Antony, Actium, the asp). Wonder Pattern: answer = the named/vivid specific. Honest about the end of 3,000 years of pharaohs as Egypt becomes a Roman province — stated plainly.

**30. Egyptian achievements: calendar, medicine, geometry** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
365-day calendar, surgery, pyramid math · *src: World History textbooks; Education.com Middle East*  
> Ordinary ingenuity + beauty of mechanism (moral_vision §2): the 365-day solar calendar we still basically use, setting bones and stitching wounds (the Edwin Smith papyrus is the oldest surgical text), and the rope-and-knot geometry that squared the pyramids. INVERT the still-with-us specifics into the stem (§19) — 'why does our year have 365 days?' with the Egyptian origin as answer beats a generic 'name an Egyptian achievement.' Real practical science we still build on — present as the genuine achievement it is, no condescension (§4).

**31. The Ten Commandments and the Decalogue as a law tradition** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Moses, Sinai, and a moral law given to a whole people · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED DECOMPOSITION of the Hebrew strand (moral_vision §3.4 Jerusalem as covenantal-ethics headwater; §1 master lineage). The Decalogue is the deep root of the Western moral-law tradition and a deliberate CONTRAST to Hammurabi's class-based, transactional code: a single moral law binding king and commoner alike, given to the whole people rather than dictated by a god-king for his own glory. Tell the STORY (Moses, Sinai, the tablets) — never 'who wrote the law' attribution-rote (§6). Present on its own terms, straight, no smug believer OR atheist voice (§4 both banned). Answer = the named idea or vivid specific (the two tablets, the covenant, the contrast with lex talionis). This pairs directly with the Hammurabi cell to teach two rival visions of law.

**32. The Exodus and the birth of a covenant people** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Bondage in Egypt, the escape, and a people defined by a story · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED DECOMPOSITION (moral_vision §3.4 Jerusalem; framework pillar 1 Western tradition unbroken). The Exodus narrative is among the most culturally consequential stories in human history — it shaped Western ideas of liberation from bondage and was explicitly invoked by the American founders, the abolitionists, and Martin Luther King ('I've been to the mountaintop'). Tell the STORY on its own terms (Moses, Pharaoh, the plagues, the parting sea, 40 years wandering) as the foundational narrative of a people — NOT as a history-vs-archaeology debate and NOT with a smug 'did it really happen' frame (§4, §6 no TIL-reveal; the bank treats the tradition's own story straight). Answer = the named/vivid specific. Links forward to Ramesses (the traditionally-associated pharaoh).

**33. Child sacrifice in the ancient Near East (the Tophet)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Canaanite/Carthaginian infant sacrifice to Baal and Moloch · *src: vision-injected*  
> HONEST PRE-MODERN BRUTALITY, stated plainly (moral_vision §4; framework pillar 5 + forbidden-list item 4 'sanitizing... is a fail'). Archaeology of the Carthaginian Tophet and ancient sources (and the Hebrew Bible's own horror at it) record infant sacrifice to Baal/Moloch among some Canaanite and Phoenician/Carthaginian peoples. State it as the historical fact it is — NO sensationalism, NO 'savages' framing, NO theatrical reveal; the record is the argument (framework §5). This is also the dark counterpoint that sharpens WHY the Hebrew prohibition of child sacrifice (and the Akedah / binding-of-Isaac story, where the sacrifice is HALTED) was experienced as a moral revolution. Answer = the named specific (Tophet, Moloch/Baal, Carthage).

**34. The Cyrus Cylinder and early imperial tolerance** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Cyrus's clay decree; conquered peoples allowed their own gods · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-RESONANT (moral_vision §3.4 — Cyrus's freeing of the Hebrews; §4 honest, not preachy). The Cyrus Cylinder is a real baked-clay artifact recording Cyrus's policy of restoring deported peoples and their temples — including authorizing the rebuilt Temple in Jerusalem. Present it as the genuinely striking early experiment in imperial tolerance it was, while being HONEST that it was Persian royal propaganda and NOT a modern human-rights charter despite sometimes being marketed as 'the first declaration of human rights' (steel-man the claim, then state the sober scholarly correction — §3.9/§6 anti-anachronism, anti-advocacy). Answer = the named artifact or the concrete deed (restoring the exiles/temples).


## Ancient Greece (39)

**1. Homer and the Trojan War (Iliad and Odyssey)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Homer's epics; Achilles, Hector, Odysseus · *src: Core Knowledge G6; AP World; Hirsch cultural literacy*  
> Pillar 1 headwater: the West's first great stories, the foundation of Greek (and Western) education. Lead with the vivid scene and the named thing the cool fact IS the answer (Achilles' heel, Hector dragged round the walls, the rage that opens the Iliad, Odysseus and the Cyclops, the bow only he can string). Tell the stories straight on their own terms; no smug 'people used to believe' voice. Names/dates of composition go in context, never as the test (anti-rote).

**2. The Trojan Horse and the fall of Troy** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=high  
The wooden horse ruse ends the war · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural literacy canon*  
> Ordinary ingenuity / the memorable trick the kid should recognize plus the living idiom ('Trojan horse'). Cool fact is the answer: the hollow wooden horse, Odysseus' scheme, Laocoon's warning, Sinon the planted liar. VIVID ACTION over label. Cultural-literacy cornerstone every child should own.

**3. Greek mythology: the Twelve Olympians** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Zeus, Athena, Poseidon and Mount Olympus · *src: Core Knowledge G2/G6; encyclopedias*  
> Athens' cultural inheritance that saturates Western art, language, and the night sky present the myths straight, no condescension toward ancient belief (moral_vision §4). Anchor on NAMED THINGS and domains (Athena's owl and aegis, Poseidon's trident, Zeus' thunderbolt) and the stories, not on rote 'who is god of X' definition-lookup. Note: doctrinal/worship treatment of the Greek pantheon lives in the THEOLOGY bank; here the angle is myth as Western cultural literacy.

**4. Greek myths and heroes (Heracles, Theseus, Perseus, Icarus)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Labors, Minotaur, Medusa, melted wings · *src: Core Knowledge G6 Greek myths; cultural literacy*  
> The West's shared moral storybook (celebrate section). VIVID ACTIONS are the answers: Heracles strangling the Nemean lion, Theseus and the thread through the labyrinth, Perseus using the polished shield to behead Medusa, Icarus' wings melting near the sun. Each carries a transmissible lesson by example (hubris, courage, cunning) show virtue, don't preach it.

**5. Sparta: the agoge and warrior society** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Boys taken at 7; militarized state · *src: Core Knowledge G2/G6; AP World city-states*  
> The classic militarized polis and Athens' foil. Present on its own terms AND honestly: this is the honest-record principle (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5 / moral_vision §4) the same bank that celebrates Athenian liberty states plainly Sparta's dark side at higher tiers (newborns inspected and exposed at the Apothetae, helots held in permanent terror, the krypteia secret-police killings, the black broth). Vivid specifics (the agoge, encouraged stealing-but-not-getting-caught, the laconic 'with your shield or on it') are the cool facts; do not romanticize the regime.

**6. Athens vs. Sparta: two rival city-states (the polis)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Democracy-city vs. warrior-city compared · *src: Core Knowledge G6; AP World foundations*  
> Core framing for all Greek political history and a natural seen/unseen contrast the bank's tradition draws openly: Athens' open society of citizen self-rule, trade, philosophy, and theater vs. Sparta's closed warrior-collective. Steel-man each polis (no strawman Sparta) but the bank does not pretend the two regimes were equally fertile for human flourishing honest comparison is not condescension (moral_vision §4).

**7. Birth of Athenian democracy (Cleisthenes, Solon, ostracism)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
First citizen self-rule; banishment by potsherd · *src: Core Knowledge G6 'beginnings of democratic government'; AP*  
> HIGH-PRIORITY Pillar 1: Athens literally invented the word and practice of democracy the first link in the chain that runs to Philadelphia (moral_vision §1, §3). Concrete handles are the answers: Solon cancelling debt-slavery (the seisachtheia), Cleisthenes' reorganized tribes, ostracism by scratching a name on a potsherd (ostrakon). Honest about its limits (citizenship excluded women, slaves, metics) stated plainly, not theatricalized.

**8. Battle of Marathon (490 BC) and the runner Pheidippides** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Athens repels Persia; legendary 26-mile run · *src: Core Knowledge G2; AP World; military canon*  
> Explicitly named in HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1 ('Marathon'). Free-Greeks-beat-the-empire moment that the bank frames as why the survival of Greece shaped the West (moral_vision civilizational stakes). Cool fact is the answer: the run that names the modern race, the Athenian hoplite phalanx charging the Persian line. The death-on-arrival legend can be flagged as legend in context.

**9. Thermopylae (480 BC): Leonidas and the 300 Spartans** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Last stand in the narrow pass · *src: Core Knowledge G2 'heroic behavior'; military canon*  
> Named FIRST in HISTORY_FRAMEWORK's marquee list ('Thermopylae') the iconic free-men's sacrifice. NAMED THINGS / QUOTES are the answers: 'Molon labe' (come and take them), 'then we will fight in the shade,' the goat-path betrayal by Ephialtes, the Thespians and Thebans who also stood. Courage-at-cost taught by example (celebrate section). Top-tier wonder hook.

**10. Battle of Salamis (480 BC): Themistocles' naval trap** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Greek triremes crush Persian fleet in strait · *src: AP World; great-battles military canon*  
> Pillar 3 (great battles) + Pillar 1 stakes: the naval turning point that saved Greek freedom. Cunning-as-answer: Themistocles luring Xerxes' fleet into the narrow strait where numbers became a trap, Xerxes watching from his throne on the shore. The 'wooden walls' oracle reinterpreted as ships is a great surprise-reversal hook.

**11. Battle of Plataea (479 BC): Persia driven out** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Final land battle ends the invasion · *src: AP World Persian Wars; military canon*  
> Completes the Persian Wars arc the decisive land victory (Pausanias, the combined Greek hoplite army) that finished what Marathon and Salamis began. Less famous, so weight medium-low; valuable for closing the 'why Greek freedom survived' story. Specifics over labels.

**12. Xerxes' invasion and the Greco-Persian Wars overview** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Persian Empire vs. free Greek cities · *src: Core Knowledge G6 'Persian Wars'; AP World*  
> Frames the civilizational stakes the bank cares about: a few free city-states against the largest empire on earth, and what their survival meant for everything downstream (moral_vision §3, Western inheritance). Present Persia on its own terms (a sophisticated empire, not a cartoon horde no exotic-foil framing per §4); the contrast is liberty-vs-autocracy, drawn honestly. Vivid hooks: Xerxes whipping the Hellespont, the pontoon bridge of boats, the army 'that drank rivers dry.'

**13. Pericles and the Golden Age of Athens** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
5th-century cultural and democratic peak · *src: Core Knowledge G6 'Pericles and the Golden Age'*  
> The classical high point of Pillar 1 democracy, drama, philosophy, and architecture cresting together. The Funeral Oration (via Thucydides) on citizen self-government is a foundational text of the Western political tradition quote it. Honest, not hagiographic: the same Pericles steered the empire toward the Peloponnesian War. Cool specifics (rebuilding the Acropolis, the Delian League treasury moved to Athens) as answers.

**14. The Parthenon and Phidias (the classical ideal in art)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Acropolis temple; 40-foot Athena statue · *src: Core Knowledge G6 'classical ideal'; art canon*  
> Pillar 1's 'Western art + architecture threaded through every tier' the framework explicitly names 'Parthenon (Phidias) and Doric/Ionic/Corinthian orders.' Defining monument of Western architecture and proportion. The beauty-of-mechanism angle: the entasis (columns subtly curved so they LOOK straight), the gold-and-ivory (chryselephantine) 40-foot Athena. Objects/named things as answers.

**15. Greek architecture: Doric, Ionic, Corinthian orders** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
The three classical column styles · *src: AP Art History; Hirsch cultural literacy*  
> Explicitly named in HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1 ('Doric/Ionic/Corinthian orders'). Column vocabulary reused for 2,500 years of Western building right down to courthouses and bank facades. Avoid pure definition-lookup (anti-rote): anchor on the recognizable visual tell (the acanthus-leaf Corinthian capital, the Ionic scroll/volute, the plain Doric) and where the kid has SEEN them.

**16. Greek theater: tragedy and comedy (Sophocles, the amphitheater)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Masks, open-air theaters, drama festivals · *src: Core Knowledge G6; AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Athens invented Western drama (celebrate section: 'the inheritance of Athens'). Give the words their living force: 'tragedy,' 'comedy,' 'catharsis,' 'protagonist,' 'chorus' all Greek. Concrete handles as answers: the masks, the Theater of Dionysus carved into the hillside, the City Dionysia competition, Sophocles' Oedipus, Aristophanes' satire. Story-led, not a vocabulary drill.

**17. The Peloponnesian War (Athens vs. Sparta, 431-404 BC)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Long war that broke Athens' power · *src: Core Knowledge G6; AP World 'causes/effects'*  
> Greece's self-inflicted civil war and the birth of critical history: Thucydides' founding work of analytical history, written by a participant-general who told the truth about his own side (a virtue-by-example case honesty at cost). Honest, not preachy: the plague of Athens, the Melian Dialogue ('the strong do what they can'), the Sicilian Expedition disaster as the cautionary arc. Higher-tier weight.

**18. Socrates' trial and death by hemlock (399 BC)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Philosopher chooses poison over exile · *src: Core Knowledge G2/G6 philosophers; cultural literacy*  
> HIGH: the martyr of free inquiry and origin of the Socratic method exactly the 'courage in pursuit of truth' the bank exists to transmit (celebrate section; he sits in the lineage of Galileo/Hypatia/Solzhenitsyn as a truth-teller who paid). Drama-of-minds at full strength: 'the unexamined life is not worth living,' drinking the hemlock rather than fleeing or recanting, 'I know that I know nothing.' Quote/named-thing as answer. Coordinate with the PHILOSOPHY bank (method, not attribution-trivia) but the trial/death is rightly history.

**19. Plato, the Academy, and the Republic** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Socrates' student; first university; ideal state · *src: Core Knowledge G6 philosophers; AP*  
> Pillar 1: the most influential philosopher in Western thought, founder of the Academy (root of 'academic'). Concrete handles as answers: the Allegory of the Cave (prisoners mistaking shadows for reality), the philosopher-king. Honest seriousness, not endorsement note in context that the Republic's ideal state is also history's first sketch of a closed/controlled society later critics (the bank's anti-totalitarian lens, e.g. Popper) read as a warning present the idea straight, no strawman. Doctrinal philosophy lives in the PHILOSOPHY bank; here it is Western intellectual history.

**20. Aristotle, the Lyceum, and tutoring Alexander** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Logic, science, tutor of a conqueror · *src: Core Knowledge G2/G6 philosophers; AP World*  
> Explicitly named in HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1 ('Aristotle'). Founder of formal logic and empirical biology the method underneath the entire Scientific Revolution downstream and the bridge to Alexander's story. Per moral_vision §4: do NOT mock him for being wrong (heart as seat of thought, etc.) premodern people were as smart as you, their world was different. Cool facts as answers: walking-and-teaching Peripatetics, dissecting sea creatures, syllogistic logic, tutoring the boy who would conquer the world.

**21. Diogenes the Cynic and the barrel** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Lived in a jar; 'stand out of my sun' · *src: Cultural literacy; encyclopedia philosophy canon*  
> Memorable rebel-philosopher whose VIVID ACTIONS are the answers: living in a clay jar, carrying a lamp in daylight 'looking for an honest man,' telling Alexander the Great 'stand out of my sun.' A delightful drama-of-minds hook that teaches the Cynic stance by image, not by jargon. Present straight; no condescension.

**22. Pythagoras and his theorem** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Right-triangle rule; number-mystic cult · *src: AP World; Hirsch; math history canon*  
> The name every kid meets in geometry plus the wonder of the secret brotherhood. Beauty-of-mechanism + surprise-reversal: the cult that thought numbers ran the cosmos, the (legendary) drowning of the man who revealed irrational numbers, the ban on eating beans. Cool fact as answer, not a formula-definition drill. Coordinate with MATH bank for the theorem itself; here it's the human/historical story.

**23. Archimedes: 'Eureka!' and the siege of Syracuse** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Buoyancy, war machines, 'don't disturb my circles' · *src: AP World; science/math history; cultural literacy*  
> EXPLICITLY named as a model in BOTH docs (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK T1 wonder list 'Archimedes don't disturb my circles'; moral_vision quotes 'don't disturb my circles' as exemplar). The greatest ancient scientist drama-of-minds at full power. NAMED QUOTES/ACTIONS as answers: leaping from the bath shouting 'Eureka!', the claw and burning-mirrors defending Syracuse, the last words to the Roman soldier 'do not disturb my circles.' A flagship Pillar-1/Pillar-6 topic across every tier.

**24. Eratosthenes measures the Earth's circumference** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Used shadows and geometry to size the globe · *src: AP World; science history canon*  
> Pillar 1 + 'beauty of mechanism': proved the Earth round AND measured it with nothing but a stick, a shadow, and geometry the ancient world already knew the Earth was a sphere (puncture the flat-earth-myth, a clean surprise-reversal). Cool fact is the answer: the noon sun straight down a well at Syene vs. the shadow-angle at Alexandria. The chief librarian of Alexandria doing real science.

**25. Euclid and the Elements (geometry)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
The geometry textbook used for 2,000 years · *src: AP World; math history; Hirsch*  
> Pillar 1: the foundation of logical PROOF the deductive method (axioms to theorems) that underlies Western mathematics and arguably Western rigor itself, the most-used textbook in history after the Bible. Reframe away from 'who wrote' rote (anti-rote): anchor on the idea (building certainty step by proven step) and the legend 'there is no royal road to geometry' said to Ptolemy. Coordinate with MATH bank.

**26. Hippocrates and the Hippocratic Oath** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Father of medicine; 'do no harm' · *src: AP World; cultural literacy; science canon*  
> Pillar 1: separated medicine from magic/superstition and gave doctors an ethical code still sworn today living inheritance. The named-thing/quote is the answer: 'first, do no harm' (primum non nocere), the oath itself. The surprise-reversal hook: looking for natural causes of disease instead of blaming angry gods. A virtue (integrity in a profession) taught by example.

**27. Herodotus, 'the Father of History'** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
First true historian; chronicled Persian Wars · *src: AP World; cultural literacy canon*  
> Pillar 1 AND meta-mandated: history itself is a Greek invention Herodotus turned the past from myth into INQUIRY (the literal meaning of his word 'historia,' 'inquiry'). Naturally self-referential for a history bank. Reframe past the 'father of history' label (anti-rote): anchor on what he DID gathering eyewitness accounts and rival versions, traveling to see for himself, telling the Persian Wars story. Pair honestly with Thucydides' more skeptical method.

**28. The ancient Olympic Games at Olympia** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Pan-Greek athletic festival; sacred truce · *src: Core Knowledge G2 'origins of the Olympics'; AP*  
> Direct ancestor of the modern Games every kid knows and a window into pan-Hellenic identity (the one thing the squabbling city-states shared). Cool specifics as answers: the sacred truce (ekecheiria) that halted wars for the Games, the olive-wreath prize, athletes competing nude, the foot-race (stadion) as the original event, held every four years from 776 BC at Olympia. Note: sports-as-trivia is otherwise out of HISTORY scope, but the ANCIENT Olympics are squarely Greek civilizational history.

**29. Alexander the Great's conquest of the Persian Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Macedonian king conquers Egypt to India · *src: Core Knowledge G2; AP World; military canon*  
> Pillar 3 flagship + the engine of Pillar 1's spread (Greek thought reaching three continents). Undefeated to the edge of the known world by 30. VIVID ACTIONS/NAMED THINGS as answers: taming the unrideable Bucephalus as a boy, founding Alexandria, weeping that there were no more worlds to conquer, the death at 32 in Babylon. Steel-man, not hagiography note honestly in context the cost (the razing of Thebes, the burning of Persepolis, mass-casualty sieges like Tyre) honest record, not theatricalized.

**30. The Gordian Knot** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low  
Alexander slices the unsolvable knot · *src: Cultural literacy; encyclopedia Alexander canon*  
> Living idiom for a bold solution to an intractable problem ('cutting the Gordian knot'). VIVID ACTION is the answer: told whoever undid the knot would rule Asia, Alexander simply drew his sword and sliced it. A crisp T2-T3 wonder hook that pays off in everyday language the kid will hear for life.

**31. Battle of Gaugamela (331 BC): Alexander beats Darius III** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Outnumbered Greeks shatter the Persian army · *src: AP World; great-battles military canon*  
> Pillar 3: the decisive battle that ended the Persian Empire outnumbered Greeks breaking a vastly larger force through tactical genius (the deliberate gap in the line, the cavalry wedge straight at Darius, who fled the field). Cool tactical specifics as answers, not just the date/place. Higher-tier weight.

**32. The Hellenistic Age: spread of Greek culture** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Greek language and ideas from Egypt to India · *src: Core Knowledge G6 'spread of Greek culture'; AP World*  
> Pillar 1 connective tissue: how Greek thought, the Greek language (koine the tongue the New Testament was later written in), art, and city-planning saturated the whole ancient world from Egypt to the edge of India a precondition for the Athens-to-Jerusalem-to-Rome chain. Greco-Buddhist art (Buddha sculpted in Greek style at Gandhara) is a stunning surprise-reversal hook. Substantive, not a vocabulary label.

**33. The Library and Lighthouse of Alexandria** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Greatest ancient library; a wonder lighthouse · *src: AP World Hellenistic; cultural literacy*  
> The symbol of collected human knowledge the dream of gathering every book in the world plus the Pharos, one of the Seven Wonders. Cool facts as answers: ships searched at the docks so their scrolls could be copied, scholars like Eratosthenes and Euclid working there, the 100m+ mirror-topped lighthouse. The loss is told honestly as a wound to human knowledge, not as a preachy 'we now know better' moment.

**34. Greek alphabet and the gift of vowels** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
First alphabet with written vowels · *src: AP World; Hirsch cultural literacy*  
> Pillar 1: the direct ancestor of the Latin letters the kid writes in daily the Greeks took the Phoenician consonant-script and did something nobody had: wrote the VOWELS, making true alphabetic literacy possible. Reframe past dry 'first alphabet' recall (anti-rote): the surprise-reversal is the leap (vowels) and the lineage (Phoenician to Greek to Latin to us). 'Alphabet' itself = alpha + beta.

**35. Aesop's Fables** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T2 · wt=medium  
Talking-animal tales with morals · *src: Core Knowledge early grades; cultural literacy*  
> Cultural-literacy bedrock known to every young child the tortoise and the hare, the boy who cried wolf, the ant and the grasshopper morals transmitted by story, which is exactly the bank's 'show, don't preach' method in its oldest form. NAMED tales as answers. Light, kid-accessible T1-T2 wonder.

**36. Stoicism and Epicureanism (Hellenistic philosophies of life)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T5 · wt=medium  
How to live: endurance vs. pleasure · *src: AP World Hellenistic; philosophy canon*  
> Pillar 1 ideas with living vocabulary ('stoic,' 'epicurean'). Plain-language-then-term-then-plain (voice rule): the Stoics on enduring what you cannot control with calm; the Epicureans NOT on indulgence (the common misreading) but on simple pleasures and freedom from fear a clean surprise-reversal. Present each on its own terms, no strawman. T5 weight; the deeper doctrinal treatment lives in the PHILOSOPHY bank.

**37. The Socratic method as a way of thinking (the elenchus)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Question-and-answer cross-examination that exposes muddled assumptions; 'I know that I know nothing' · *src: vision-injected*  
> The consensus list covers Socrates' DEATH but the framework explicitly names the 'Socratic method' itself as a top-billing Western achievement the actual transmissible thinking tool. The bank's whole purpose is teaching kids to think (celebrate section: logical clarity, fallacy-catching). Show the method by image: Socrates in the agora asking a confident general 'what is courage?' and, question by question, revealing the man didn't actually know exposing assumptions rather than asserting answers. Coordinate the boundary with the PHILOSOPHY bank, but the method's ORIGIN is rightly Greek history.

**38. Greek natural philosophy: the first search for natural causes (Thales to the atomists)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
The Milesians and Democritus sought natural explanations of the cosmos instead of myth; Democritus proposed everything is made of indivisible 'atoms' · *src: vision-injected*  
> The consensus list has applied figures (Pythagoras, Euclid, Archimedes, Hippocrates) but omits the FOUNDING MOVE of Western science: the pre-Socratics asking what the world is made of and seeking natural rather than divine causes. Surprise-reversal/discovery hook: Thales predicting an eclipse and saying everything came from water; Democritus, ~2,400 years before modern chemistry, arguing matter is made of uncuttable 'atoms' (atomos = 'uncuttable'). The early answers were often wrong, but the METHOD (nature has knowable natural causes) is the headwater of Pillar 1's science lineage present it without condescension per moral_vision §4.

**39. Sparta's helots and the krypteia: the price of the warrior state** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
A permanently enslaved population (helots) outnumbering Spartans, kept down by terror and the krypteia (sanctioned secret-police killings of helots) · *src: vision-injected*  
> The agoge topic exists but the consensus list omits the institution that MADE Sparta what it was: a tiny warrior elite ruling a vast enslaved helot majority through systematic terror, including the krypteia in which young Spartans were licensed to murder helots to keep them cowed. State it plainly as fact, paired with the contrast already drawn (Athens' freer society) honest record, not theatricalized, no 'savages' framing, no sensationalism (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5 rules). This is what keeps the Athens/Sparta comparison from sliding into 'both equally admirable' false equivalence.


## Ancient Rome (35)

**1. Romulus and Remus — the founding legend** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Twin founders, she-wolf, fratricide, 753 BC · *src: Core Knowledge Gr3 (Roman mythology); cultural-literacy canon*  
> Tell the myth straight on its own terms (moral_vision §4 — religious/legendary thought taken seriously, no smug 'people used to believe'). The she-wolf suckling the twins is the iconic NAMED image — lead with the scene, not the date. Fratricide stated plainly: Romulus killed Remus over the walls. No editorializing.

**2. The seven kings and the birth of the Republic (509 BC)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Tarquin the Proud expelled; Lucretia; Brutus founds Republic · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World (Monarchy→Republic phase)*  
> This is the FOUNDING of the West's republican-institutions inheritance (moral_vision §3 Athens→Rome line; Pillar 1 HEAVIEST). Frame as the moment Rome rejected one-man rule — the seed of the mixed government the American Founders later studied. Lead with the drama: Lucretia's death and Brutus swearing the oath against kings.

**3. The Roman Republic's mixed government** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Senate, consuls, patricians vs plebeians, SPQR · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World key terms (Senate/Consul/Patrician/Plebeian)*  
> HEAVIEST-pillar core (Pillar 1; moral_vision §3 'Rome — law, republican institutions'). Frame as the checks-and-balances model that directly shaped the American founders — two consuls checking each other, the Senate, annual terms, veto. This is the institutional inheritance the bank exists to transmit. Polybius's analysis of the mixed constitution is the through-line; the Founders read it.

**4. Cincinnatus — the dictator who returned to his plow** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Saved Rome, gave up power, went home · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1 Roman row; cultural-literacy canon*  
> VIRTUE-BY-EXAMPLE exemplar (moral_vision meta-principle 4 'virtues are real and formable'; §5 'show virtue, don't preach it'). The model of civic restraint: handed absolute power, defeated the enemy, surrendered it and went back to farming. Draw the explicit line to Washington — who was called 'the American Cincinnatus' and founded the Society of the Cincinnati. Show the virtue in the action, never moralize.

**5. The Twelve Tables — Rome's first written law** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
~450 BC law code posted in the Forum · *src: Core Knowledge (Roman law); AP World*  
> Foundational to the West's rule-of-law inheritance (moral_vision §3 'rule of law'; §3.4 natural-law lineage). The wonder is that the law was POSTED IN PUBLIC where every citizen — patrician or plebeian — could read it, ending secret aristocratic law. Frame as the ancestor of 'equal before the law' and due process. Teach the term inline (§16): what a written, public law code actually changed.

**6. The Punic Wars and Hannibal at Cannae** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high  
Rome vs Carthage; elephants over Alps; double envelopment · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World (Punic Wars emphasized); West Point*  
> Pillar 3 (great battles, story-led). The cool fact is the answer: elephants over the Alps, the double-envelopment at Cannae still taught at West Point. Lead with Hannibal the man at the breaking point — crossing the Alps in winter, losing men and elephants. Steelman distractors with real adjacent battles (Trebia, Lake Trasimene). Don't reduce Carthage to a foil — it was a serious Mediterranean power.

**7. Scipio, Zama, and the destruction of Carthage** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Scipio beats Hannibal 202 BC; Carthage razed 146 BC · *src: AP World (Punic Wars); standard ancient-history canon*  
> Pillar 3 payoff. The reversal: the one general who finally beat Hannibal, on Hannibal's own ground at Zama. Cato's 'Carthago delenda est' is the NAMED quote. State the razing of Carthage plainly (honest record of ancient warfare's brutality, moral_vision §4 'honest, not cynical') — the city burned, salt-the-earth is later legend. No theatrical reveal.

**8. Spartacus and the slave revolt** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Gladiator leads 73-71 BC rebellion; 6,000 crucified · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; standard Roman-history surveys*  
> Honest record of ancient brutality (moral_vision §4; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5 — Roman slavery + crucifixion as institution). State plainly: 6,000 of his men crucified along the Appian Way, the crosses left standing as warning. Roman slavery is not sanitized. The gladiator-turned-rebel is the drama; the mass crucifixion is the honest cost, stated as fact not sensationalized.

**9. The Gracchi brothers and the Republic's unraveling** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Tiberius & Gaius reform land, both killed · *src: AP World (late Republic crisis); standard surveys*  
> The reform-and-murder cycle that began the Republic's death (Pillar 1 — the institutions failing). Frame as the breakdown of the mixed constitution: political violence replacing the rule of law, tribunes killed in the streets. This is the cautionary half of the republican-inheritance story — what happens when norms collapse. Present both brothers' reforms as serious attempts, not strawmen (§6).

**10. Julius Caesar crossing the Rubicon** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Gallic Wars; 'alea iacta est'; civil war 49 BC · *src: Core Knowledge Gr3 (Julius Caesar); AP World*  
> Iconic-moment canon (Pillar 6; HISTORY_TEMPLATES names the Rubicon explicitly). 'Alea iacta est' / 'the die is cast' is the NAMED quote and a permanent idiom for the irreversible choice. Lead with the scene: the general halting at the river that a general may not cross with his army, then crossing. The crossing is the death-knell of the Republic — note the weight without preaching.

**11. The Ides of March — Caesar's assassination** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
44 BC; Brutus; 'Et tu, Brute?'; Senate stabbing · *src: Core Knowledge; Shakespeare/cultural-literacy canon*  
> Iconic-moment + drama (Pillar 6). 'Et tu, Brute?' is the cultural-osmosis NAMED line (via Shakespeare — fair to use, cultural literacy). The conspirators believed they were SAVING the Republic by killing the man who broke it — present their motive as a real position (§6), not as cartoon villainy. The most famous political murder in Western history; lead with the scene at the Senate.

**12. Antony, Cleopatra, and the Battle of Actium** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
31 BC sea battle; fleet flees; both suicides · *src: Core Knowledge Gr3 (Cleopatra); AP World; HISTORY_TEMPLATES*  
> Drama-rich (Pillar 6). CAUTION — apply the Wonder Pattern Drama-Available Rule (memory: the Actium failure): drama in the stem must NOT resolve to a venue/date answer. The cool fact is the answer — the asp (Cleopatra's chosen death), Antony's sword, the fleet abandoning the battle. Lead with the human stakes, ask for the vivid specific, keep the date in context.

**13. Cicero — orator, statesman, voice of the Republic** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Greatest Roman orator; killed 43 BC; hands nailed up · *src: Core Knowledge (Roman literature); cultural-literacy canon*  
> Pillar 1 (the West's rhetorical + republican inheritance; moral_vision §3 names Cicero-tier figures). The man who defined Latin prose and republican ideals — Locke and the Founders read him. The grisly end is honest record: his head and HANDS (that wrote against Antony) nailed to the Rostra. Show the courage-at-cost (§5) — he chose to face the assassins. State the brutality plainly.

**14. Augustus and the founding of the Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Octavian wins 27 BC; first emperor; 'found brick, left marble' · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World (Augustus/Empire transition)*  
> Pillar 1 hinge — the Republic becomes the Empire. The NAMED quote: 'I found Rome a city of brick and left it a city of marble.' Be honest about the trade (§4): Augustus brought peace and stability by ending the Republic — he kept the Senate's forms while holding the real power. Don't moralize; let the kid see the bargain. The month August carries his name.

**15. The Pax Romana** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
~200 years of peace and prosperity, roads, trade · *src: Core Knowledge (Pax Romana); AP World*  
> Pillar 1 (the institutional achievement that spread Roman civilization). Two centuries where a traveler could cross the Mediterranean world under one law and one peace. Frame as the real human achievement it was (moral_vision §2 — celebrate genuine progress) — roads, trade, safety — while honest that the peace rested on the legions. Concrete handle over abstraction (§5).

**16. The Roman legion and the army machine** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Legions, centurions, testudo, gladius, marching camps · *src: Core Knowledge (military); AP World key term Legion*  
> Ordinary ingenuity + mechanism (moral_vision §2). The wonder is the SYSTEM: a legion building a fortified camp every single night on the march, the testudo shield-wall, standardized engineering. Frame as the discipline-and-engineering machine that conquered the Mediterranean, not as glory-of-conquest. Concrete named things (gladius, pilum, testudo) are the answers.

**17. Teutoburg Forest — Varus loses three legions** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
9 AD German ambush; 'Varus, give me back my legions' · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3 (Teutoburg 9 AD)*  
> Pillar 3 (decisive battle + consequence) — explicitly named in HISTORY_TEMPLATES. The reversal: three whole legions annihilated in a forest, stopping Rome at the Rhine forever. The NAMED quote is Augustus beating his head: 'Quintili Vare, legiones redde!' / 'Varus, give me back my legions!' Lead with the ambush; the consequence (the Rhine as permanent border) is the payoff.

**18. Nero, Caligula, and the mad emperors** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Tyranny, the Great Fire of Rome 64 AD, excess · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; Core Knowledge (Roman emperors)*  
> Honest record (§4) + the danger of unchecked one-man power (ties to the lost Republic's checks). CORRECT the myth precisely: Nero did NOT 'fiddle while Rome burned' (no fiddles existed; the legend is the cultural artifact — name it AS legend, don't assert it as fact, per anti-fabrication §7). Present the cruelty plainly without sensationalizing. The contrast with the Republic's restraints is the deeper lesson.

**19. Trajan and the Empire at its greatest extent** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Peak ~117 AD; Dacian Wars; Trajan's Column · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1 Roman row; standard surveys*  
> Pillar 1 + art/architecture inheritance (HISTORY_TEMPLATES Roman row). The cool fact is the answer: Trajan's Column tells the entire Dacian War in a carved SPIRAL relief winding up the shaft — a stone documentary. Lead with the empire at its largest borders, then narrow to the column. The column is a Western-art monument that still stands.

**20. Hadrian's Wall and the edges of empire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
73-mile stone wall across Britain ~122 AD · *src: Standard Roman-Britain canon; cultural-literacy*  
> Roman engineering inheritance (Pillar 1). The concrete handle (§5): a 73-mile stone wall marking the literal edge of Rome's reach, still walkable today. Frame as the visible limit of empire — where Roman order stopped and 'barbarian' Britain began. The wonder is that you can still walk it 1,900 years later.

**21. Marcus Aurelius — the philosopher-emperor** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Stoic, wrote Meditations on campaign; last Good Emperor · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1 Roman row; cultural-literacy*  
> Pillar 6 (drama of the great minds) + LIVING Western inheritance (Pillar 1). The wonder: the most powerful man in the world wrote PRIVATE Stoic notes-to-self in a war-camp tent — never meant for publication — that people still read for wisdom 1,800 years later. This is Stoicism as a living tradition (Athens→Rome line). Show the virtue (self-mastery) by the action of the writing, never preach it (§5).

**22. The Colosseum and gladiator games** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high  
50,000-seat arena; gladiators; 'thumbs'; naval battles · *src: Core Knowledge (entertainment); cultural-literacy canon*  
> Engineering wonder AND honest brutality held together (moral_vision §7 dual commitment; §4). The cool facts: a 50,000-seat arena that could be FLOODED for mock naval battles, the retractable awning. AND state plainly that people and animals were killed for entertainment — the games were lethal, not sport. Don't sanitize and don't sensationalize. Correct the 'thumbs up = live' myth carefully (the gesture's meaning is genuinely uncertain).

**23. The Pantheon and Roman concrete** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Still-standing concrete dome; open oculus; lost formula · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1 Roman art row*  
> THE beauty-of-mechanism exemplar for Rome (moral_vision §2 names 'why the Roman concrete still stands' explicitly; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1 names the Pantheon dome). The wonder is the answer: the world's largest unreinforced concrete dome for ~1,300 years, with an open hole (the oculus) at the top, still standing. Lead with 'why does it still stand?' — the mechanism IS the wonder.

**24. Aqueducts, roads, and Roman engineering** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Arches, aqueducts, 'all roads lead to Rome', concrete · *src: Core Knowledge (architecture/contributions); AP World*  
> Ordinary ingenuity + the West's engineering inheritance (moral_vision §2; Pillar 1). The wonder: aqueducts moving water dozens of miles on a gradient of inches per mile, using only gravity and the arch. 'All roads lead to Rome' is the NAMED idiom. Concrete named feats are the answers (the arch, the aqueduct gradient, the Appian Way). Celebrate the genuine engineering achievement.

**25. Pompeii and the eruption of Vesuvius (79 AD)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high  
City buried in ash; plaster casts; Pliny eyewitness · *src: Core Knowledge Gr3 (Pompeii); cultural-literacy canon*  
> Wonder + honest record of disaster (§2, §4). The cool fact is the answer: the plaster CASTS — archaeologists poured plaster into the body-shaped voids in the ash, capturing people in their final moment. Pliny the Younger's eyewitness letter is the NAMED primary source. The frozen city teaches daily Roman life. State the human toll honestly without ghoulishness.

**26. Virgil's Aeneid and Roman literature** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Epic of Aeneas founding Rome's bloodline; 'arms and the man' · *src: Core Knowledge (significance of Virgil's Aeneid)*  
> Pillar 1 (Western literary inheritance). Rome's national epic, linking Rome's bloodline back to Troy through Aeneas. 'Arma virumque cano' / 'Of arms and the man I sing' is the NAMED opening line. Frame as the founding myth Augustus's Rome told about itself — and a pillar of the Western literary canon (Dante chose Virgil as his guide). AVOID 'who wrote' framing (anti-rote §6) — ask about the story or the line.

**27. Roman law and its lasting legacy** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Twelve Tables to Justinian's Code; 'innocent until proven' · *src: Core Knowledge (law/contributions); AP World*  
> HEAVIEST-pillar inheritance (moral_vision §3 'Rome — law'; §3.4 natural-law lineage; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1). Frame as the direct ancestor of Western legal systems still in use — the presumption of innocence, the burden of proof on the accuser, written codified law. This is among the most load-bearing 'real human achievement' topics in the whole strand. Concrete legal principles are the answers, not abstract praise.

**28. Christianity under Roman rule and persecution** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Jesus under Rome; martyrs; catacombs; arena deaths · *src: Core Knowledge (rise of Christianity / Christianity under Rome)*  
> Christian heritage as substantive HISTORICAL force (moral_vision §3.4; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK 'Christianity treated as substantive force'). Tell it as history, NOT doctrine and NOT with a smug-atheist OR smug-believer voice (§4): a persecuted faith spreading through the Empire, martyrs killed in the arena, believers worshipping in the catacombs. The NAMED specifics (Nero's persecution after the fire, the catacombs) are the handles. No metaphysical claims about truth — straight history.

**29. Constantine, the Milvian Bridge, and Nicaea** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
312 AD vision 'in this sign'; Edict of Milan; Council 325 · *src: Core Knowledge (Constantine); HISTORY_TEMPLATES Roman row*  
> The hinge of Christianity's place in the West (moral_vision §3.4; HISTORY_TEMPLATES Roman row). The reversal: a faith that was outlawed and fed to lions becomes favored across the Empire within one reign. 'In hoc signo vinces' / 'in this sign you will conquer' is the NAMED moment before Milvian Bridge. Present the vision as what Constantine reported (§4 — religious experience on its own terms, no debunking, no asserting).

**30. Diocletian and the splitting of the Empire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Tetrarchy; East/West division; last great persecution · *src: AP World (late Empire); standard surveys*  
> Late-Empire structural turn (Pillar 1 — the institutions adapting then fracturing). The Tetrarchy (rule of four) and the East/West split that foreshadows Rome's two destinies. Honest record: Diocletian launched the LAST and worst great persecution of Christians right before Constantine reversed it — name it plainly (§4) as the dark counterpoint to the Constantine arc. Teach 'tetrarchy' inline (§16).

**31. The barbarian invasions and the sack of Rome** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Alaric's Visigoths 410; Attila the Hun; Vandals 455 · *src: AP World (fall of Rome); HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3*  
> Pillar 3 + Pillar 5 (the non-Roman peoples on their own terms, NOT as 'savage' foils — moral_vision §4 'no condescension toward any culture'). Alaric's Visigoths sacking Rome in 410 — the first time in 800 years — shocked the world (Augustine wrote City of God in response). Present the Goths, Huns, and Vandals as real peoples with real motives, not cartoon barbarians. 'Vandalism' as a word comes from the 455 sack — the NAMED etymology.

**32. The fall of the Western Roman Empire (476 AD)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Last emperor Romulus Augustulus deposed; causes debated · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World (rise and fall); HISTORY_TEMPLATES Roman row*  
> The canonical 'end of antiquity' (Pillar 1; HISTORY_TEMPLATES Roman row). The poignant NAMED irony: the last Western emperor was Romulus Augustulus — sharing the name of Rome's FOUNDER (Romulus) and its first emperor (Augustus). Present the causes as genuinely debated (overextension, economy, the legions, the split) — not a single tidy answer; honest history admits the debate (§4). Pairs with the Justinian topic to show Rome did not fully die.

**33. Justinian and the surviving Eastern (Byzantine) Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Justinian's Code; Hagia Sophia 537; Rome lives on in the East · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES (Justinian + Hagia Sophia); AP World*  
> The surprise-reversal that corrects the 'Rome fell in 476' simplification (moral_vision §2 surprise reversals; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §1 names Hagia Sophia + 'Solomon, I have outdone thee'). Rome did NOT fully end — it lived another THOUSAND years in the East. The NAMED wonders: Justinian's Code (the law that shaped Europe) and Hagia Sophia 537 with Justinian's cry 'Solomon, I have outdone thee.' Both Western-inheritance crown jewels.

**34. Why Roman concrete still stands — the self-healing mechanism** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Lime clasts + volcanic ash (pozzolana); seawater concrete grows stronger; modern rebar-concrete crumbles in a century · *src: vision-injected*  
> Pure beauty-of-mechanism wonder — moral_vision §2 names 'why the Roman concrete still stands' as a flagship example of the wonder the bank exists to deliver, and §19 says invert the cool fact into a question rather than waste it. The consensus Pantheon topic mentions a 'lost formula' but does not deliver the MECHANISM. The answer: recent research shows lime clasts let Roman concrete self-heal cracks, and volcanic-ash seawater concrete grew STRONGER over centuries — while modern reinforced concrete crumbles in decades. This is the 'why,' not the 'that.'

**35. The Roman Republic as the American Founders' model** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Polybius's mixed constitution; Senate/consuls/veto studied by the Framers; the fasces, the eagle, 'Senate', the Cincinnati · *src: vision-injected*  
> Draws the explicit Rome→Philadelphia line that moral_vision §3 makes central (Athens→Jerusalem→Rome→...→Philadelphia as 'a single unbroken inheritance') and meta-principle 3 ('republican institutions... worth transmitting'). The consensus has 'mixed government' and 'Cincinnatus' separately but never connects Rome to the U.S. Constitution directly. The answer-grade specifics: the Framers read Polybius and Cicero, borrowed the Senate, two-consul executive checks, and the veto; American iconography (the eagle, the fasces, 'Senate,' the Capitol named for the Capitoline Hill, the Society of the Cincinnati) is Roman. This is the single most load-bearing 'why Rome matters to you' question for the bank's American-founding spine.


## Ancient India, China & East Asia (38)

**1. Indus Valley Civilization (Harappa & Mohenjo-daro)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Bronze-Age cities of the Indus River · *src: Core Knowledge Ancient India; AP World*  
> Lead with the wonder of a lost first civilization on its own terms (moral_vision §4 — no 'exotic East' framing): grid streets laid to the compass, the world's earliest known flush drains and covered sewers, the Great Bath, the Dancing Girl bronze. The hook the bank wants is the still-undeciphered script — a real living mystery, curiosity-over-completion (§2). Honest where honest: nobody knows why the cities were abandoned, and nobody can read a word they wrote. Treat these people as the engineers they were, not as a primitive curio.

**2. Aryan migrations & the Vedas** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Indo-Aryan arrival and Vedic hymns · *src: Core Knowledge Ancient India*  
> Present the Vedas as the oldest continuously recited texts on Earth — the Rigveda memorized and chanted across roughly three millennia with astonishing oral fidelity (ordinary ingenuity, §2). Take the religious material seriously on its own terms, no smug-atheist 'people used to believe' voice (§4). The Indo-Aryan migration is a contested scholarly reconstruction, not a settled date — state it as the leading historical model, not as a fact to memorize (anti-rote, §6), and do not adjudicate the politicized Aryan-migration-vs-indigenous-origins debate.

**3. Hinduism: Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva & core beliefs** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Trimurti, karma, reincarnation, dharma · *src: Core Knowledge (named deities); cultural literacy*  
> Hinduism is named in moral_vision §9 as a tradition the bank honors on its own terms. Tell it through vivid named images, not doctrine recital (anti-rote §6): Shiva's cosmic dance as Nataraja, Vishnu's ten avatars descending across the ages, the churning of the ocean of milk. Karma/dharma/reincarnation presented as the tradition understands itself — no 'primitive belief,' no scare-quotes, no smug-believer counter-voice either (§4). No metaphysical verdict on whether reincarnation is true; this is the world's oldest living religion, treated with full seriousness.

**4. The caste system & varnas** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Brahmin to Shudra social hierarchy · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World*  
> State it plainly and honestly as the defining social structure — Brahmin, Kshatriya, Vaishya, Shudra, and the 'untouchables' below the system entirely (honest record, §3). Don't sanitize the hardship and don't editorialize with 'modern audiences find this troubling' (§4 honest-not-preachy). This pairs naturally with the bank's pre-modern-brutality pillar (framework §5): hereditary degradation stated as fact. Avoid the anti-rote 'what is the term for' trap — teach it through the lived reality (who could do what, who was excluded), not as vocabulary.

**5. Mahabharata & Ramayana epics** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Great Sanskrit epics; Rama, Krishna · *src: Cultural literacy; encyclopedias*  
> Pure story-led territory the bank loves (§5 voice, story-led openings): the Mahabharata is the longest poem ever composed (~1.8M words, ~10x Iliad+Odyssey combined) — that superlative is a stem hook, with the named thing as the answer (§6 cool-fact-is-the-answer, §19 invert). Rama building the bridge to Lanka, Hanuman leaping the ocean, the Bhagavad Gita spoken on the battlefield as Arjuna freezes before kin. Treat the Gita's moral dilemma as the serious philosophy it is, not a curio. Named things over labels (Wonder Pattern).

**6. The Buddha (Siddhartha Gautama)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Prince to enlightenment under bodhi tree · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Buddhism is named in moral_vision §9 as honored on its own terms. A born story-arc (§5 story-led, §2 courage-in-pursuit-of-truth): the sheltered prince who saw old age, sickness, and death for the first time, abandoned the palace, nearly starved as an ascetic, then sat under the bodhi tree vowing not to rise until he understood. The named details are the gold — the Four Sights, the bodhi tree, the Middle Way discovered after rejecting extremes. No condescension, no 'mythologized' hedging; tell it straight.

**7. Buddhism: Four Noble Truths & Eightfold Path** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Core teachings, nirvana, the middle way · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Honored tradition (§9). Resist the rote-list trap (§6 anti-rote): don't make the question 'name the four truths' — teach the moves through concrete images and the logic (suffering rooted in craving; the path as a practical discipline, not a creed). The Middle Way framed as the discovery between self-indulgence and self-torture is a genuine insight worth its seriousness (§4 no condescension to any culture; Buddhist analysis treated like any rigorous philosophy). Nirvana glossed plainly before any technical term (§5).

**8. Mauryan Empire & Ashoka the Great** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high  
First Indian empire; Ashoka's edict pillars · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> A genuine surprise-reversal the bank prizes (§2): the conqueror who, sickened by the Kalinga bloodbath he himself caused (~100,000 dead by his own edict's count), renounced war and carved his remorse into stone pillars across the empire — the polished sandstone Pillars of Ashoka, the lion capital that is India's emblem today. Count the Kalinga slaughter honestly (§3 honest record) — the reversal only lands because the carnage was real. Not theatricalized; stated plainly. Named things: the edicts, the Lion Capital, the Dhamma he proclaimed.

**9. Gupta Empire — India's Golden Age** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Classical math, science, art flourishing · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Civilization-on-its-own-terms done right (framework §6 — substantive achievement, NOT exotic foil): Aryabhata computing pi to four places and proposing a rotating Earth and a heliocentric-leaning model ~500 AD; Sushruta's surgical compendium; the iron pillar of Delhi that has resisted rust for 1,600 years (mechanism wonder, §2). Lead with the real discoveries and the people who made them. This is the natural home for the zero/decimal achievement (see standalone below).

**10. Indian numerals, zero & decimal system** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high  
Invention of zero and place-value · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> A real human achievement the bank celebrates without chauvinism (§2 ordinary ingenuity; §4 no condescension): India gave the world zero as a NUMBER (not just a placeholder) and the place-value decimal system, transmitted via Arab scholars to become 'Arabic numerals.' Brahmagupta (628 AD) wrote the first rules for arithmetic with zero and negative numbers. Frame as the conceptual leap it was — picture a world of arithmetic with no zero — not a 'who invented' rote lookup (§6 anti-rote; §19 invert the cool fact into the answer).

**11. Jainism & Mahavira** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Ahimsa, extreme non-violence · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*
