> Honored on its own terms (§4, §9 honors all major traditions). The wonder hook is the radicalism of ahimsa carried to its limit: monks sweeping the path before each step and straining water to avoid harming the smallest life, Mahavira renouncing even clothing. Present the commitment seriously, with fascination, not as eccentricity to be smiled at (§4 no condescension to any culture). Born in the same century as the Buddha — the two as parallel responses to Vedic ritualism.

**12. Ancient China's rivers & oracle bones (Shang)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Huang He, Yangtze, earliest Chinese writing · *src: Core Knowledge Grade 2/4 China*  
> Lead with the oracle bones as the vivid mechanism (§2 beauty of mechanism, named things): Shang diviners scratched questions onto ox shoulder-blades and turtle shells, applied a hot poker, and read the cracks as the ancestors' answers — then carved the outcome beside it, leaving the oldest Chinese writing we can still read and a direct ancestor of characters used today. That unbroken script-to-present continuity is the real wonder. Take the ancestor-divination on its own terms (§4). The Yellow River as 'China's Sorrow' (flood-and-silt) is a strong concrete hook.

**13. Mandate of Heaven & Zhou dynasty** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Divine right to rule; dynastic cycle · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge*  
> A genuinely powerful political idea, treated as the serious concept it is (§4 no condescension): Heaven grants the right to rule but withdraws it from the unjust — so flood, famine, or defeat could be read as Heaven revoking the mandate, making rebellion legitimate. This is the engine of the whole dynastic cycle for 3,000 years. Teach the LOGIC and its consequences (a ruler could lose legitimacy), not the term as vocabulary (§6 anti-rote). It is China's homegrown answer to the question of what makes power rightful.

**14. Confucius & Confucianism** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Filial piety, the Analects, social order · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Major civilizational force, full seriousness (§4 no condescension — Confucian ethics treated like any serious moral philosophy). A man largely unsuccessful in his own lifetime — wandering state to state looking for a ruler who'd listen — whose recorded sayings then shaped East Asia for 2,500 years (surprise-reversal, §2). Teach the actual moves: the five relationships, ren (humaneness), 'do not do to others what you would not want done to you' (the Golden Rule, centuries before its Western phrasing — a real comparison, not false equivalence, §4). Story-led via the Analects, not doctrine-recital (§6).

**15. Daoism & Laozi** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The Dao, wu wei, yin-yang, Tao Te Ching · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Daoism is named in moral_vision §9 as honored on its own terms, and §4 explicitly uses 'Daoist and Cartesian skepticism are the same kind of move — treat them with the same seriousness' as the model for this whole strand. Lead with the concrete paradox-images the bank loves (§5 concrete over abstract): water as the softest thing that wears away rock, wu wei as effortless action, the uncarved block, yin-yang. Gloss each term plainly (§5). Whether Laozi was one historical person is itself uncertain — fine to note, don't fabricate biography (framework §7 no fabrication).

**16. Qin Shi Huang & China's unification** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
First emperor; standardized writing, Legalism · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge*  
> Honest both ways — the achievement AND the brutality, stated plainly (§3 honest record, framework §5): he unified the warring states, standardized the script, the currency, even axle-widths so carts fit every rutted road. AND he buried scholars and burned books under Legalist terror, conscripted vast forced labor to build wall and tomb, and chased immortality with mercury elixirs that likely killed him (his tomb is ringed by rivers of mercury). Don't sanitize the tyranny; don't theatricalize it either (§6 no TIL-reveal). A tyrant who built something that outlasted him.

**17. Terracotta Army** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
8,000 clay soldiers guarding Qin's tomb · *src: Cultural literacy; encyclopedias*  
> Archaeology-as-wonder (§2), and a kid-magnet. The detail that lands: every one of the ~8,000 warriors has a different face, found by farmers digging a well in 1974, originally painted in bright colors that flaked within minutes of hitting the air. Named/vivid specifics over the bare count (Wonder Pattern: vivid actions/objects > numbers). Pairs with the honest note that countless conscripted laborers died building this for one man's afterlife (§3) — but keep the awe; the army itself is the marvel.

**18. The Great Wall of China** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Defensive wall built across dynasties · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural literacy*  
> Iconic enough for T1, but puncture two myths the bank exists to correct (§2 surprise-reversal): it is NOT a single wall and NOT one continuous structure, and you genuinely CANNOT see it from the Moon with the naked eye (a famous falsehood worth correcting). The honest human cost belongs in the record (§3): hundreds of thousands of laborers died on the Ming and earlier walls — 'the longest cemetery on Earth.' Lead with the most-Ming visible wall most people picture, dated honestly to the 14th–16th c., not the legendary Qin patchwork.

**19. Han dynasty & the Silk Road origins** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Golden age; silk trade route opens · *src: Core Knowledge Grade 4; AP World*  
> Civilization on its own terms (framework §6 explicitly names Han astronomers): the dynasty so defining that Chinese people still call themselves 'Han.' Lead with real discovery (§2): Zhang Heng's bronze seismoscope (132 AD) that dropped a ball from a dragon's mouth toward a distant earthquake; Han astronomers logging sunspots and comets centuries before Europe; the invention of paper under Cai Lun (105 AD). Zhang Qian's embassy west opening the trade routes is the story hook. Substantive achievement, not exotic backdrop.

**20. The Silk Road** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Trans-Eurasian trade of goods and ideas · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World (heavily)*  
> The bank's favorite kind of story — ideas and goods crossing deserts, not just commodities (§2 wonder, ordinary ingenuity). The reversal hook: silk's secret was guarded on pain of death for centuries until silkworm eggs were allegedly smuggled west in hollow canes; the road carried Buddhism INTO China and paper toward Europe as much as it carried silk. Concrete waypoints (Dunhuang's caves, the Taklamakan, Samarkand). Frame as a network of relays, not a single paved route (correct the cartoon image). Connects East and West as one human story.

**21. Four Great Inventions (paper, printing, compass, gunpowder)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
China's world-changing technologies · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Squarely in moral_vision §2 (ordinary ingenuity) and framework §6 (Song printing + gunpowder named): real inventions that reshaped the whole world. Lead with the mechanism and the irony (§2 beauty of mechanism, surprise-reversal): gunpowder began as Daoist alchemists hunting an immortality elixir and instead found an explosive; the magnetic compass started as a divination 'south-pointing' spoon; Bi Sheng's movable clay type (~1040) predated Gutenberg by four centuries. Invert superlatives into answers (§19). Celebrate without chauvinism — these are humanity's gains (§4).

**22. Spread of Buddhism into China & Xuanzang** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Pilgrim's journey west for sutras · *src: AP World*  
> Courage-in-pursuit-of-truth, the bank's core virtue-by-example move (§2, §5 show-don't-preach): the monk Xuanzang defied an imperial travel ban and crossed the deserts and the Pamirs alone to reach India, studied for years, and hauled hundreds of sutras back to translate — a 16-year, ~10,000-mile journey later mythologized as Journey to the West (Monkey King). Tell the real expedition; the named pilgrim and his risk are the gold. Religious motive treated seriously (§4).

**23. Tang dynasty — golden age of poetry & arts** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Cosmopolitan peak; Li Bai, Chang'an · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Framework §6 names 'Tang poets' and 'Tang Chang'an as world's largest city' explicitly — vision-mandated coverage. Chang'an at ~1 million, a planned cosmopolitan grid drawing Persians, Sogdians, and Japanese envoys. Lead with named drama (§2 drama of the great minds, applied to poets): Li Bai, the wandering genius who legend says drowned reaching for the moon's reflection in a river while drunk; Du Fu writing through the An Lushan rebellion's devastation. Civilization on its own terms, the cultural peak of imperial China.

**24. Song dynasty & the civil service exam / scholar-gentry** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Merit exams, commerce, gunpowder, gentry · *src: AP World (heavily)*  
> Substantive achievement (framework §6): one of history's first large-scale merit bureaucracies — grueling exams on the Confucian classics, sat in sealed cells for days, that could lift a farmer's son into the governing elite (the meritocracy reversal, §2). Plus a pre-modern economic and technological boom: the world's first government paper money (jiaozi), the magnetic compass for navigation, gunpowder weapons, vast iron output. Take the institution seriously as a real innovation in governance — not a foil.

**25. Grand Canal of China** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
World's longest ancient canal · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Engineering-marvel wonder (§2 beauty of mechanism) with the honest cost attached (§3): the longest artificial waterway in the world, ~1,800 km linking the rice-rich south to the northern capitals — and dug by millions of conscripted laborers under the Sui at brutal human cost that helped topple the dynasty that built it. The achievement and the suffering stated together, plainly, neither sanitized nor theatricalized (framework §5). The thing that physically knit China together.

**26. Genghis Khan & the Mongol Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Largest land empire; steppe conquest · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Honest BOTH ways — this is the test of the strand (§3 honest record, framework §5/§6). The achievements are real and named in framework §6 (the Mongol postal system / yam relay, the Pax Mongolica that made the Silk Road safe end to end, religious tolerance, a written law code). The brutality is also real and must be counted plainly: cities like Nishapur and the Khwarezmian heartland annihilated, whole populations put to the sword, demographic catastrophe across Central Asia and northern China — stated as fact, not adventure-sanitized and not theatricalized. A boy from the steppe who built the largest contiguous land empire ever. Do NOT romanticize away the slaughter.

**27. Kublai Khan & the Yuan dynasty** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Mongols rule China; Marco Polo's visit · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Foreign conquerors ruling China — tell it straight, including that the Mongols ranked the native Chinese at the bottom of a four-tier ethnic hierarchy (honest record, §3, no sanitizing). The wonder hook is Marco Polo's astonished report of Xanadu, paper money, and coal ('black stones that burn') — so marvelous Europeans doubted him for centuries (surprise-reversal, §2). Frame Polo's account as a window AND as a contested source (some scholars question how much he saw) — don't present travelers' tales as unquestioned fact (framework §7).

**28. Ming dynasty & the Forbidden City** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Han restoration; imperial palace built · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World*  
> Native rule restored after the Mongols. The vivid handle (§2 named things, §6 cool-fact-is-answer): the Forbidden City's ~9,000 rooms where for ~500 years commoners entered on pain of death, built in the early 1400s and still standing as the largest preserved wooden-palace complex on Earth. Civilization on its own terms. Connects forward to Zheng He (below) and the inward turn that halted the voyages — the bank's honest 'decline thread' (framework §6, §3 — orthodoxy/insularity stifling outward reach, stated as fact not editorialized).

**29. Zheng He's treasure fleet voyages** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Giant Ming fleets across Indian Ocean · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Framework §6 names Zheng He explicitly — vision-mandated, and a perfect surprise-reversal (§2): a Muslim eunuch admiral led fleets of hundreds of ships and ~28,000 men, with 'treasure ships' dwarfing anything Europe had, reaching East Africa decades BEFORE Columbus and bringing back a giraffe to the emperor's court — and then the Ming court burned the records and turned inward, ceding the oceans to Europe. Count the halt honestly as the consequential choice it was (§3 honest record; the road not taken). Named things and vivid scale over bare numbers (Wonder Pattern).

**30. Prince Shotoku & early Japan (Yamato/Nara)** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Buddhism, Chinese model, first state · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Civilization on its own terms (§4), with the genuine theme of deliberate cultural borrowing: Japan consciously imported Chinese writing, Buddhism, Confucian administration, and city planning (Nara modeled on Chang'an) to forge its first centralized state. Shotoku's Seventeen-Article 'constitution' (a code of governing ethics, glossed plainly — not a modern constitution, §5). Tell it as a civilization choosing what to adopt, not as derivative imitation. Buddhism's arrival treated seriously (§9).

**31. Heian Japan & The Tale of Genji** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Courtly culture; world's first novel · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Virtue-by-example and a real human-achievement hook (§2, §4 — celebrate the accomplishment without exoticizing): around 1000 AD a court lady, Murasaki Shikibu, wrote what is widely called the world's first novel — a psychologically rich work centuries before the European novel form. The named woman and the superlative are the gold (invert into the answer, §19). An aesthetic court world of poetry-duels and seasonal ritual, taken on its own terms.

**32. Samurai, shoguns & bushido (feudal Japan)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Warrior class, shogunate, code of honor · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> A kid-magnet (framework: 'warrior code and feudal Japan fascinate every kid'). Lead with vivid named specifics (§2, Wonder Pattern named-things): the daimyo and the shogun who ruled while the emperor reigned in name only; the katana's folded steel; seppuku as ritual; the warrior-ethic of bushido. Honest, not romanticized — bushido as a code was substantially codified and idealized LATER (Edo-period and modern), so present it as a living ethic without fabricating a tidy medieval rulebook (framework §7, §6 no false reveal). The drama and honor are real; keep them grounded.

**33. The kamikaze & failed Mongol invasions of Japan** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Divine wind wrecks Kublai's fleets · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Story-led drama of survival against the largest empire on Earth (§2, §5 story-led): twice (1274, 1281) Kublai Khan launched massive invasion fleets at Japan, and twice typhoons shattered them — the Japanese called the storms 'kamikaze,' the divine wind. State the meteorological fact AND what the Japanese believed it meant, side by side, taking the religious interpretation seriously without endorsing or mocking it (§4). The named term and the named storms are the memorable payoff.

**34. Korea: Three Kingdoms & Goryeo/Joseon** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Korean states; movable metal type, Hangul · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Substantive civilization, real inventions (§2 ordinary ingenuity; §4 on its own terms): Korea cast the world's oldest surviving book printed with movable METAL type (the Jikji, 1377 — predating Gutenberg); and King Sejong commissioned Hangul, a scientifically designed alphabet whose letter-shapes mimic the mouth's position, created so commoners could read (a humane, deliberate act of literacy, §2). Celebrate these as genuine human achievements, not as a junior China. The named king and the named script are the gold.

**35. Angkor Wat & the Khmer Empire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Vast temple city in Cambodia · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Wonder + lost-civilization romance (§2): the largest religious monument on Earth, built by the Khmer in the 12th c. as a Hindu temple to Vishnu, later turned Buddhist, its towers mirroring the cosmic Mount Meru — then swallowed by jungle and 'rediscovered' by the wider world centuries later. The sophisticated hydraulic engineering (vast reservoirs and canals) is a mechanism hook (§2). Civilization on its own terms; treat the religious meaning seriously (§4, §9). Named thing as answer (§6).

**36. Indian Ocean trade & spread of Indian culture to SE Asia** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Monsoon trade; Hinduism/Buddhism spread south · *src: AP World*  
> The quiet, peaceful counterpart to conquest — culture carried by monsoon-timed sail (§2 ordinary ingenuity): merchants and monks spread Hinduism, Buddhism, Sanskrit, and Indian art across Southeast Asia (Srivijaya, Majapahit, Champa, the Khmer) WITHOUT armies, riding seasonal winds sailors learned to time precisely. A genuine 'soft power before the term existed' story. Keep it on its own substance — trade routes, faiths, and the kingdoms they shaped — not a modern geopolitics overlay (§6 anti-drift). Pairs with Angkor and the Silk Road as the maritime half of the Eurasian exchange.

**37. Chinese foot-binding** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
The 1,000-year practice of binding girls' feet; its human cost and its end · *src: vision-injected*  
> Framework §5 names foot-binding EXPLICITLY in the pre-modern-brutality pillar, and the consensus list omits it entirely — a required injection. State it plainly and without flinching (§3 honest record): for roughly a millennium, young girls' arches were broken and toes folded under to force the prized 4-inch 'golden lotus,' crippling tens of millions of women for life. Pair it, per the framework's pattern, with the named forces that ended it — late-19th/early-20th-c. Western missionary anti-binding societies working alongside Chinese reformers (the Kang Youwei / natural-foot movement). No 'savages' framing, no sensationalism, no 'modern audiences' editorializing (§4 honest-not-preachy); the historical record is the argument. This is in-period for the strand (Song through Qing) and central to the bank's honest-brutality mandate.

**38. The Bhagavad Gita's battlefield dilemma** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low ⭐  
Krishna and Arjuna on duty, action, and conscience before the war · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §4 demands non-Western philosophy be treated with the SAME seriousness as Western ('Daoist and Cartesian skepticism are the same kind of move'), and §9 honors Hinduism on its own terms. The Gita is the great philosophical moment of the Indian epics and deserves its own cell beyond the Mahabharata-as-story topic: Arjuna freezes on the battlefield, unwilling to kill his own kin, and Krishna answers with a meditation on duty (dharma), selfless action, and acting without attachment to the fruits — one of the most influential ethical texts ever written (it shaped Thoreau, Emerson, Gandhi). Present the moral problem as the serious philosophy it is — the tension between conscience and duty — without adjudicating its metaphysics or staging a reveal (§4, no verdict on contested metaphysics). Story-led: open on Arjuna's paralysis, then ask about the move (§5).


## Pre-Columbian Americas (36)

**1. Olmec civilization and the colossal heads** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Mesoamerica's 'mother culture'; giant basalt heads · *src: Britannica; LibreTexts Art History; AP World*  
> Present on its own terms as a real human achievement (moral_vision §4 'no condescension toward any culture'). Lead with the wonder: 17-ton basalt heads hauled tens of miles with no wheels or draft animals, each a distinct portrait. The cool fact is the answer (the transport feat / the individualized faces), not a 'mother culture' label.

**2. Teotihuacan, City of the Gods** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Great metropolis; Pyramids of Sun and Moon · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia Pre-Columbian; AP World*  
> A substantive civilization on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5), not an exotic foil. Wonder-led: a planned grid city of ~125,000 at its height — one of the largest cities on Earth in its day — whose builders' name we don't even know (the Aztecs named it 'City of the Gods' centuries after it fell). The mystery of its abandonment is a real hook.

**3. Pyramid of the Sun** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
Massive pyramid at Teotihuacan · *src: LibreTexts; Britannica*  
> Wonder Pattern: the named structure and its staggering scale are the payoff (one of the largest pyramids in the world by volume, built without metal tools, the wheel, or draft animals). Present as engineering achievement, not rote dimensions. The Avenue of the Dead alignment is a concrete handle.

**4. The Maya and their stepped pyramids** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high  
Classic Maya cities; Tikal, Palenque, Chichen Itza · *src: Core Knowledge G5 Unit 2; AP World*  
> Keystone non-Western civilization on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5). Named cities are the cool-fact answers — Tikal's jungle temples rising above the canopy, Palenque, El Castillo at Chichen Itza. Celebrate the achievement straight; no romanticizing and no condescension.

**5. Maya writing and the Dresden Codex** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Hieroglyphic script; surviving bark-paper books · *src: Core Knowledge; Britannica*  
> Celebrate a genuine intellectual achievement: the only fully developed writing system in the pre-Columbian Americas. Honest-record corollary (moral_vision §4): the Spanish friar Diego de Landa burned nearly all the Maya codices in 1562 — only a handful (the Dresden among them) survived. State that plainly as part of the same record; do not theatricalize it. The named codex / the decipherment is the answer.

**6. Maya astronomy and the Long Count calendar** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Calendars, zero, eclipse and Venus tables · *src: Core Knowledge G5 Unit 2; AP World*  
> A real human achievement worth celebrating (moral_vision §2 'wonder'): the Maya independently invented a symbol for zero and computed Venus and eclipse cycles to astonishing precision. Lead with the wonder of independent discovery. The concept (zero / the Venus table accuracy) is the payoff, not a date.

**7. The Mesoamerican ballgame** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Rubber-ball ritual game; stone courts · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia Pre-Columbian*  
> Present on its own terms with the high stakes stated honestly, not sensationalized (moral_vision §4). The vivid specifics are the answers: a solid rubber ball, hips not hands, stone hoops set vertically, and at some sites the losers (or winners) sacrificed. State the ritual-death dimension as fact, paired with the wonder of the oldest known team sport.

**8. Collapse of the Classic Maya** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Mysterious 9th-century abandonment of cities · *src: Core Knowledge ('mystery of the disappearance'); AP World*  
> Honest, not cynical (moral_vision §4): present the genuine archaeological mystery — great cities abandoned over a century — and the competing real hypotheses (drought, warfare, overpopulation/soil exhaustion) as competing answers. Correct the myth in the stem: the Maya people did NOT vanish (millions of Maya live today); the Classic cities were abandoned. A surprise-reversal hook.

**9. The Toltecs and Tula** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Warrior culture; Atlantean warrior columns · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia List of cultures*  
> Present on its own terms. The vivid named thing is the answer: the giant stone Atlantean warrior columns at Tula. Honest note that the Aztecs revered the Toltecs as a legendary golden-age ancestor culture — useful steel-man framing for how later peoples built identity, not a 'primitive' aside.

**10. Founding of Tenochtitlan (eagle on the cactus)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Aztec capital founded on lake island · *src: Core Knowledge G5 Unit 2; AP World*  
> Wonder Pattern — the eagle-perched-on-a-cactus-devouring-a-serpent omen is the named, memorable image and the answer (it is on Mexico's flag today, a concrete present-day handle). Celebrate the ingenuity of building a great city on a lake island. Tell the founding legend straight.

**11. The Aztec Empire and the Triple Alliance** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Mexica warrior state; 1428 three-city alliance · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World 1.4; Kaplan*  
> Keystone civilization on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5) — a sophisticated imperial state, NOT an exotic foil. Hold the dual commitment (moral_vision §7): celebrate the real organizational achievement (the 1428 three-city alliance, tribute system, Tenochtitlan's scale) AND tell the truth about the tributary terror and mass sacrifice that bound it. Both, without flinching and without sensationalism.

**12. Chinampas, the Aztec floating gardens** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Rectangular farm plots built on lake beds · *src: AP World (Fiveable/Albert); Britannica*  
> Celebrate ordinary ingenuity (moral_vision §2): a hyper-productive farming system built on a lake that fed one of the world's largest cities. The mechanism is the wonder — staked beds of mud and vegetation, multiple harvests a year. Present as the engineering marvel it was.

**13. Aztec religion and human sacrifice** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Sun god Huitzilopochtli; Templo Mayor rituals · *src: Core Knowledge ('warrior culture'); AP World*  
> HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §3.7 names this EXPLICITLY: state the mass human sacrifice as fact — Templo Mayor dedication 1487, contemporary Spanish and Aztec sources reporting 20,000–80,000 over four days, obsidian-knife heart extraction, the flayed-skin ritual of Xipe Totec. NO 'savages' framing, NO sensationalism, NO condescension (moral_vision §4) — the historical record IS the argument. Present the worldview (feeding the sun to keep it moving) on its own terms, then state what was done. Pair with the named Western response (see injected Las Casas / Salamanca topic).

**14. Quetzalcoatl, the feathered serpent** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Feathered-serpent deity across Mesoamerica · *src: Britannica; LibreTexts*  
> Present the religious figure seriously on its own terms (moral_vision §4, no smug voice toward any tradition). The named deity and its span across Teotihuacan, Toltec, and Aztec cultures is the cool-fact answer. Treat the Cortés-as-returning-Quetzalcoatl story with care — historians now doubt it; present it as a later-recorded legend, not settled fact.

**15. Moctezuma II and the Spanish conquest of the Aztecs** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Cortes, Malinche, fall of Tenochtitlan 1521 · *src: Core Knowledge ('Spanish conquistadors and demise'); AP World*  
> World-changing clash told straight and honestly (moral_vision §4). Real specifics are the answers — Cortés, La Malinche the interpreter, the role of Tlaxcalan and other Indigenous allies who resented Aztec tribute (correct the 'a few hundred Spaniards' myth: it was an alliance of tens of thousands), and the decisive role of smallpox. State the brutality of conquest plainly without theatricalizing; this is honest history, not a morality play in either direction.

**16. Chavin de Huantar (early Andean culture)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Early Andean religious center; Lanzon idol · *src: Britannica Pre-Columbian (Andes); Wikipedia*  
> Present on its own terms as a foundational Andean culture. The named vivid thing is the answer: the Lanzón, a carved fanged stone idol deep inside a temple maze, and the engineered acoustics/water channels that may have awed pilgrims. Wonder over rote; deep-tier (T4-T5) fandom-depth Andean content.

**17. The Nazca Lines** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Giant desert geoglyphs of Peru · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia Pre-Columbian*  
> Lead with the wonder (moral_vision §2): enormous hummingbird, monkey, and spider figures scratched into the desert, fully legible only from the air. The named figures / their scale are the answer. Honest note: keep it grounded — the real human achievement is the point, not 'ancient aliens' pseudo-mystery (moral_vision §9, not pseudo-scientific).

**18. The Moche civilization** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
North-coast Peru; Lord of Sipan tomb · *src: Britannica Pre-Columbian (Andes)*  
> Present on its own terms; celebrate the artistry (moral_vision §4). The named treasure is the answer: the untouched royal tomb of the Lord of Sipán, gold-and-turquoise regalia, and the astonishingly lifelike portrait pottery. Deep-tier Andean coverage that rewards the fan.

**19. The Inca Empire and Cusco** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Largest pre-Columbian empire; Andean spine · *src: Core Knowledge G5 Unit 2; AP World*  
> Keystone non-Western civilization on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5 names the Inca explicitly). Celebrate the genuine achievement: the largest empire in the pre-Columbian Americas, spanning thousands of miles of Andes, administered without writing or the wheel. Cusco as the puma-shaped capital is a concrete handle. Substantive civilization, not an exotic backdrop.

**20. Machu Picchu** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=high  
Inca mountaintop royal estate · *src: Core Knowledge G5 Unit 2; AP World*  
> The world-famous named place every kid recognizes — ideal T1 wonder hook. Lead with the image (a stone city on a cloud-wrapped ridge) and a real specific as the answer (a royal estate the Spanish never found; 'rediscovered' by Hiram Bingham 1911 though locals always knew it). Celebrate straight.

**21. The Inca road system and rope bridges** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Qhapaq Nan; chasqui relay runners · *src: Core Knowledge ('complex network of roads'); AP World*  
> HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 6 names 'Inca road network 40,000 km' as a substantive achievement — celebrate the ingenuity (moral_vision §2 'ordinary ingenuity'). The vivid specifics are the answers: chasqui relay runners carrying messages ~250 km a day, and woven-grass suspension bridges rebuilt by hand each year (Q'eswachaka). Engineering marvel, told straight.

**22. Quipu, the Inca knotted-string records** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Knotted cords recording numbers/census · *src: AP World (Albert/Fiveable); Britannica*  
> Celebrate a genuine intellectual achievement (moral_vision §4): a sprawling empire administered with no writing system, using a base-10 code of knots in colored cords. The surprise-reversal is the hook — how do you run a census and tax records without writing? The mechanism (knot position = place value) is the wonder.

**23. Inca stonework and Sacsayhuaman** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Mortarless megalithic fitted-stone walls · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia Pre-Columbian*  
> The beauty of mechanism (moral_vision §2): multi-ton stones cut and fitted so precisely a knife blade won't slide between them, with no mortar — and they've survived centuries of Andean earthquakes. The named site (Sacsayhuamán) and the no-mortar precision are the answers. Engineering wonder, grounded (not 'aliens').

**24. Pizarro and the Spanish conquest of the Inca** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Atahualpa captured 1532; fall of empire · *src: Core Knowledge ('demise of the Inca'); AP World*  
> Honest history, told straight (moral_vision §4). Real specifics as answers: the ambush at Cajamarca 1532, Atahualpa's room filled with gold for a ransom then his execution anyway, and the smallpox-driven Inca civil war (Huáscar vs Atahualpa) that had already gutted the empire before Pizarro arrived. State the treachery and the brutality plainly; correct the lone-conquistador myth (disease + civil war + native allies).

**25. The Mound Builders (Adena and Hopewell)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Eastern earthwork-building cultures; Serpent Mound · *src: Wikipedia Pre-Columbian; encyclopedias*  
> Surprise-reversal hook for North-American kids (moral_vision §2): vast earthworks were built across the eastern woodlands — the quarter-mile Great Serpent Mound is the named, memorable answer. Honest-record note: 19th-c. settlers invented a fictional 'lost race' to deny Native peoples built these; the truth (Native ancestors built them) corrects that myth in the stem.

**26. Cahokia and Mississippian culture** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high  
Largest North American city; Monks Mound · *src: AP World 1.4 (Kaplan/Albert); Wikipedia*  
> Strong surprise-reversal (moral_vision §2 + §6 'curiosity over completion'): a city of perhaps 10,000–20,000 near modern St. Louis around 1100 — bigger than London at the time — that most American kids have never heard of. Monks Mound (largest earthwork in the Americas) and 'Woodhenge' are the named answers. Celebrate a major North American civilization on its own terms.

**27. Ancestral Puebloans (Anasazi) of the Southwest** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Cliff dwellers; kivas, pottery, Chaco roads · *src: Britannica; Wikipedia; encyclopedia.com*  
> Present on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5). Use 'Ancestral Puebloans' as the primary name (modern Pueblo peoples find 'Anasazi', a Navajo word, less apt) — a small honest-respect note, not a lecture. The named cliff villages and kivas are the answers. Celebrate desert builders straight; the abandonment/migration is a real mystery hook, not a 'vanishing.'

**28. Cliff Palace at Mesa Verde** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
150-room cliff dwelling in Colorado · *src: Encyclopedia.com; Wikipedia Ancestral Puebloans*  
> Wonder Pattern: the named, picturable thing (a 150-room stone village tucked under a cliff overhang) is the answer. Concrete handle ideal for lower tiers. Celebrate the engineering and the choice to build into the rock; present on its own terms.

**29. Chaco Canyon and Pueblo Bonito** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Great Houses; astronomical alignments; long roads · *src: Britannica Ancestral Pueblo; Wikipedia*  
> Celebrate a real intellectual/engineering achievement on its own terms (moral_vision §4): multi-story Great Houses, dead-straight roads running for miles, and buildings aligned to solar and lunar cycles (the Sun Dagger petroglyph marking the solstices). The named site and the astronomical alignment are the answers. Deep-tier Southwest content.

**30. The Iroquois Confederacy (Haudenosaunee)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Five/Six Nations league; Great Law of Peace · *src: Wikipedia Pre-Columbian; AP US History P1*  
> Present on its own terms with intellectual seriousness (moral_vision §6). The Great Law of Peace uniting the Five (later Six) Nations is the substantive achievement and the answer. Handle the 'influence on the U.S. founders' claim HONESTLY — it is debated by historians; present it as an argued claim (Franklin admired the league), not as established fact, to avoid overstating either way. No false equivalence and no flame-bait.

**31. Three Sisters agriculture and the domestication of maize** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Corn/beans/squash; maize from teosinte · *src: AP World (Mississippian corn); AP US P1*  
> Celebrate ordinary ingenuity that reshaped the world (moral_vision §2): Native farmers bred maize from a scrawny grass (teosinte) over millennia, and the corn/beans/squash 'Three Sisters' system is real agronomy (beans fix nitrogen, corn gives the climbing pole, squash shades out weeds). The mechanism is the wonder. A genuine Indigenous contribution to global diets — stated as the achievement it is.

**32. The Bering land bridge and peopling of the Americas** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Beringia migration; earliest Americans · *src: AP US History Period 1 (1491); Britannica*  
> Honest about live scientific debate (moral_vision §4, not 'settled'): present Beringia migration as the leading model AND note that newer evidence (e.g., very early coastal/southern sites) keeps pushing dates back and complicating the single-corridor story. 'How and when the first people reached the Americas' is an open, fascinating question — frame it as discovery in progress, with the migration route as the answer at the appropriate tier.

**33. Clovis points and the first big-game hunters** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Fluted spear points; mammoth hunting · *src: Wikipedia Pre-Columbian; archaeology canon*  
> Wonder + honest-debate (moral_vision §4): the distinctively fluted Clovis spear point is the named answer, and the image of hunting mammoths is the hook. Note honestly that 'Clovis First' is no longer settled — pre-Clovis sites are now widely accepted. Deep-tier archaeology that rewards the fan; frame the controversy as live science, not a closed case.

**34. Las Casas, the Salamanca School, and the debate over Indigenous rights** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Bartolomé de las Casas defends Native peoples; Francisco de Vitoria and the School of Salamanca (Suárez) found natural-rights theory on Indigenous humanity; the 1550–51 Valladolid debate · *src: vision-injected*  
> This is the named Western/Christian response the framework EXPLICITLY pairs with Aztec sacrifice and conquest (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §3.7: 'ended by Spanish conquest + Las Casas + the Salamanca School's natural-rights theology (Vitoria, Suárez)'; moral_vision §3.4 names 'the Spanish Scholastics at Salamanca (Vitoria, Suarez) on the rights of Indigenous peoples'). Tell it straight: Las Casas, a Spanish friar who first held Indigenous laborers and then spent his life condemning the system, argued before the crown that Native peoples were fully human with natural rights; Vitoria built international-law and natural-rights theory on that premise; the 1550–51 Valladolid debate pitted Las Casas against Sepúlveda. Real specifics are the answers (the named friar, the named debate). NOT triumphalist and NOT self-loathing — the same honest record that states the conquest's brutality also states that the natural-rights critique of it came from within the Christian West.

**35. The Columbian Exchange and Indigenous disease mortality** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Old World diseases (smallpox, measles) devastate Indigenous populations after 1492; two-way transfer of crops, animals, and pathogens between hemispheres · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §4 uses this as its CANONICAL example of 'honest, not cynical': 'The Columbian Exchange caused devastating Indigenous mortality from disease — state it. Don't editorialize.' So: state plainly that Old World diseases (especially smallpox, to which Native peoples had no immunity) killed a large share of the Indigenous population — the largest demographic catastrophe of the contact era — and that this disease mortality, not Spanish arms alone, is why small forces could topple vast empires. NO 'modern audiences find this troubling' framing, NO theatrical reveal, NO editorializing about guilt. Also present the two-way wonder: maize, potatoes, tomatoes, and chocolate going east reshaped Old World diets; horses going west reshaped Plains life. The named disease / the named crop is the answer.

**36. The Aztec Sun Stone (calendar stone)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
The massive carved basalt Sun Stone unearthed in Mexico City 1790; Aztec cosmology of the five suns/world-ages · *src: vision-injected*  
> HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 6 names 'the Aztec calendar' specifically among substantive non-Western achievements to celebrate. Wonder Pattern: the named object — a 24-ton carved basalt disk nearly 12 feet across, buried after the conquest and rediscovered under Mexico City's main square in 1790 — is the cool-fact answer. Present the Aztec cosmology it encodes (the five world-ages / 'five suns') on its own terms, seriously, with the central sun-face (Tonatiuh) as a concrete handle. Celebrate the artistry and the astronomy; distinct from the Maya Long Count topic.


## Sub-Saharan & other world civilizations (39)

**1. Mansa Musa & the Mali Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Richest king's Mecca pilgrimage, gold · *src: Core Knowledge G4; AP World*  
> Present on its own terms as a substantive civilization, NOT an exotic foil (moral_vision §4). Tell the 1324 Hajj as the wonder it is — the cool fact is the answer (so much gold given away in Cairo that Egypt's gold price stayed depressed for ~a decade). Named-things hierarchy: the hajj, the gold, Sankore. No condescension; medieval West Africa's wealth and reach shatter the 'no African history' myth.

**2. Sundiata Keita, the Lion King of Mali** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Mali's founder; epic of Sundiata · *src: Core Knowledge G4; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Story-led founding epic — real history behind a legend, told straight like Arthur or any Western founding tale (no 'primitive myth' framing). The boy who could not walk rising to found an empire is the wonder hook. Distinguish the oral-tradition legend from the documented history without condescension toward either.

**3. Timbuktu: city of books and scholars** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Trade hub, Sankore, manuscripts · *src: Core Knowledge G4; AP World*  
> Framework-named (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6: 'University of Sankore at Timbuktu'). An African center of learning — celebrate the manuscripts and scholarship on their own terms. Honest comparison is allowed but this is a genuine intellectual achievement, not a foil. The surviving manuscripts are the cool-fact answer.

**4. Empire of Ghana, the Land of Gold** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Gold-salt trade, first Sudanic empire · *src: Core Knowledge G4; AP World*  
> First great West African trade empire on its own terms. The wonder: salt once traded ounce-for-ounce with gold because the desert made it scarcer than the metal. Named/vivid-fact hierarchy over generic 'trade empire' labels.

**5. Songhai Empire & Askia the Great** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Largest West African empire, Gao · *src: Core Knowledge G4; AP World*  
> Peak West African state, substantive administration and scholarship presented straight. Honest about the 1591 fall to a Moroccan army crossing the Sahara with firearms — stated as fact, not theatricalized. The cool fact (gunpowder ended the empire) is the answer.

**6. Trans-Saharan trade & the salt-gold caravans** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Camel caravans, salt, gold, Islam spread · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge*  
> The mechanism that made the Saharan empires rise — ordinary ingenuity (the camel caravan crossing the world's harshest desert). Concrete image: salt slabs from Taghaza, gold from Wangara, neither side seeing the other. Wonder-first; the spread of Islam rides along the route as fact, not advocacy.

**7. Ibn Battuta, the great Muslim traveler** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
30-year journey across Africa/Asia · *src: Core Knowledge G4; AP World*  
> Courage/curiosity in pursuit of the world — the greatest medieval traveler, ~75,000 miles over 30 years, as eyewitness to Mali, the Swahili coast, and beyond. Story-led with the specific detail as answer. His Rihla is the named thing.

**8. Kingdom & Bronzes of Benin** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
West African brass plaques, oba, walls · *src: AP World; British Museum canon*  
> Celebrate the sophisticated forest kingdom and its stunning lost-wax brass casting on its own terms — genuine artistic achievement, the named Benin Bronzes as the cool-fact answer. SEPARATELY, the framework requires honesty about Benin's human-sacrifice altars piled with skulls encountered at the 1897 British Punitive Expedition (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5) — covered in the injected brutality topic, not warped into this art cell. Do not sanitize, do not theatricalize.

**9. Kingdom of Aksum (Axum)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Red Sea empire, obelisks, coinage · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Early African empire with its own coinage, script (Ge'ez), and towering carved stelae — substantive, on its own terms. The single 24m granite obelisk (tallest the ancient world tried to erect) is the named wonder. One of the few ancient states minting its own gold coins.

**10. Ezana of Aksum & Ethiopian Christianity** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
First Christian African king, 4th c. · *src: AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Christianity as a substantive historical force (moral_vision §3.4) on the African plane — Africa Christian centuries before most of Europe, ~340 AD under Ezana. Told straight, no smug believer OR atheist voice (moral_vision §4). The coins switching from crescent to cross is the concrete detail.

**11. Lalibela's rock-hewn churches** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Ethiopian churches carved from solid rock · *src: Encyclopedias; UNESCO canon*  
> Framework-named ('Lalibela's churches carved below ground', HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6). Pure wonder + beauty of mechanism: eleven churches cut DOWNWARD into living volcanic rock, monolithic, still in use ~800 years later. The carved-from-one-stone fact is the answer. Belongs alongside Chartres and Brunelleschi as architecture-as-inheritance, just non-Western.

**12. The Swahili Coast city-states** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Kilwa, Mombasa, Indian Ocean trade · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> African-Arab-Persian trading cities on their own terms; the birth of the Swahili language from that fusion is the named cool fact. Kilwa's coral-stone palace (Husuni Kubwa) and the monsoon-timed dhow trade are concrete handles. Wonder-first.

**13. Great Zimbabwe** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Massive stone city, no mortar, cattle/gold · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge supp.*  
> Framework-named (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6: 'Great Zimbabwe's stone city'). Pure wonder: the largest ancient stone structure in sub-Saharan Africa, ~11m walls of fitted granite laid WITHOUT mortar. The mortarless construction is the answer. Honest aside available: colonial-era authorities long refused to credit Africans with building it — stated as fact, not theatricalized.

**14. The Bantu migrations** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Iron, farming, language spread across Africa · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> How most of sub-Saharan Africa was peopled and farmed — substantive on its own terms. The wonder is the scale: one language family carrying iron and farming across a continent over ~2,000 years. Concrete (the iron and the yam/banana, not abstract 'migration theory'). Avoid jargon walls per moral_vision §6.

**15. Iron-working in Sub-Saharan Africa (Nok)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Nok culture, terracotta, early iron · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Early African ironworking + the famous Nok terracotta heads as genuine achievement, on its own terms. The hollow-eyed terracotta head is the named-thing answer. Ordinary ingenuity (smelting iron from the earth) celebrated without exotic-foil framing.

**16. Kingdom of Kush & Nubia (Meroë)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
Nubian pharaohs, pyramids, iron at Meroë · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World*  
> Substantive on its own terms — the wonder/surprise-reversal the framework loves: Black Nubian pharaohs (the 25th Dynasty) actually RULED Egypt, and Nubia built MORE pyramids than Egypt did (the steep little Meroitic pyramids). The 'who ruled whom' reversal and the pyramid count are the cool-fact answers.

**17. Mansa & griots: African oral tradition** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Griots as living libraries, oral history · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> History kept alive by trained memory and song, not writing — presented with full seriousness, the same kind of intellectual feat as a Western scribal tradition (no condescension, moral_vision §4). The griot as a living library is the named concrete handle. Note: scope label 'Mansa & griots' is loose — the griot is the real subject; frame the question around the griot, not a confused 'mansa' tie-in.

**18. Kingdom of Kongo** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Central African kingdom, early Atlantic contact · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Powerful Central African state on its own terms that met Portugal early (~1480s) and whose king Afonso I converted and corresponded with Lisbon. Honest thread available (and required if the slave trade is raised): Afonso's own letters protesting Portuguese slavers carrying off his people — primary-source fact, stated plainly, connects to the injected Atlantic/internal slave-trade record.

**19. Shaka Zulu & the Zulu kingdom** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Military reformer, iklwa, mfecane · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; world history*  
> A brilliant military innovator who forged a nation — the iklwa short stabbing-spear and the bull-horns encirclement are the named cool-fact answers (great-battles pillar, non-Western). Honest, not sanitized AND not sensationalized: the mfecane's enormous death and displacement stated plainly as fact (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5 honesty standard), no 'savage' framing.

**20. The Maya civilization** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
City-states, glyphs, calendar, math, zero · *src: Core Knowledge G5; AP World*  
> Substantive on its own terms — a genuine intellectual achievement the framework celebrates: a true writing system, astronomy, and an independent invention of ZERO in the Americas. The concept-of-zero and the Long Count calendar are the cool-fact answers. Wonder-first; no exotic foil.

**21. The Aztec Empire & Tenochtitlan** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Floating city, chinampas, eagle-on-cactus · *src: Core Knowledge G5; AP World*  
> Framework-named ('Aztec calendar + Tenochtitlán on a lake', HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6). Celebrate the engineering wonder on its own terms: an island megacity of ~200,000 on a lake, fed by chinampa floating gardens, the eagle-on-cactus founding legend kids meet. The chinampas and the causeways are the cool-fact answers. The honest sacrifice record is a SEPARATE injected topic (Templo Mayor 1487) — do not warp this engineering/founding cell into it, and do not sanitize it away either.

**22. The Inca Empire & Machu Picchu** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Andes empire, roads, quipu, terraces · *src: Core Knowledge G5; AP World*  
> Framework-named ('Inca road network 40,000 km + terraced agriculture', HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6). Ordinary-ingenuity wonder: a mountain empire that ran a 40,000 km road network and kept its records on knotted-string quipu — with NO writing and NO wheel. The quipu and the road network are the cool-fact answers. Mortar-free Sacsayhuaman stonework as concrete handle.

**23. The Olmec: mother culture of Mesoamerica** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Colossal stone heads, earliest Mesoamerica · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> The first great American culture on its own terms. The named wonder: colossal basalt heads, some over 3m and 20+ tons, with the stone hauled ~100 km from distant quarries with no wheels or draft animals. The transport feat and the giant heads are the cool-fact answers.

**24. Cahokia & Mississippian mound-builders** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Monks Mound, largest pre-Columbian US city · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World*  
> Surprise-reversal the framework loves: a great city of earthen pyramids NORTH of Mexico (near modern St. Louis), bigger than London at its peak (~1100s), with Monks Mound larger at its base than the Great Pyramid. The 'a city THAT big in North America' reversal is the answer. Substantive, on its own terms.

**25. Polynesian wayfinding & settling the Pacific** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Star navigation, canoes, vast ocean voyages · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Framework-named ('Polynesian wayfinding by stars + wave patterns', HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6). One of the bank's flagship wonders: navigators crossing thousands of miles of open Pacific with no instruments — reading stars, swell patterns, cloud reflections, and seabird flight. The non-instrument wayfinding method is the cool-fact answer. Ordinary ingenuity at its most astonishing.

**26. Easter Island (Rapa Nui) moai** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Giant stone statues, isolated island society · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Pure wonder + beauty of mechanism: hundreds of giant stone moai on the world's most isolated inhabited island. The named cool fact is the engineering puzzle — how they 'walked' the multi-ton statues upright with ropes. Present the ecological-collapse story honestly but note the older 'ecocide' account is now contested by scholars; do not assert a single settled morality tale.

**27. Borobudur & Srivijaya (maritime SE Asia)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Giant Buddhist monument; trading thalassocracy · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Colossal stepped Buddhist temple of island SE Asia + the sea-power that traded across it — on its own terms. Named wonder: the world's largest Buddhist temple, ~2 million stone blocks, a stone mandala you climb level by level. The walk-up-the-mandala structure is the cool-fact answer.

**28. Mongol Empire & Genghis Khan** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Largest land empire, steppe horsemen, Pax Mongolica · *src: AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Framework-named ('Mongol postal system + Pax Mongolica', HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6). Substantive great-military-history pillar: the largest contiguous land empire ever, built by mounted nomads; the yam relay postal system is a named ordinary-ingenuity cool fact. Honest BOTH ways: the genuine Pax Mongolica that reopened the Silk Road AND the staggering massacres of resisting cities (Nishapur, Baghdad 1258) — stated plainly, not theatricalized, no condescension.

**29. Kublai Khan & the Yuan / Marco Polo** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Mongol China, Xanadu, Polo's travels · *src: AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> The Mongol emperor of China who hosted Marco Polo — story-led, the Venetian's eyewitness wonders (paper money, coal burning as 'black stones', the postal relays) as the cool-fact handles. Present Polo's Travels as the era-shaping account it was; note scholarly debate over how much he saw firsthand without adjudicating it as settled.

**30. Timur (Tamerlane) & Samarkand** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Central Asian conqueror, blue-tiled capital · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Honest dual record at upper tiers: the feared conqueror who built towers of skulls from sacked cities (Isfahan, Delhi) AND the patron who turned Samarkand into a blue-tiled jewel of the Silk Road (the Registan, the Gur-e-Amir). Both stated as fact, neither sanitized nor sensationalized. The brutality-plus-beauty contrast is the substance.

**31. Mound of empires' end: Atlantic slave trade origins** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Triangular trade, Middle Passage, African states · *src: AP World; AP US; Core Knowledge*  
> Hard topic, handled honest-not-cynical (moral_vision §4). State the Middle Passage and triangular trade plainly as the moral evil they were — no euphemism, no theatricalized reveal. CRITICAL per HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5: the bank's distinctive, under-taught honesty is that African coastal kingdoms (Asante, Dahomey, the Kongo) captured and SOLD the enslaved to European traders — the internal African slave trade predates and feeds the Atlantic one. Pair with the named Western abolition that ended it: Wilberforce, the Quakers, and the Royal Navy West Africa Squadron (1808-1860s) — abolition as a Western-led, Christian-led moral crusade (moral_vision §3.4). The garbled scope name 'Mound of empires' end' should be dropped; this is the Atlantic slave trade.

**32. Aztec mass human sacrifice (Templo Mayor 1487)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Templo Mayor dedication; obsidian-knife heart extraction; 20,000-80,000 over four days per Spanish + Aztec sources; Xipe Totec flayed-skin ritual · *src: vision-injected*  
> Stated as historical fact, not theatricalized and not sensationalized — NO 'savages' framing (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5). The honesty is the point: the same Aztec civilization the bank celebrates for Tenochtitlan also practiced industrial-scale human sacrifice, heart extraction with obsidian knives atop the Templo Mayor. Pair with the named Western response: Las Casas and the Salamanca School natural-rights theology (Vitoria, Suarez) arguing for Indigenous rights even as conquest ended the practice. Honest record cuts in all directions — Spanish conquest brutality belongs in the same plain-fact register.

**33. The internal African slave trade & its Western suppression** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Africans selling captives to coastal kingdoms (Asante, Dahomey) for centuries before the Atlantic trade; Tippu Tip's Zanzibar/East-African network into the 1890s; suppressed by the Royal Navy West Africa Squadron 1808-1860s + Livingstone's documentation · *src: vision-injected*  
> Stated plainly as fact, honest-not-cynical. The under-taught truth the framework names: slavery was an internal African institution for centuries before Europeans arrived, with coastal kingdoms (Asante, Dahomey) and the East-African network (Tippu Tip) as the captors and sellers. Pair with the named Western intervention that ended it — the Royal Navy West Africa Squadron (1808-1860s) and Livingstone's documentation — abolition as a Western-led moral crusade (moral_vision §3.4). No anti-anti-white framing and no group-supremacy framing (moral_vision §9); the historical record is the argument.

**34. Dahomey & Benin human sacrifice altars** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Benin's bronze altars piled with skulls encountered at the 1897 British Punitive Expedition; Dahomey's 'Annual Customs' mass killings · *src: vision-injected*  
> Stated as fact, plainly, not sensationalized (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5). The deliberate counterweight to the celebrated Benin Bronzes topic: the same kingdom's artistry and its human-sacrifice altars are part of one honest record. Dahomey's Annual Customs killings stated likewise. No 'savages' framing; the record is the argument. Upper-tier only given the gravity.

**35. Han & Tang China — astronomers, Chang'an, printing & gunpowder** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Han court astronomers recording supernovae/comets; Tang Chang'an as the world's largest city; Song-era woodblock printing and the first gunpowder · *src: vision-injected*  
> Substantive Chinese civilization on its own terms, NOT an exotic foil (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6 names 'Han astronomers; Tang Chang'an as world's largest city; Song printing + gunpowder'). Ordinary-ingenuity and discovery wonders: Han astronomers logging a 'guest star' (supernova) centuries before the West, Tang Chang'an at ~1 million people, the first printed book and the first gunpowder. The named invention/record is the cool-fact answer.

**36. Ming China — Zheng He's treasure fleets & the Forbidden City** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Zheng He's seven voyages (1405-1433) with treasure ships dwarfing European caravels; a giraffe carried back to Beijing; the abrupt imperial decision to burn the fleet and turn inward · *src: vision-injected*  
> On its own terms — the wonder/surprise-reversal the framework loves: a Chinese fleet of 300 ships and ~28,000 men reached East Africa decades BEFORE Columbus crossed the much smaller Atlantic, then the emperors chose to burn it and turn inward. The 'reached Africa first, then stopped' reversal and the treasure-ship scale are the cool-fact answers. Honest decline thread (the inward turn) stated as fact, not editorialized.

**37. Mughal India — Akbar's Sulh-i-kul & the Taj Mahal** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Akbar's Sulh-i-kul ('universal peace') policy of religious tolerance and debate house; Shah Jahan's Taj Mahal built as a tomb for Mumtaz Mahal · *src: vision-injected*  
> Substantive on its own terms (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6 names 'Mughal Akbar's Sulh-i-kul religious tolerance; Taj Mahal'). Akbar inviting scholars of every faith to debate in his Ibadat Khana is a genuine institutional achievement told straight; the Taj Mahal as a grief-monument is the named beauty-and-story answer. Architecture-as-inheritance, non-Western. Wonder-first, no exotic foil.

**38. The decline of the Islamic Golden Age** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Abbasid Baghdad's House of Wisdom; al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100) and the closing of the rationalist tradition; the Mongol sack of Baghdad 1258 ending the House of Wisdom · *src: vision-injected*  
> Stated as historical fact, NOT editorialized (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6 explicitly: 'The pattern (religious orthodoxy stifling inquiry) is stated as historical fact, not editorialized'). Honor the genuine golden-age achievement first — the House of Wisdom translation movement, the preserved Greek learning. Then the honest decline thread: al-Ghazali's critique helping close the rationalist line and the 1258 Mongol sack of Baghdad ending the House of Wisdom — a pattern the West later escaped via its own Scientific Revolution. No condescension toward Islam; the record is the argument.

**39. Fijian ritual cannibalism & its end at Christianization** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low ⭐  
Endemic ritual cannibalism in pre-Christian Fiji ('Cannibal Isles' was a literal description); ended at the mid-1800s Christianization · *src: vision-injected*  
> Stated plainly as fact, no sensationalism (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5). The honesty pattern: a real, widespread pre-modern practice ended by a named Western/Christian intervention (the mid-1800s missionary Christianization). 'Cannibal Isles' as the era's literal label is the concrete handle. Upper-tier only given the subject matter. No 'savages' framing — the historical record carries it.


## Byzantium & the Islamic world (34)

**1. Constantine founds Constantinople** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
New Rome on the Bosporus, 330 AD · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World/Euro*  
> Part of the unbroken Western inheritance (moral_vision Athens-Jerusalem-Rome-Canterbury line): the Roman state did not 'fall' in 476 so much as relocate east and live on a thousand more years. Lead with the wonder of a Christian Rome refounded on a Greek city, named for the emperor who legalized the faith. Story-led: Constantine pacing the walls he traced. No 'decline-of-Rome' fatalism.

**2. Justinian and Theodora** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Reconquest, law, empress from the circus · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Drama-of-the-great-figures pillar at full strength. Theodora's Nika-riot line ('purple makes a fine shroud') is the cool-fact-is-the-answer: she stiffened Justinian's spine and saved the throne. Tell it straight as courage under pressure (moral_vision virtue-by-example), not as a girlboss reveal. Justinian's reconquest of Italy/Africa is the high-water mark of Roman continuity.

**3. Justinian's Code (Corpus Juris Civilis)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Roman law codified, basis of Western law · *src: AP Euro; AP World; cultural literacy*  
> HEAVIEST-pillar material: Rome's bequest of law to the West (moral_vision §3.4 rule-of-law lineage; framework Pillar 1). This is the textbook that fed the medieval universities at Bologna and underwrites modern European civil law. Frame as a real human achievement that still governs lives, not a dry codification date. The cool fact (it organized centuries of contradictory edicts into one usable system) is the answer.

**4. Hagia Sophia** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Great domed church; 'Solomon, I have outdone you' · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; art-history canon*  
> Explicitly named in the framework's Western-architecture thread ('Justinian: Solomon, I have outdone thee', 532-537). Top-billing celebration of Western/Christian engineering wonder: the dome that seemed to hang from heaven on a chain. Carry the full arc honestly across the ladder — church, then mosque after 1453, then museum — stated plainly, no theatrical reveal, no editorializing about any of the three uses.

**5. Byzantine mosaics and icons** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Gold mosaics; icon veneration · *src: Art-history canon; AP World*  
> Western-art-as-inheritance pillar. Present icon veneration on its own theological terms (moral_vision: no smug atheist voice, no 'primitive belief') - the gold ground as a window into eternity, not a quaint superstition. The beauty-of-mechanism lens: WHY gold tesserae set at angles make the figures shimmer in candlelight.

**6. The Iconoclasm controversy** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Smashing holy images, 8th-9th centuries · *src: AP Euro; encyclopedias*  
> A serious theological argument, steel-manned on both sides (moral_vision §4, §6 no-strawman): the iconoclast worry about idolatry was a real reading of the Second Commandment, and the iconophile defense (the image honors the prototype, not the wood) was a real answer. Present it as a genuine fight over a hard question, not as fanatics smashing art. Higher tiers per the abstraction.

**7. Greek fire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Byzantine secret flaming naval weapon · *src: Cultural literacy; military-history canon*  
> Pure wonder + ordinary-ingenuity hook (moral_vision §2): a weapon that burned ON water and saved Constantinople from Arab fleets twice. The cool fact IS the answer - the recipe was a state secret so well kept it is still not fully known. Lead with the siphon spraying liquid fire across the Golden Horn.

**8. The Great Schism of 1054** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Catholic-Orthodox split; Pope vs Patriarch · *src: AP World; AP Euro; Core Knowledge*  
> Present BOTH sides on their own terms (moral_vision §4: no smug believer voice favoring Rome OR Constantinople). The filioque clause and papal-authority dispute were substantive theological disagreements, not a petty spat - though the mutual excommunications (legates slapping a bull on the Hagia Sophia altar) are a vivid story-led hook. Neither church is staged as the villain.

**9. Cyril, Methodius, and the Cyrillic alphabet** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Missionaries who Christianized the Slavs · *src: AP World; AP Euro; encyclopedias*  
> Reformation-prefiguring achievement honored in moral_vision (vernacular Scripture, literacy as a gift of the faith): two brothers INVENTED an alphabet so the Slavs could read Scripture in their own tongue. The cool fact - a script still used by hundreds of millions, named for one of them - is the answer. A Christian-civilization achievement told straight.

**10. Fall of Constantinople, 1453** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Ottoman cannons end the Byzantine Empire · *src: AP World; AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> A genuine civilizational hinge (framework: 'End of Rome; pushed Europe toward the Renaissance'). Tell the drama straight - Mehmed II's giant bombards, the chain across the Golden Horn, ships dragged overland, the last emperor Constantine XI dying sword in hand in the breach. Honest both ways: a real Ottoman achievement AND a real loss; Greek scholars fleeing west with their manuscripts feeds the Renaissance (ties to the injected Byzantine-preservation topic). No civilizational gloating in either direction.

**11. Muhammad and the origins of Islam** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Prophet, Mecca, the Hijra to Medina · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; world-religions canon*  
> moral_vision §9: 'honors Islam as it understands itself.' Present on its own terms, no smug atheist framing, no scare quotes, no 'Muslims believe' distancing where straight narration serves - the Hijra of 622 (year one of the Islamic calendar) is the cool-fact anchor. Equally, no believer-voice advertising it as true. Story-led: the night flight from Mecca to Medina. Theology-bank symmetric-voice discipline applies.

**12. The Quran and the Five Pillars** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Holy book; faith, prayer, alms, fasting, hajj · *src: Core Knowledge; world-religions canon*  
> Taken seriously on its own terms (moral_vision §9). Anti-rote caution: do NOT let this collapse into 'name the five pillars' definition-recall (§6 anti-rote gate) - hook each pillar to a vivid practice (the pre-dawn Ramadan meal, the direction of prayer toward Mecca, the 2.5% alms). Respectful, concrete, never a catechism drill.

**13. Mecca, the Kaaba, and the hajj** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Holiest site; the pilgrimage to Mecca · *src: Core Knowledge; world-religions canon*  
> Wonder of scale told respectfully: millions circling the Kaaba in white ihram, the largest annual human gathering on Earth. Present on its own terms (moral_vision §9). The cool concrete image - all pilgrims dressed identically so prince and pauper are indistinguishable - is the memorable answer.

**14. The early Muslim conquests** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Arab armies spread from Spain to Persia · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Honest history both ways (moral_vision §4: state what happened, don't editorialize, don't sanitize). The speed is the genuine wonder - within a century, from the Atlantic to the Indus, two exhausted empires (Byzantine and Sasanian) toppled. State it as the staggering military fact it was; the conquered Christian/Zoroastrian populations and the jizya tax are part of the same plain record, neither hidden nor sensationalized.

**15. Sunni-Shia split** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Succession dispute; Ali, Karbala, Husayn · *src: AP World; world-religions canon*  
> Present both sides on their own terms (moral_vision §4, §6 no strawman of either branch). The martyrdom of Husayn at Karbala in 680 - vastly outnumbered, killed with his family - is the story-led emotional core that still defines Shia devotion (Ashura). A succession question (who rightfully leads after the Prophet) that became a theological divide. No verdict on which branch is 'right.'

**16. The Umayyad Caliphate and Damascus** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
First dynasty; Arab empire from Damascus · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> On its own terms as a real civilization (framework Pillar 5, not an exotic foil). The Dome of the Rock (691, one of Islam's oldest surviving monuments) and the empire stretching from Spain to India are the concrete anchors. Straightforward dynastic history with a wonder hook, not a value-judgment.

**17. The Abbasid Caliphate and Baghdad** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Golden-age dynasty; round city of Baghdad · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge*  
> Non-Western civilization on its own terms (framework Pillar 5). The Round City of Baghdad - planned in a perfect circle, becoming the largest city in the world - is the wonder hook. This is the seat of the Golden Age the framework says to cover 'but not over-weighted.' Celebrate the real achievement; the decline thread (injected topic) is the honest other half.

**18. The House of Wisdom** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Baghdad center translating Greek and Persian texts · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Explicitly named in framework Pillar 5 / §6 ('Abbasid Baghdad's House of Wisdom translation movement'). Courage-and-curiosity-in-pursuit-of-truth (moral_vision §2, where Ibn Sina is a named exemplar): scholars translating Aristotle, Galen, and Euclid, preserving Greek learning the West had largely lost. Honest nuance available at high tiers: this preserved-and-transmitted role is real AND its rationalist tradition later hardened shut (see injected decline topic). The cool fact is the answer, not the abstraction.

**19. Harun al-Rashid and the 1001 Nights** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Legendary caliph; Arabian Nights tales · *src: Cultural literacy; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder + cultural-literacy hook: the real caliph at the Golden Age's peak who became the legendary backdrop for Scheherazade, Aladdin, and Sinbad. Concrete cool fact - he exchanged gifts (an elephant, an elaborate water clock) with Charlemagne - is the answer. Distinguish the historical man from the storybook frame cleanly; no fabricated 'Nights' details presented as history.

**20. Islamic Golden Age science and math** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Algebra, al-Khwarizmi, optics, the astrolabe · *src: AP World; STEM-history canon*  
> Framework Pillar 5 names al-Khwarizmi's algebra and Ibn al-Haytham's optics explicitly. Real human achievement celebrated honestly (moral_vision §2 ordinary ingenuity + beauty of mechanism): 'algebra' and 'algorithm' are Arabic-derived words; Ibn al-Haytham did controlled optics experiments centuries before the 'scientific method' was named. Cool-fact-is-the-answer. Covered but not over-weighted per framework; the decline thread is the honest counterpart.

**21. Arabic numerals and the spread of zero** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Number system carried from India to Europe · *src: Cultural literacy; math-history canon*  
> Wonder of transmission (moral_vision §2): the digits the whole world now writes traveled from India, through Arabic mathematicians, to Europe - which is why we call them 'Arabic' numerals though they began in India. Honest about the chain (Hindu-Arabic) rather than crediting one culture alone. Beauty-of-mechanism: WHY a place-value zero made arithmetic explode in power versus Roman numerals.

**22. Avicenna (Ibn Sina) and Islamic medicine** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Canon of Medicine; medieval medical authority · *src: AP World; medicine-history canon*  
> Ibn Sina is a NAMED courage-in-pursuit-of-truth exemplar in moral_vision §2 ('Galileo, Hypatia, Ibn Sina, Mary Anning...'). Top billing among Golden-Age figures. The Canon of Medicine taught in European universities for 600 years is the cool fact. A real human achievement of the mind, told as the drama of a polymath (philosopher, physician, astronomer). Higher tiers per the framework's tier_span.

**23. Al-Andalus and Moorish Spain** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Muslim Spain; Cordoba's library and learning · *src: AP World; AP Euro*  
> Non-Western civilization on its own terms inside medieval Europe (framework Pillar 5). Cordoba's vast library and paved, lamp-lit streets while much of Europe was rural are the genuine wonder. Honest, not romanticized: present the convivencia AND its limits plainly (dhimmi status, periodic persecution) - no theatrical 'lost paradise' OR 'it was secretly oppressive' reveal (moral_vision §6 both-directions TIL ban). The Reconquista context belongs in the Crusades/Spain strand.

**24. The Great Mosque of Cordoba and the Alhambra** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Striped arches; Granada's palace fortress · *src: Art-history canon; AP Euro*  
> Architectural wonder on its own terms (framework Pillar 5 art coverage). The forest of red-and-white double arches in the Mezquita and the impossibly fine stucco of the Alhambra are masterpieces - beauty-of-mechanism lens (HOW the horseshoe arches carry the weight). Cool-fact-is-the-answer (e.g. the Court of the Lions' hidden water-clock fountain). Honor the building; the later cathedral-inside-the-mosque is plain history, not a reveal.

**25. Saladin and the recapture of Jerusalem** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Kurdish sultan who retook Jerusalem, 1187 · *src: AP World; AP Euro; encyclopedias*  
> Saladin is in the framework's headline roll of names kids should know ('...Saladin...') and is explicitly framed as the 'chivalrous foe of the Crusaders.' Drama-of-the-great-figures pillar. Tell the virtue straight (moral_vision: show virtue by example): his clemency at Jerusalem in 1187 (ransoming and freeing inhabitants) contrasted with the 1099 Crusader bloodbath, and his mutual respect with Richard the Lionheart. Honest both ways - a great and humane commander who was also Islam's champion against the Crusader states. No flattening into hero OR villain.

**26. Mansa Musa and the Mali pilgrimage** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Richest man ever; gold-laden hajj, 1324 · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Explicitly named in framework Pillar 5 / §6 ('Mansa Musa's 1324 Hajj inflating Egypt's gold supply'). Non-Western civilization on its own terms, a genuine wonder. The cool fact IS the answer: he gave away SO much gold crossing Cairo that he crashed its value for a decade - one of the few times one man's generosity moved a regional economy. Tie to the framework's Sankore/Timbuktu learning thread.

**27. Timbuktu and trans-Saharan trade** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Desert city of gold, salt, and books · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Framework Pillar 5 names 'Mali's University of Sankore at Timbuktu.' African center of learning on its own terms, NOT an exotic foil (moral_vision §4 no condescension to any culture). The wonder: a desert city where books were worth more than gold and salt traded ounce-for-ounce with gold. Hundreds of thousands of manuscripts - the cool concrete fact - is the answer.
