**28. Mosque architecture: minaret, dome, mihrab** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low  
Parts of a mosque; call to prayer · *src: Art-history canon; Core Knowledge*  
> Anti-rote risk is HIGH (moral_vision §6): a 'what is a mihrab?' definition drill fails the gate. Reframe each element by FUNCTION/usage - the mihrab niche points the way to Mecca, the minaret carries the muezzin's call across the city. Present respectfully on its own terms. Concrete usage beats vocabulary recall.

**29. Islamic art: calligraphy and geometric patterns** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
No figures; arabesque, tile, script as art · *src: Art-history canon; AP World*  
> Present on its own theological terms (moral_vision §9, §4 no condescension): the avoidance of figural images in sacred art turned WRITING itself into the supreme art form, and turned geometry into worship. Beauty-of-mechanism: HOW a handful of rules generate infinitely complex non-repeating tile patterns (anticipating ideas mathematicians formalized only recently). Genuine wonder, told straight.

**30. Ibn Battuta's travels** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Greatest medieval traveler, 75,000 miles · *src: AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Named in framework Pillar 5 context as a Golden-Age figure. Drama/wonder of one human life: ~75,000 miles across the whole known Muslim world and beyond, three times Marco Polo's distance, dictated as the Rihla. Story-led - a young scholar who set out for one hajj and did not come home for 24 years. The staggering distance is the cool-fact answer.

**31. The Rise of the Ottoman Empire** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Turkish empire; janissaries, Mehmed II, sultans · *src: AP World; AP Euro*  
> A real empire on its own terms (framework Pillar 5), the power that ended Byzantium and shaped southeastern Europe for 500 years. Honest history (moral_vision §4): the janissaries via the devshirme (Christian boys levied, converted, and raised as elite soldiers) is a genuinely strange institution stated plainly - neither sanitized nor sensationalized. Suleiman the Magnificent as lawgiver. The siege of Vienna 1683 belongs in the Crusades/Europe strand (Sobieski).

**32. The Crusades** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Christian wars for the Holy Land · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; AP Euro*  
> Honest both ways, no theatrical reveal in either direction (moral_vision §4, §6, framework). State the real motives (pilgrimage protection, Byzantine appeal for aid after Manzikert, papal call) AND the real atrocities (the 1099 Jerusalem massacre; the 1204 sack of Christian Constantinople by the Fourth Crusade - a genuine scandal). Avoid both the 'barbaric Christian aggression' frame and the pure-pilgrimage whitewash. Steel-man the medieval worldview without endorsing or condemning by modern slogan. Pairs with Saladin.

**33. The closing of the Islamic Golden Age (al-Ghazali, 1258, hardening orthodoxy)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Why the rationalist golden age ended: al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100), the Mongol sack of Baghdad and the House of Wisdom (1258), and the subsequent hardening of orthodoxy that prevented an internal Renaissance · *src: vision-injected*  
> EXPLICITLY MANDATED by HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 5 / §6, almost verbatim: 'The decline thread is part of honest history: al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100) helped close the golden age's rationalist tradition; Mongol destruction of Baghdad 1258 ended the House of Wisdom; subsequent orthodoxy hardening prevented the recovery the West achieved through its own Renaissance and Scientific Revolution. The pattern (religious orthodoxy stifling inquiry) is stated as historical fact, not editorialized.' Story-led at high tiers: the Tigris said to run black with ink from libraries thrown in the river in 1258. The honest counterweight to the Golden-Age-celebration topics - without it the strand tells only the triumphal half. No gloating, no anti-Islam framing; the same kind of honest decline-narrative the bank applies to every civilization.

**34. Byzantium as the bridge that preserved Greek learning for the West** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Byzantine monks and scholars copying and preserving classical Greek texts (Plato, Aristotle, Euclid, Archimedes) for a millennium; Greek scholars fleeing to Italy after 1453 carrying manuscripts that helped ignite the Renaissance · *src: vision-injected*  
> Foregrounds the WHY behind the framework's note that 1453 'pushed Europe toward the Renaissance.' moral_vision §2 explicitly celebrates 'the unnamed monks who copied through plague years' as courage-in-pursuit-of-truth, and Pillar 1 (Western tradition unbroken) is the HEAVIEST. The texts that fed the Italian Renaissance survived because Byzantine scribes copied them by hand for a thousand years; refugee Greek scholars (e.g. carrying Plato to Florence's new academy) handed them west as the empire fell. The cool fact - that the Renaissance was partly a deathbed gift from Eastern Rome - is the answer. Virtue-by-example, told straight, no preaching.


## Medieval Europe (53)

**1. Fall of the Western Roman Empire (476)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Rome falls; Odoacer deposes Romulus Augustulus · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Medieval Europe; AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Present as the hinge where the Western tradition does NOT die but transmutes — Rome's law, language, and roads outlive the empire, and Christendom inherits the wreckage. Wonder Pattern: the boy-emperor's name (Romulus Augustulus, ironically echoing Rome's founder) and Odoacer sending the imperial regalia to Constantinople are the cool-fact answers, not the date. Avoid 'Dark Ages' decline-narrative editorializing.

**2. Byzantine Empire & Justinian** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Eastern Rome survives; Justinian, Theodora, Hagia Sophia · *src: Core Knowledge (Eastern Roman Empire); AP World/Euro*  
> Pillar 1 (Western tradition unbroken) at its core: Rome lived a thousand more years in the East. Lead with Justinian's 'Solomon, I have outdone thee' at Hagia Sophia's dedication (537) and Theodora's 'purple makes a fine shroud' steadying him in the Nika riots — vivid human moments per the framework's named medieval-art thread. Hagia Sophia's still-standing dome ties to the bank's 'beauty of mechanism' celebration.

**3. Rise of Islam & expansion to Europe's edge** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Muhammad, caliphate conquests, Iberia 711 · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; encyclopedias*  
> Present on its own terms, seriously and without smug-atheist or smug-believer voice (moral_vision §4). The hijra of 622, Tariq ibn Ziyad landing at the rock that bears his name (Jabal Tariq → Gibraltar) and burning his boats are the wonder hooks. Honest comparison, not false equivalence: the framework wants Islam covered substantively AND the later decline thread told honestly elsewhere. No condescension toward the tradition.

**4. Battle of Tours / Charles Martel (732)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Frankish hammer halts Muslim advance into Gaul · *src: Western-civ canon; AP Euro*  
> Named in HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3 (Tours 732, Charles Martel). Wonder Pattern: the epithet 'the Hammer' (Martel) is the Tier-1 named-thing answer. Present as a genuine turning point in Western-civ canon without overclaiming a civilizational inevitability — honest history states the stakes plainly. Distractors: real adjacent Frankish/Carolingian facts.

**5. Charlemagne & the Carolingian Empire** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Crowned emperor 800; unites much of Western Europe · *src: Core Knowledge (Charlemagne); AP Euro; encyclopedias*  
> Pillar 1 keystone, named in templates ('Charlemagne crowned 800'). Lead with the surprise coronation — Leo III crowning him Emperor on Christmas Day 800 supposedly without warning (Einhard's claim he'd have skipped Mass had he known) — a human-moment wonder hook. Frame as the first reknitting of the Roman/Christian West. His near-illiteracy yet patronage of learning is a sticky reversal.

**6. The Carolingian Renaissance** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Alcuin, monastic schools, Carolingian minuscule script · *src: AP Euro; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Strongly vision-mandated: §3.4 (Christian intellectual heritage) + 'the unnamed monks who copied through plague years' (§2 courage-in-truth). The cool fact: Carolingian minuscule — the lowercase script Alcuin's scribes standardized — is the ancestor of the letters this sentence is printed in; most surviving classical Latin texts reach us through these copies. A monastic-preservation wonder, top of the 'ordinary ingenuity' celebration.

**7. Viking raids & Lindisfarne (793)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high  
Norse longships, raids, Danelaw, Leif Erikson · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> High kid-fascination; present the Norse on their own terms (their world was different, not 'primitive' — moral_vision §4). Wonder hooks: Leif Erikson reaching Vinland ~500 years before Columbus, the longship's clinker-built shallow draft, Lindisfarne 793 as the monastic-world's shock. Honest about the raids' brutality on monasteries without theatricalized 'savages' framing. Pairs naturally with the Christianization-of-Scandinavia arc (see injected topic).

**8. Feudalism (lords, vassals, fiefs)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Land-for-loyalty pyramid; homage and obligation · *src: Core Knowledge (feudal system); AP Euro*  
> Foundational social-order content; present concretely (the homage ceremony, hands-between-hands, the fief as land-for-service) per 'concrete over abstract.' Frame honestly as a decentralized order born of collapsed central authority — neither romanticized nor condemned. Mechanism over jargon: a 14-year-old must grab a word in the first ten.

**9. Manorialism & the medieval peasant** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Self-sufficient manor; serfs, demesne, three-field system · *src: Core Knowledge (manors); AP Euro*  
> 'How most people actually lived' — ordinary-life texture the bank values. Wonder hook: the three-field rotation as a genuine agricultural ingenuity (raised yields ~50% over two-field) and the heavy mouldboard plough opening northern Europe's clay soils. No condescension toward premodern people; their world was different, their solutions were clever.

**10. Knights, chivalry & the code** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T3 · wt=high  
Knighthood, dubbing, chivalric code, tournaments · *src: Core Knowledge (knights/chivalry); cultural-literacy canon*  
> The iconic image kids picture instantly — high T1 wonder. Present chivalry as a real (if imperfectly practiced) ideal of virtue, courage, and protection of the weak — virtue-by-example, not preached. Ties to the Arthurian and Crusader scenery. Honest that the ideal and the reality often diverged. Concrete named things (the accolade, the vigil-at-arms, the destrier).

**11. Castles & siege warfare** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high  
Motte-and-bailey to concentric; moat, keep, trebuchet · *src: Core Knowledge (castles); cultural-literacy canon*  
> Core kid fascination + 'beauty of mechanism.' Wonder hooks: the trebuchet's counterweight physics, concentric walls' overlapping fields of fire, the murder-hole and the bent gatehouse, mining-and-countermining. Vivid concrete objects with sticky detail — exactly the bank's wheelhouse. Named real engines (Warwolf, the great trebuchet) for Tier-1 named-thing answers.

**12. Medieval knight's arms & armor** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Mail to plate, lance, sword, heraldry on shields · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Vivid concrete objects — the bank's 'image-bearing language beats jargon' sweet spot. Wonder hooks: heraldry's blazon language, the evolution mail→plate, why a knight needed help mounting (myth-vs-reality: plate armour was NOT immobilizing — a behind-the-scenes wonder per templates). Named things (Durendal, Joyeuse, Excalibur cross-reference to legend/theology).

Present seriously on its own terms (no smug voice either way). Honest about real power and real abuses (simony, the later indulgence trade) without theatricalized reveal. Excommunication/interdict as concrete instruments of medieval power.

The scriptorium preserved classical learning; 'ora et labora' (pray and work) as a transmissible virtue-by-example. Wonder hooks: monks as Europe's librarians, the Rule's humane moderation, monasteries draining swamps and (later) inventing champagne — ordinary ingenuity. Top of the courage/preservation celebration.

Wonder hooks: the Chi-Rho page's impossible density, lapis-lazuli blue carried from Afghanistan, a single Gospel-book taking a scribe's lifetime. 'Beauty of mechanism' applied to the made object. Concrete named manuscripts as Tier-1 named-thing answers.

The church-state separation question is a deep root of Western liberty (the distinction between secular and sacred authority the natural-rights tradition inherited). Wonder hook: Henry IV standing barefoot in the snow at Canossa for three days — the phrase 'going to Canossa' still means abject submission. A human-moment answer, not a date.

Neither crusade-glorification nor a guilt-narrative; the bank states what happened and lets the player form a view.

**23. Magna Carta (1215)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐

> HEAVILY vision-mandated and named in the framework's opening roll-call ('Magna Carta') + templates. A cornerstone of the Locke→Founders rule-of-law/liberty lineage the bank stands in: no free man imprisoned but by lawful judgement of his peers (clause 39 → due process → the Fifth Amendment). Frame as the medieval taproot of Anglo-American liberty. Wonder hook: the Runnymede meadow, the king who repudiated it within months, the four surviving 1215 originals.

**24. Origins of Parliament & the English Commons** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Simon de Montfort 1265; king must consult · *src: Core Knowledge (Parliament); AP Euro*  
> Vision-mandated as the medieval seedbed of representative self-government in the bank's classical-liberal lineage. Frame as 'the king must consult' becoming an institution — the deep ancestor of the consent-of-the-governed principle the American Founding inherited. Wonder hook: Simon de Montfort summoning burgesses from the towns in 1265, widening the circle beyond barons. No rote 'who founded Parliament' framing — ask the vivid specific.

**25. Rise of medieval towns, guilds & commerce** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Burghers, charters, craft guilds, Hanseatic League · *src: Core Knowledge (growth of towns); AP Euro*  
> Economic-liberty seedbed the bank values: 'Stadtluft macht frei' — town air makes you free (a serf who lived a year and a day in a chartered town became free). That proverb is the cool-fact answer and a genuine early liberty mechanism. Guilds and the Hanseatic League as the commercial reawakening; honest that guilds both protected craft and restricted entry (a real economic trade-off, lightly).

**26. Medieval universities & scholasticism** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Bologna, Paris, Oxford; faith-and-reason method · *src: AP Euro; Fiveable AP-Euro study guide*  
> STRONGLY vision-mandated: §3.4 names the medieval universities (Bologna, Paris, Oxford, Cambridge) as ecclesiastical foundations and a real human achievement. This is the institution we still use, born in Christendom. Frame faith-and-reason (the scholastic method, disputation) as a genuine intellectual flowering, not a 'dark-ages' foil. Wonder hook: Bologna's students hiring and fining their own professors; the birth of the lecture, the degree, the 'university.'

**27. Thomas Aquinas & the Summa Theologica** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐

> HEAVILY vision-mandated: framework names Aquinas in the Christendom roll-call AND §3.4 ties his natural-law theology directly to the natural-rights lineage feeding Locke and the Founders. Treat as a towering mind who married Aristotle to Christian faith. Wonder/drama hook: after a mystical experience near the end he called all his writing 'so much straw' and stopped — a human-at-the-breaking-point moment fitting the drama-of-minds pillar. Present his arguments, do not adopt or mock (anti-advocacy rule).

**28. Gothic cathedrals (Chartres, Notre-Dame)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐

Western architecture-as-inheritance celebrated + 'beauty of mechanism' (why the flying buttress lets walls become glass). Wonder hooks: the Blue-of-Chartres glass whose recipe is still not fully reproduced; cathedrals taking centuries so no builder saw his work finished. Cross-references the 2019 Notre-Dame fire + restoration.

**31. Chaucer's Canterbury Tales** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Pilgrims' tales in Middle English; the Wife of Bath · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; English-lit canon*

Venetian at Kublai Khan's court; travel book · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Named in templates' China thread ('Yuan + Kublai Khan + Marco Polo at court') and Mongol Pax Mongolica. The traveler who opened the East to the European imagination. The catastrophe that broke the medieval order — state the mortality (a third to half of Europe) plainly, honestly, without theatricalizing. Wonder/mechanism hooks: the Genoese ships at Messina, the Mongol siege of Caffa flinging plague-dead over the walls (early biological warfare), 'ring around the rosie' folk-etymology (myth-vs-reality candidate). Pairs with the labor/serfdom upheaval that followed.

Peasants' Revolt 1381, Jacquerie, flagellants, wage shifts · *src: AP Euro; encyclopedias*

Wonder hooks: John Ball's 'When Adam delved and Eve span, who was then the gentleman?'; the boy-king Richard II facing the rebels at Smithfield. Honest about the revolts' suppression.

> Named in templates' medieval-music thread ('Hildegard of Bingen, Gregorian chant, Notre-Dame school polyphony'). A brilliant nun — composer, visionary, natural-philosopher, correspondent of popes and emperors. Virtue/genius by example. **47. Medieval Jewish life & persecution** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Jewish communities, expulsions, blood libel, ghettos · *src: AP Euro; encyclopedias*  
> Honest record of a persecuted medieval minority — exactly the bank's 'honest, not cynical; state what happened' stance (§4). State the expulsions (England 1290, Spain 1492), the blood-libel slander, and the pogroms plainly, without theatricalized reveal and without either minimizing or weaponizing. Balance with substantive Jewish achievement: Rashi's commentaries, Maimonides (Guide for the Perplexed) as a towering medieval mind bridging Aristotle and faith. Present the tradition on its own terms.

**48. Medieval medicine, the calendar & daily life** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Four humors, barber-surgeons, fairs, food, festivals · *src: Encyclopedias; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Texture of ordinary life — the bank values this. No condescension toward premodern medicine: the four-humors theory was a coherent system reasoned from observation, not stupidity (Aristotle-was-not-stupid principle). Wonder hooks: barber-surgeons' red-and-white pole still outside barbershops today; the festival calendar structuring the year; genuinely effective herbal remedies amid the bloodletting. Cross-references science (myth-vs-reality: educated medieval people knew Earth was round).

**49. Aquinas, the Salamanca School & the natural-law taproot of rights** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Aquinas's natural law → the Spanish Scholastics (Vitoria, Suárez) on the rights of all peoples → Locke; the medieval theological root of the natural-rights tradition · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §3.4 EXPLICITLY names this lineage: 'Natural rights theory came from theological foundations — Aquinas, the Spanish Scholastics at Salamanca (Vitoria, Suarez) on the rights of Indigenous peoples, Locke building on this lineage.' The consensus has Aquinas-the-theologian but omits the load-bearing claim that medieval Christian natural-law thought is the SEEDBED of the bank's entire classical-liberal tradition. Frame as recognition: Vitoria arguing at Salamanca that New World peoples had real rights kings could not violate — the medieval root of the idea the Founders inherited. Present the argument; do not adopt or preach it (anti-advocacy rule).

**50. al-Ghazali, 1258, and the closing of the Islamic golden age** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100) and the Mongol sack of Baghdad (1258) ending the House of Wisdom; the religious-orthodoxy-stifling-inquiry pattern · *src: vision-injected*  
> REQUIRED by HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §6: 'The decline thread is part of honest history: al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100) helped close the golden age's rationalist tradition; Mongol destruction of Baghdad 1258 ended the House of Wisdom; subsequent orthodoxy hardening prevented the recovery the West achieved... The pattern (religious orthodoxy stifling inquiry) is stated as historical fact, not editorialized.' The consensus covers Moorish Spain's flowering but omits this required counter-arc. Wonder hook: the Mongols throwing so many books into the Tigris that the river reportedly ran black with ink. State the pattern as fact, no editorializing, no anti-Islam framing.

**51. Roman concrete, the lost dome, and medieval engineering memory** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Why medieval builders couldn't match the Pantheon/Roman dome until Brunelleschi; lost Roman concrete; Hagia Sophia as the surviving exception · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §2 repeatedly cites 'why the Roman concrete still stands' as a flagship 'beauty of mechanism' wonder, and HISTORY_FRAMEWORK names the Pantheon's still-standing concrete dome + Hagia Sophia. The consensus has Gothic cathedrals but omits the haunting medieval reality: the knowledge to build a Pantheon-scale dome was LOST for a thousand years (the exact Brunelleschi-T1 wonder hook in templates §1). Frame as the lost-knowledge cool fact — a millennium of builders unable to recover what Rome had, until 1436. Mechanism over rote.

**52. The Christianization of Scandinavia & the end of pagan blood-sacrifice** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Norse conversion (Olaf; Iceland's peaceful adoption ~1000) ending the human/animal sacrifices at Uppsala; the Peace and Truce of God curbing feudal violence · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §3.7 / HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §5 mandate the bank's signature move: honest pre-modern brutality stated as fact, PAIRED with the named force that ended it. Adam of Bremen's account of the Uppsala temple sacrifices (bodies hung in the sacred grove) is the honest-brutality half; the Norse conversion and Iceland's remarkable peaceful vote to adopt Christianity (~1000) is the named intervention. Likewise the Church's Peace/Truce of God limiting private warfare. State plainly, no 'savages' framing, no triumphalism — present the Norse on their own terms AND record what changed.

**53. English common law, the jury, and 'innocent until proven'** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Henry II's legal reforms; the jury of peers; the writ; trial by ordeal abolished 1215; the deep root of Anglo-American due process · *src: vision-injected*  
> The bank's whole tradition (moral_vision §1, §3.6) runs Locke→Founders on the Anglo-American common-law/due-process line, and §3.4's 'rule of law' is named as a Western achievement. Magna Carta is in the consensus but the broader medieval common-law machinery — the jury of one's peers, the writ system, the 1215 Lateran abolition of trial-by-ordeal pushing England toward the jury trial — is the institutional substance behind it. Wonder hook: trial by ordeal (carrying a red-hot iron, the wound 'judged' after three days) giving way to twelve men weighing evidence. Frame as the medieval birth of due process the Fifth/Sixth Amendments inherited.

Island city, causeways, chinampas, markets · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World Unit 1/4*  
> Non-Western civilization ON ITS OWN TERMS (§4 / pillar 6) — celebrate the engineering: a city on a lake bigger than most in Europe, chinampa floating gardens, three great causeways, an aqueduct from Chapultepec, the Tlatelolco market that stunned the Spanish. Genuine wonder, no exotic-foil framing. The honest brutality (Templo Mayor sacrifice) is a SEPARATE honest topic, not smuggled in here. The engineering specific (chinampas, the causeways) is the answer.

**13. Inca road network & Machu Picchu** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Andean engineering: roads, quipu, terraces · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World Unit 1*  
> Non-Western civilization on its own terms (§4 / pillar 6) — celebrate ordinary ingenuity: ~40,000 km of road run by chasqui relay runners, the quipu knot-record keeping an empire's accounts WITHOUT writing, suspension bridges of woven grass rewoven yearly, terraced agriculture and freeze-dried chuño. Real wonder. The quipu or the relay-runner system is the answer. No condescension, no romanticizing.

**14. The Columbian Exchange** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Transfer of crops, animals, diseases, people · *src: AP World 4.3; Core Knowledge*  
> Marquee concept. moral_vision §4 names this EXPLICITLY as the model for honest-not-cynical: 'The Columbian Exchange caused devastating Indigenous mortality from disease — state it. Don't editorialize.' Present the full two-way reshaping as genuine wonder (potatoes feeding Europe, horses remaking the Plains) AND the catastrophic disease mortality as plain fact in the same breath — the dual commitment held together (§7 meta-principle). Answer = a concrete crop/animal/pathogen specific.

**15. Smallpox and the demographic collapse** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Old World disease devastates the Americas · *src: AP World 4.3; standard world-history canon*  
> Honest-not-cynical (§4). State plainly that disease — not soldiers — killed the overwhelming majority (estimates up to ~90% in some regions), with no acquired immunity in Native populations. This is the 'germs killed more than swords' surprise-reversal, but it is REAL history, not a reveal staged for effect. No editorializing, no blame-framing, no minimizing. The mechanism (no prior exposure / no immunity) or a named epidemic is the answer.

**16. New World crops: potato, maize, tomato** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
American foods that fed the Old World · *src: AP World 4.3; Core Knowledge*  
> Wonder + ordinary-ingenuity celebration (§2). The surprise-reversal kids love: Italy had no tomatoes, Ireland no potatoes, Asia no chili peppers until after 1492 — and American crops then drove population booms across the Old World. Invert the superlative into the stem (SHARED §19): the consequence sets up, the named crop is the answer. Pure wonder, light tier.

**17. The horse returns to the Americas** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Old World animals transform Plains cultures · *src: AP World 4.3; Core Knowledge*  
> Wonder + non-Western-on-its-own-terms (pillar 6). The horse had gone EXTINCT in the Americas millennia earlier and returned with the Spanish, and within a few generations the Comanche and Lakota built mounted cultures so formidable they dominated the southern Plains — a genuine surprise-reversal. Celebrate Native adaptation and skill, no condescension. The 'one animal remade a whole way of life' fact is the answer.

**18. Potosí silver mountain** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Andean silver fuels a global economy · *src: AP World Unit 4; AP Euro*  
> Global-economy wonder AND honest brutality held together. The Cerro Rico's silver minted the world's first global currency and the phrase 'vale un Potosí' (worth a fortune) — celebrate the world-spanning trade. But state plainly the human cost: the mita forced-labor draft and the deadly mercury amalgamation that killed countless Indigenous and African workers (forced-labor economy, pillar 5). Both, in proportion. The silver-circling-the-globe fact or the mita is the answer.

**19. Spanish galleons & the Manila trade** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Silver-for-silk route across the Pacific · *src: AP World Unit 4*  
> Wonder of the FIRST truly global, Pacific-spanning trade loop — Potosí/Mexican silver to Manila, Chinese silk and porcelain back to Acapulco, for 250 years. Economic-history wonder (the world's economies stitched together by one galleon run). No moral-vision tension here; tell it straight as ordinary-ingenuity-at-planetary-scale. The named route or cargo specific is the answer.

**20. The Atlantic (triangular) slave trade** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Forced migration of millions of Africans · *src: AP World 4.7; Core Knowledge; AP US*  
> Honest record of a great evil, stated PLAINLY without theatrical reveal (§4, framework pillar 2/5). ~12.5 million Africans forcibly shipped; the triangular economics named honestly. CRITICAL per framework §5 / moral_vision §7 dual-commitment: pair it with the honest fact that African coastal kingdoms (Asante, Dahomey, Oyo) captured and SOLD most of the enslaved — Africans selling Africans into the trade — WITHOUT using this to excuse the European buyers. Both truths, no minimizing either, no anti-Western OR exculpatory framing. Sets up the next-strand abolition arc (Royal Navy West Africa Squadron, Wilberforce). The named magnitude or economic mechanism is the answer.

**21. The Middle Passage** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The brutal ocean crossing of enslaved people · *src: AP US/World; Core Knowledge*  
> Show the evil (framework §1 forbids sanitizing slavery). State the horror plainly — packed holds, ~15% death rates on the crossing, the Zong massacre of 1781 as a named specific at higher tiers (and its role in galvanizing abolition). Honest, never glib about real suffering (§7 viral test), never theatricalized either. The concrete named detail is the answer, carrying the weight without sensationalism.

**22. Sugar plantations & the encomienda system** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Coerced-labor economies of the New World · *src: AP World 4.4/4.7; Core Knowledge*  
> Honest forced-labor history (pillar 5). Explain the MECHANISM plainly: how sugar's brutal labor demand drove the plantation complex and the encomienda grant of Indigenous labor to colonists. State the cruelty as fact. Pair honestly with the Spanish CROWN debate it provoked (the New Laws of 1542, Las Casas vs. Sepúlveda at Valladolid) — the West producing its own internal moral critique (§3.4 Salamanca natural-rights lineage). The named system or its mechanism is the answer.

**23. Bartolomé de las Casas** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Friar who defended indigenous peoples · *src: AP World/Euro; Core Knowledge*

Courage-in-pursuit-of-truth virtue-by-example (§2): a colonist who renounced his own encomienda and spent his life arguing, in the Valladolid debate against Sepúlveda, that Indigenous peoples were fully rational humans with natural rights — the West's CHRISTIAN-rooted self-critique producing the natural-rights tradition. His actual ARGUMENT (the substance, not the label) is the answer. Promote weight; the consensus under-tiers this.

**24. John Cabot & the Northwest Passage search** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
English/Dutch hunt for a polar Asia route · *src: Core Knowledge CKLA Grade 3*  
> Story-led courage/folly of discovery — the centuries-long doomed quest for an Arctic shortcut to Asia that swallowed expeditions whole. Cabot (1497, English claim to North America under Henry VII) as the opener. Lead with the lure of the shortcut. The named explorer-detail or the reason it was doomed (the ice) is the answer, not a bare date.

**25. Henry Hudson & later North American explorers** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Hudson, Champlain, Coronado, La Salle · *src: Core Knowledge CKLA Grade 3*  
> Story-led exploration drama. Best human hook: Hudson's own mutinous crew set him, his son, and a few loyal men adrift in a small boat in the bay that bears his name (1611), never seen again — a real surprise-reversal. Champlain/Coronado/La Salle map the interior. The vivid named fate or river-claim is the answer. Avoid 'who explored' rote by leading with the scene.

**26. Ming voyages of Zheng He** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Chinese treasure fleets, 1405-33 · *src: AP World Unit 4; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Non-Western civilization on its own terms (§4 / framework pillar 6 names Zheng He explicitly). Celebrate the staggering scale — treasure ships dwarfing European caravels, fleets reaching East Africa decades BEFORE Henry's caravels crept down Guinea (surprise-reversal: China explored first). Then the honest historical turn (framework §5/§6 decline-thread logic): the Ming CHOSE to burn the fleet records and turn inward — a civilization abandoning its own lead. State as fact, not editorialized. The scale-detail or the turning-inward decision is the answer.

**27. The Ottoman Empire & fall of Constantinople** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high  
1453 conquest; Suleiman; Mediterranean power · *src: AP World/Euro Unit 4; Core Knowledge*

Story-led drama: Mehmed II's giant bombard cannon, dragging ships overland into the Golden Horn, the last Byzantine emperor Constantine XI dying in the breach — 'cannons ended the last Roman city,' the end of a 1,000-year empire (ties the Western thread: this fall pushed Greek scholars and texts west, feeding the Renaissance). Honest, treat the Ottomans on their own terms. The named siege specific is the answer.

**28. The Mughal Empire & the Taj Mahal** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Akbar, Shah Jahan, gunpowder empire of India · *src: AP World Unit 4; cultural-literacy canon*
