Celebrate substance: Akbar's Sulh-i-kul (universal religious tolerance) as a genuine achievement worth knowing, and Shah Jahan's Taj Mahal built as a tomb of grief for Mumtaz Mahal — the human story is the wonder. No exotic-foil framing. The named ruler's policy or the Taj's grief-origin is the answer.

**29. Gunpowder empires concept** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Cannon-built land empires of Eurasia · *src: AP World Unit 4*  
> Mechanism-of-history concept (Ottoman, Safavid, Mughal): siege cannon let rulers smash the old stone-castle nobility and centralize huge land empires. Beauty-of-mechanism (§2) applied to military history — WHY the technology reshaped power. The causal mechanism (cannon vs. castle) is the answer, not the label 'gunpowder empire' as a definition lookup (anti-rote §6).

**30. The Renaissance: rebirth of art & learning** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Florence, Medici, humanism, classical revival · *src: AP Euro Unit 1; Core Knowledge*  
> HEAVIEST pillar — Western tradition as real human achievement (§3.4, framework pillar 1). Celebrate fully: Europe rediscovering Athens and Rome, Medici patronage, humanism, the recovery of classical texts (accelerated by scholars fleeing the 1453 fall of Constantinople). This is the inheritance the bank exists to transmit (§5 parenting-through-curation). The named figure/work/idea is the answer. Top weight.

**31. Leonardo da Vinci & Michelangelo** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Mona Lisa, Sistine Chapel, the universal genius · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Western art as inheritance worth celebrating (framework §1 names Sistine ceiling + David explicitly). Drama of the great minds (pillar 6): Leonardo's mirror-writing notebooks and flying-machine sketches; Michelangelo painting the Sistine ceiling on his back for four years, insisting 'I am a sculptor, not a painter.' The vivid making-detail is the answer. Full ladder, high weight — this is celebrated content.

**32. Gutenberg's printing press** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Movable type, c.1450, spread of books · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro/World*  
> Western-tradition + Christian-heritage thread (§3.4 Reformation literacy). The invention that 'spread ideas to everyone' — celebrate the mechanism (movable metal type) and the consequence (the 42-line Bible; literacy; the printed word making the Reformation unstoppable). Beauty-of-mechanism (§2). The named first-product or the mechanism is the answer. High weight — civilization-altering.

**33. Martin Luther & the Protestant Reformation** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
95 Theses, 1517, splitting of the Church · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro Unit 2*  
> Christian heritage as substantive historical FORCE (§3.4; framework 'Christianity TREATED AS SUBSTANTIVE'). Story-led: Luther nailing the 95 Theses to the Wittenberg door Oct 31 1517, then 'Here I stand, I can do no other' at the Diet of Worms — courage at cost (§2). Present the theology on its own terms, no smug-secular and no smug-believer voice (§4). Note the printing-press link (his pamphlets spread in weeks). The named act/quote/consequence is the answer.

**34. The Spanish Armada** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
1588 fleet vs. Elizabeth I's England · *src: AP Euro; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Great-military-history pillar (3) + the English/American-liberty thread (this victory secured a Protestant England and the seedbed of the later American colonies). Story-led drama: fireships scattering the anchored Armada at Calais, the 'Protestant wind' wrecking the fleet round Scotland and Ireland, Elizabeth's Tilbury speech 'I have the heart and stomach of a king.' The named turning-point or quote is the answer.

**35. Copernicus & the Scientific Revolution** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Sun-centered cosmos, Galileo, Kepler · *src: AP Euro Unit 2; Core Knowledge*  
> Western tradition's crown (§3.4: 'modern science was largely the work of believers — Newton, Kepler...'; framework §1). Courage-of-truth (§2): Copernicus publishing De Revolutionibus on his deathbed 1543; Galileo's 'Eppur si muove'; Kepler's elliptical orbits wrung from Tycho's data. CRITICAL framing — do NOT cast it as science-vs-Christianity (§3.4 'does not treat science and Christianity as opponents'); these were believers doing empirical work. The named discovery (heliocentrism, elliptical orbits) is the answer. High weight.

**36. Jamestown & the first English colonies** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
1607 Virginia, John Smith, Pocahontas · *src: Core Knowledge CKHG Unit 7; AP US*  
> The on-ramp to the AMERICAN STORY (pillar 2 — 'the American story deep'). Story-led: the Starving Time winter, John Smith's 'he that will not work shall not eat,' the Powhatan/Pocahontas encounter told honestly (not the Disney version), the 1619 arrival of the first enslaved Africans AND the first representative assembly (House of Burgesses) the SAME year — both founding facts stated plainly. Sets up the deep American arc in the next strand. The named survival-detail or institution is the answer. Honest, no hagiography (framework §1.5).

**37. The Salamanca School & the birth of natural-rights theory** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Vitoria & Suárez at Salamanca argue Indigenous peoples have natural rights, c.1530s-40s · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED and consensus-OMITTED. moral_vision §3.4 names this explicitly: 'the Spanish Scholastics at Salamanca (Vitoria, Suarez) on the rights of Indigenous peoples, Locke building on this lineage.' The West producing, from its CHRISTIAN intellectual heritage, the natural-rights argument against its OWN colonial conquest — Francisco de Vitoria's lectures arguing the conquest needed moral justification and Indigenous peoples were true owners of their lands. This is the lineage that runs to Locke and the American Founding. The substantive ARGUMENT is the answer, not the label. Pairs with Las Casas.

**38. The internal African slave trade that fed the Atlantic trade** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Asante, Dahomey, Oyo coastal kingdoms captured and sold the enslaved; centuries-old internal trade · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED, consensus UNDER-COVERS. Framework pillar 5 names this explicitly: 'Africans selling Africans to coastal kingdoms (Asante, Dahomey) for centuries before Atlantic trade.' The honest fact, held WITHOUT exculpating European buyers and WITHOUT anti-African OR anti-Western framing (§3.7 same standard all directions, §6 no inherent-condemnation of any group): the Atlantic trade ran on African coastal kingdoms supplying captives from interior wars, and a domestic African slave system long predated 1492. State plainly as fact, no minimizing the European demand that fueled it. The named kingdom or the supply-mechanism is the answer.

**39. Mansa Musa's 1324 Hajj and the Mali Empire** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Mali emperor's pilgrimage to Mecca with so much gold he crashed Egypt's gold price; Timbuktu/Sankore · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-NAMED, consensus OMITTED. Framework pillar 6 names Mansa Musa AND the Sankore university by name. Non-Western civilization on its own terms (§4, no exotic-foil): the richest man perhaps in history, whose 1324 pilgrimage gave away so much gold in Cairo that he depressed its value for years, and whose Timbuktu became a center of manuscripts and learning. Pure wonder + ordinary-ingenuity-at-scale. The gold-crash fact or Sankore is the answer.

**40. The Valladolid debate (Las Casas vs. Sepúlveda, 1550-51)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Formal Spanish crown debate on whether conquest and Indigenous subjugation were just · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED enrichment. The first great formal European debate over the rights of conquered peoples: Las Casas arguing Indigenous peoples were fully rational with natural rights, against Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda arguing Aristotle's 'natural slavery' justified the conquest. STEELMAN both (§6, §4 no strawmen) — Sepúlveda's was a serious argument of its day, not a cartoon villain. The point is that the West argued this out IN PUBLIC, under its own crown, on natural-law grounds — virtue and self-critique by example (§2). The substantive position at issue is the answer.


## Scientific Revolution & the Enlightenment (31)

**1. Copernicus and Heliocentrism** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Sun-centered model replaces Earth-centered cosmos · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro Unit 3*  
> Pillar 1 keystone: the move that opened the Scientific Revolution, the West's own inheritance. Present Copernicus on his own terms — a cautious canon of the Church who dedicated De Revolutionibus to the Pope and published as he was dying (1543). NO smug science-vs-religion framing (moral_vision §3.4): the man was a churchman. Lead with the wonder — he dared to put the Earth in motion when common sense screamed it stood still. The cool fact (deathbed publication, the Commentariolus circulated quietly for decades) is the answer, not a date-recall.

**2. Galileo and the Telescope** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Telescope discoveries; trial by Inquisition · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro; cultural-literacy canon*  
> moral_vision §2 names Galileo explicitly under 'courage in pursuit of truth.' Lead with the discoveries as WONDER — Jupiter's four moons, the phases of Venus, mountains on the Moon, the Milky Way resolved into countless stars. 'Eppur si muove' is the memorable answer (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK T1). Handle the Church conflict honestly but WITHOUT the cartoon: Galileo was a believer who thought he was defending a better reading of Scripture; the clash was as much about personality, papal politics, and his mockery of the Pope (Simplicio) as about heliocentrism per se. No 'religion vs reason' strawman.

**3. Isaac Newton and Universal Gravitation** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Laws of motion, gravity, the Principia · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro; E.D. Hirsch canon*  
> Pillar 1 capstone genius AND moral_vision §3.4 explicitly lists Newton among the believing scientists ('Newton, Boyle, Faraday, Maxwell, Mendel — all believers'). Celebrate the unification of heaven and earth — the same force pulls the apple and holds the Moon. Drama-of-minds (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK T1): the apple in plague-year 1666 at Woolsthorpe, the obsessive solitary, the Hooke and Leibniz priority feuds (T3). His deep theological and alchemical writing is part of the honest portrait, not an embarrassment to hide. The Principia (1687) as the keystone book.

**4. Kepler's Laws of Planetary Motion** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Elliptical orbits replace perfect circles · *src: AP Euro Unit 3; standard physics-history canon*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism (moral_vision §2): the wonder that planets trace ELLIPSES, not the perfect circles two thousand years of philosophy demanded — Kepler broke the circle reluctantly, forced by Tycho's data. A devout Lutheran who saw the cosmos as God's geometry and titled his work Harmonices Mundi. The cool fact (he agonized over abandoning circles; the orbits sweep equal areas in equal times) is the answer. Believer-scientist thread again (§3.4).

**5. Tycho Brahe's Observations** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Precise naked-eye astronomy; metal nose, observatory · *src: AP Euro Unit 3; history-of-astronomy canon*  
> Pure drama-of-minds (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §7, T1/T3 'Tycho's silver nose'). Lead with the vivid eccentric: the metal nose after a duel, his island observatory Uraniborg paid for by the Danish king, his pet moose that died drunk falling down stairs, the most precise naked-eye data ever taken — the foundation Kepler needed. The memorable physical detail IS the answer. Ordinary-and-extraordinary ingenuity: best data before the telescope existed.

**6. The Scientific Method (Bacon and Descartes)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Empirical induction and rational deduction · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro Unit 3*  
> How modern knowledge is TESTED — the method that made the whole revolution possible (Pillar 1). Concrete handles over jargon (moral_vision §5): Bacon's induction (gather evidence, then generalize) vs Descartes's doubt-everything-then-rebuild ('I think therefore I am' as the one thing he could not doubt). Watch the anti-rote gate — never 'what is empiricism'; frame as the move each man made and why it mattered. Both were believers reasoning toward truth, not against faith.

**7. Vesalius and Human Anatomy** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Dissection-based anatomy textbook overturns Galen · *src: AP Euro Unit 3; history-of-medicine canon*  
> Courage-in-pursuit-of-truth + no-condescension-to-the-past (moral_vision §4: 'Aristotle was not stupid'). Vesalius did not sneer at Galen — he revered him, then discovered by his own hands that Galen had dissected apes and pigs, not humans, and got 200 things wrong. De Humani Corporis Fabrica (1543, same year as Copernicus). The wonder: a young man trusting his own eyes over 1,300 years of authority. The vivid fact (he stole corpses from the gallows to dissect them) is the answer.

**8. William Harvey and Blood Circulation** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Discovery that the heart pumps blood in circuit · *src: AP Euro Unit 3; history-of-medicine canon*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism (moral_vision §2): the body works by knowable physical law — the heart is a PUMP, the blood travels in a closed circuit, overturning Galen's belief that the liver constantly made new blood consumed by the organs. Harvey proved it by measurement (the heart moves more blood in an hour than the whole body holds, so it must recirculate). The quantitative argument IS the answer — discovery by counting.

**9. The Microscope and Microorganisms** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Leeuwenhoek and Hooke reveal a hidden world · *src: Core Knowledge science; history-of-science canon*  
> Wonder, lead-with-the-strange-thing (moral_vision §2). A Dutch draper grinding his own tiny lenses sees 'animalcules' swarming in a drop of pond water and in the scrapings of his own teeth — a whole living world no one knew existed. Hooke's Micrographia (1665) coined 'cell' from monastery cells and drew a flea the size of a cat. The vivid first-sight detail is the answer. Ordinary ingenuity: Leeuwenhoek was an amateur with no university training.

**10. John Locke and Natural Rights** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Life, liberty, property; blank-slate mind · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro/US; civics canon*  
> CRITICAL vision anchor. moral_vision §1 names Locke as the head of the bank's entire intellectual lineage ('Locke -> the American Founders -> Tocqueville...'). §3.9 GOVERNS the framing: Locke's rights are NEGATIVE rights — life, liberty, property require only that others LEAVE YOU ALONE; they are freedoms, never entitlements to anyone's labor. A question must NEVER teach a child that Locke grounded a right to food/housing/care. The drama: Locke building on the Salamanca Scholastics and Aquinas (§3.4), writing to justify resisting tyranny, his Two Treatises feeding straight into Jefferson's Declaration. Attribute the framing to Locke ('Locke argued...'), do not adopt it as neutral fact (moral_vision §6 advocacy rule).

**11. Thomas Hobbes and the Leviathan** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Social contract; life 'nasty, brutish, short' · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro Unit 3*  
> Steel-man the rival of the bank's own tradition (moral_vision §6, §1 meta-principle 6). Hobbes is the powerful COUNTERWEIGHT to Locke: from the same starting point (a state of nature) he reasons to absolute sovereignty rather than limited government, because without it life is 'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short.' Present his argument at full strength — the trade of liberty for security is a real and serious position, written amid the terror of the English Civil War. Attribute to Hobbes, don't editorialize. The vivid quote is the memorable answer.

**12. Voltaire and Freedom of Speech** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Wit, tolerance, attacks on church abuses · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro Unit 4*  
> Free expression is a core bank value (moral_vision celebrates free speech in the classical-liberal tradition, §1). Celebrate Voltaire's defense of tolerance and his courage taking up real cases of injustice (the Calas affair — he spent years clearing a Protestant wrongly broken on the wheel). Note honestly: the famous line 'I disapprove of what you say, but will defend to the death your right to say it' was written by his biographer Evelyn Beatrice Hall, not Voltaire himself — no fabricated quotes (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbid #7). Avoid flattening him into anti-religion sloganeering; he was a deist who attacked abuses, not belief as such.

**13. Montesquieu and Separation of Powers** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Three branches of government check each other · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP US/Euro; civics canon*  
> HIGH vision priority — the direct line into the American Founding (moral_vision §3.6, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 2). Montesquieu's insight in The Spirit of the Laws (1748): liberty survives only when power checks power — legislative, executive, judicial separated so none can dominate. This is the literal blueprint the Framers wired into the Constitution. Celebrate it as the engineering of freedom. Connect explicitly to Madison and the 1787 Convention (the injected Founding topic).

**14. Rousseau and the Social Contract** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
General will; 'born free, everywhere in chains' · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP Euro Unit 4*  
> Steel-man honestly, then let the honest record speak (moral_vision §6, §1 meta-principle 6). Present Rousseau's actual argument at strength — 'Man is born free, and everywhere he is in chains'; legitimacy flows from the people's general will. AND tell the truth that his 'general will' and 'forced to be free' became, in the hands of Robespierre and the Jacobins, intellectual fuel for the Terror — the consequence is part of the honest record, stated plainly, not theatricalized. Sets up the Enlightenment's two divergent children: the American founding (Locke/Montesquieu) vs the French Terror (Rousseau radicalized). Do not strawman; do not whitewash.

**15. Diderot's Encyclopedia** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Vast reference compiling all human knowledge · *src: AP Euro Unit 4; Enlightenment canon*  
> Ordinary-ingenuity + spread-of-ideas (moral_vision §2; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 1, printing/ideas). The audacity: 28 volumes, 20+ years, 150 contributors, smuggling knowledge of crafts and trades and dangerous ideas past the censors who twice tried to suppress it. The wonder is the SCALE and the defiance — Diderot kept going after his publisher secretly censored the plates. The vivid fact (it was banned, its privilege revoked, copies seized — and it survived) is the answer.

**16. Adam Smith and The Wealth of Nations** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Free markets; the 'invisible hand' · *src: AP Euro/US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> HEAVY vision priority. moral_vision §3.2-§3.3 make sound economics and the classical-liberal market tradition a load-bearing commitment, and Smith is its 1776 fountainhead (same year as the Declaration). Celebrate the central insight as WONDER: the butcher, brewer, and baker feed the city not from benevolence but from self-interest, and an 'invisible hand' coordinates them better than any planner could — the seed of Hayek's knowledge problem (§3.2). Pair honestly with his earlier Theory of Moral Sentiments (Smith was a moral philosopher first; markets rest on sympathy and justice) — this is the bank's 'free markets AND virtue' fusion (§1 meta-principle 7). Attribute to Smith, don't preach.

**17. Benjamin Franklin: Science and the Enlightenment** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Kite-and-key electricity; American polymath · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; American canon*  
> The American face of the Enlightenment — bridges Pillar 1 and Pillar 2 (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK). Lead with wonder + ordinary ingenuity (moral_vision §2): drawing lightning from a storm cloud with a kite to prove it was electricity, then inventing the lightning rod, bifocals, the Franklin stove, the glass armonica — and refusing to patent any of them so all could benefit. The self-made printer turned scientist turned Founder. The vivid invention/experiment is the answer. Honest portrait: a slaveholder who became, late in life, president of an abolition society — flawed humans who built something extraordinary (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbid #5).

**18. Mary Wollstonecraft and Women's Rights** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Vindication of the Rights of Woman · *src: AP Euro Unit 4; modern curriculum canon*  
> A genuine Enlightenment argument FROM WITHIN the natural-rights tradition (moral_vision §1) — present on its own terms, NOT as modern identity-politics retrojected. Wollstonecraft's 1792 case was that reason is not sexed: if rights rest on rational nature (the Lockean premise), then denying women education is incoherent on the tradition's OWN logic. That is the substance — an extension of natural-rights reasoning, argued against Rousseau's view of women. Avoid both hagiography and present-day grievance framing (moral_vision §6, anti-advocacy). The vivid fact (she wrote it in six weeks, answering Burke and Rousseau directly) is the answer.

**19. Enlightened Despots** — standard (~3q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Frederick the Great, Catherine, Joseph II reforms · *src: AP Euro Unit 4*  
> Honest, on-its-own-terms (moral_vision §4). The serious tension: monarchs applying reason FROM ABOVE — Frederick the Great's religious toleration and legal reform while playing the flute and corresponding with Voltaire; Catherine the Great's Nakaz; Joseph II's frantic top-down decrees. Present the genuine reforms AND the limit honestly: reform granted by a ruler can be withdrawn by a ruler — it is not the same as rights secured by a people (connects to §3.9 and the American contrast). The memorable specific (Frederick at Sanssouci, Voltaire as his houseguest until they fell out) is the answer.

**20. Linnaeus and Biological Classification** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Two-name system to classify living things · *src: Core Knowledge science; history-of-science canon*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism / ordering the wonder of life (moral_vision §2). Lead with the achievement: one devout Swede gave every living thing a two-word Latin name (genus + species) and a nested system — the reason a wolf is Canis lupus everywhere on Earth. He saw himself as cataloguing God's creation. Watch the anti-rote gate — frame as the PROBLEM he solved (naming chaos before him: a plant might have a twelve-word descriptive name) and why his fix endured, not 'what is binomial nomenclature.' The vivid fact (he even classified himself, Homo sapiens) is the answer.

**21. Lavoisier and Modern Chemistry** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Conservation of mass; naming oxygen · *src: AP Euro; history-of-chemistry canon*  
> Discovery + the most haunting drama-of-minds moment in the strand (moral_vision §2; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK T2/T4 names 'Lavoisier guillotined'). The science: he weighed everything and proved matter is neither created nor destroyed in reaction — turning alchemy into measured chemistry, naming oxygen and hydrogen, killing the phlogiston theory. The tragedy: the Revolution he'd served guillotined him in 1794 as a former tax-farmer — 'the Republic has no need of savants' — and Lagrange said 'it took only an instant to cut off that head, and a hundred years may not produce another like it.' The verified quote is the answer. Real specifics only.

**22. Edward Jenner and the Smallpox Vaccine** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Cowpox inoculation defeats smallpox · *src: Core Knowledge; history-of-medicine canon*  
> Celebrate the REAL human progress straight (moral_vision §2 'courage in pursuit of truth'; the immune mechanism is genuine, honored science). This is a 1796 historical DISCOVERY — Jenner noticing milkmaids who'd had cowpox never caught smallpox, then testing it on James Phipps — and it is the heroic story it appears to be: the disease that killed ~300 million in the 20th century alone was eradicated worldwide by 1980. Tell it as the lifesaving triumph it was. Do NOT relitigate modern vaccine-institution capture here — that contemporary debate lives in the science bank, not in an 18th-century history cell. The vivid origin detail (the word 'vaccine' comes from vacca, Latin for cow) is the answer.

**23. The Salon and the Republic of Letters** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Parisian gatherings where ideas were debated · *src: AP Euro Unit 4; Enlightenment social-history canon*  
> Present on its own terms (moral_vision §4) — the SOCIAL machinery of the Enlightenment. Educated women (Madame Geoffrin, Madame du Deffand, Julie de Lespinasse) ran the salons where philosophes, mathematicians, and visitors argued ideas across rank — a genuine and underappreciated role for women in the intellectual life of the age. The 'Republic of Letters' was a Europe-wide correspondence network linking thinkers who never met. The vivid concrete (whose salon, what was argued there) is the answer, not an abstract 'what was the function of the salon.'

**24. The Printing Press and Spread of Ideas** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Cheap books and pamphlets carry new thought · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro context*  
> Pillar 1 ENGINE (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK: printing threaded through the Western inheritance; ties to the Reformation's 'literacy, conscience, vernacular Scripture' in moral_vision §2). Gutenberg's movable type (~1440) is the precondition for everything in this strand — Copernicus, the Reformation, the Encyclopedie all travel on cheap print. Lead with the staggering scale (a scribe copied one book in months; a press printed hundreds in the same time). The vivid fact (the Gutenberg Bible, ~180 copies, the 42-line page) is the answer. Note the chronology honestly: the press predates this strand and made it possible.

**25. Galileo vs. the Inquisition (Trial of 1633)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Church forces recantation of heliocentrism · *src: AP Euro Unit 3; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Handle with the bank's MOST careful anti-strawman discipline (moral_vision §3.4 'does not treat science and Christianity as opponents'; §4 no-condescension; §6 no-strawman). State the facts plainly — Galileo was tried, made to recant on his knees, and spent his last years under house arrest — WITHOUT the Enlightenment-myth cartoon of reason crushed by faith. The honest record: the evidence for heliocentrism was genuinely incomplete (no observed stellar parallax yet), the conflict was tangled with Galileo's mockery of his former ally the Pope and with Counter-Reformation politics, and many churchmen were themselves astronomers. The defiant 'Eppur si muove' (probably apocryphal — flag honestly, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbid #7) is the memorable hook. The real, decidable specifics are the answer.

**26. Reason, Deism, and Religion in the Enlightenment** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
God as 'clockmaker'; reason over revelation · *src: AP Euro Unit 4; intellectual-history canon*  
> Take religious thought seriously on its own terms — NO smug atheist voice, NO smug believer voice (moral_vision §4, §9 'not anti-religious'). Present deism as the genuine intellectual position it was: a law-bound, orderly universe implies a rational Creator — the 'clockmaker' who set it running. This is NOT 'the Enlightenment defeated religion'; it is believing scientists (Newton, Boyle) and deist Founders (Jefferson, Franklin) reconciling faith with natural law (moral_vision §3.4). Steel-man both the deist and the orthodox-Christian positions of the era. Attribute claims to thinkers, never adjudicate the metaphysics (moral_vision no-verdict). The concrete image (Paley's watch on the heath, framed historically) is the handle.

**27. Enlightenment Ideas in the American Founding** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Locke and Montesquieu in Declaration and Constitution · *src: Core Knowledge Gr6; AP US; civics canon*  
> HIGHEST vision priority in this strand — the climax of the whole lineage (moral_vision §1, §3.6; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 2 HEAVY). This is where the philosophy became a real government 'founded on philosophical principles rather than blood, ethnicity, or established religion' (§3.6). Celebrate it: Locke's natural rights -> the Declaration's 'Life, Liberty, and the pursuit of Happiness' (NOTE §3.9 — Jefferson's substitution of 'pursuit of Happiness' for Locke's 'property' is itself a freedoms-not-entitlements point; these are negative rights); Montesquieu's separation -> three branches. Honest about the contradiction: the men who wrote 'all men are created equal' held slaves (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbid #5, #3 — do NOT adopt the 1619 reframe that the founding was 'really about' slavery). The American experiment as the most successful Enlightenment experiment, stress-tested over 250 years.

**28. The Believing Scientists of the Revolution** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Newton, Boyle, Kepler, Pascal as devout men of faith doing the new science · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §3.4 is explicit: 'Modern science was largely the work of believers: Newton, Boyle, Faraday, Maxwell, Mendel, Lemaitre.' The consensus list treats each scientist in isolation; the vision REQUIRES the bank to make the pattern legible — the Scientific Revolution was NOT a revolt against God but was largely carried out BY devout Christians who saw lawful nature as evidence of a lawgiver. Robert Boyle (a founder of modern chemistry, Boyle's Law) endowed lectures to defend Christianity and funded Bible translation; Pascal had a mystical night of fire he sewed into his coat (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK T2); Newton wrote more on theology than physics. Present as honest historical fact (NOT a tract proving religion true — moral_vision §9 'not Christian nationalist'), the direct corrective to the science-vs-faith myth.

**29. Robert Boyle and the Birth of Chemistry** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Boyle's Law, the Sceptical Chymist, experiment over alchemy · *src: vision-injected*  
> A flat consensus gap: Boyle is a first-rank figure of the Scientific Revolution (a founder of modern chemistry and of the Royal Society) yet appears nowhere in the consensus list, while Lavoisier and the microscope do. Celebrate the science as WONDER + beauty-of-mechanism (moral_vision §2): his air-pump experiments, Boyle's Law (pressure times volume is constant), and The Sceptical Chymist (1661) dragging chemistry out of secretive alchemy into public, repeatable experiment. moral_vision §3.4 names him among the believers — he spent his fortune defending Christianity and funding Scripture translation, the perfect embodiment of the injected believing-scientists thread. The vivid specific (the air pump, the dying-mouse-in-a-vacuum demonstrations) is the answer.

**30. Edmund Burke and the Conservative Critique of Revolution** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Reflections on the Revolution in France; prudence, tradition, inherited liberty · *src: vision-injected*  
> HISTORY_FRAMEWORK's own Pillar-1 text names the Enlightenment trio as 'Locke, Smith, Burke' — but the consensus list has Locke and Smith and DROPS Burke. The vision requires him: he is the tradition's own voice (moral_vision §1 is 'classical-liberal TRADITIONALISM') and the essential counterweight distinguishing the two Enlightenment children. Burke in 1790 predicted the French Revolution would end in terror and a military strongman — and was proved right by the guillotine and Napoleon. Present his actual argument at strength (moral_vision §6 steel-man): liberty is best secured through inherited institutions and prudent reform, not abstract reason wiping the slate clean (the answer to Rousseau and the radicalized Encyclopedists). The verified prophetic specific (he forecast the Terror and the dictator before either happened) is the answer.

**31. The French Revolution's Terror as the Enlightenment's Dark Child** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Robespierre, the Committee of Public Safety, reason turned to the guillotine · *src: vision-injected*  
> The consensus list gives Rousseau and the philosophes but stops at the IDEAS, omitting where one branch of them LED — exactly the honest consequence the vision refuses to skip (moral_vision §1 meta-principle 2 'anti-totalitarianism is foundational, in EVERY form'; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbid #4 'sanitizing... fail. Show the evil.'). Tell it straight, not theatricalized: the Revolution that proclaimed the Rights of Man and enthroned 'reason' as a goddess also built the guillotine, executed ~17,000 in the Terror, de-Christianized by force, and slid into Napoleon's dictatorship — vindicating Burke. This is the indispensable CONTRAST that makes the American founding legible: same Enlightenment seedbed, opposite fruit (Locke/Montesquieu/limited government vs Rousseau-radicalized/unlimited 'general will'). The vivid, decidable specific (the Cult of Reason in Notre-Dame; Robespierre himself guillotined in the Thermidorian reaction) is the answer. Honest, not preachy (moral_vision §4).


## The American Founding & early Republic (45)

**1. Stamp Act & 'No Taxation Without Representation'** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Parliament taxes colonies; colonial protest erupts · *src: Core Knowledge Grade 4/5; AP US History Period 3*  
> Present as the constitutional principle fight it was — colonists claimed the rights of Englishmen (consent to taxation, trial by jury) being violated, not a tax-rate grievance. The story-led hook is Patrick Henry's 'If this be treason, make the most of it' in the Virginia House of Burgesses. Cool fact is the answer; the principle of self-government via consent ties straight to the bank's Locke-to-Founders lineage.

**2. Boston Massacre (1770)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium ⭐  
British soldiers fire on Boston crowd · *src: Core Knowledge; Hirsch cultural literacy*  
> Tell it straight and honest both directions per moral_vision §4 (honest not cynical) and the framework's no-hagiography rule: it was a chaotic riot AND John Adams defended the soldiers in court and won acquittals because the rule of law mattered more to him than the mob. That John-Adams-defends-the-redcoats fact is the wonder reversal and a virtue-by-example beat. Revere's engraving was effective propaganda — name it as propaganda plainly. Crispus Attucks named.

**3. Boston Tea Party (1773)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Colonists dump tea in Boston Harbor · *src: Core Knowledge Grade 4; AP US History*  
> Concrete-image wonder hook (Sons of Liberty in Mohawk disguise, 342 chests, ~£10,000 of tea into the harbor). Note the counterintuitive economics — the Tea Act actually LOWERED the price of tea; the protest was about the principle of the tax, not the cost. That reversal is the cool-fact answer and reinforces the consent-not-price theme. Tell the cause (principle) accurately, not as a tax-was-too-high story.

**4. Paul Revere's Ride & Lexington and Concord** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Midnight warning; 'shot heard round the world' · *src: Core Knowledge; Longfellow poem; cultural canon*  
> Iconic-moment wonder at full strength — 'shot heard round the world' (Emerson's phrase), the minutemen, 'the redcoats are coming' (more accurately 'the regulars are out'). At higher tiers honor the real history vs the Longfellow legend (Revere was captured; Samuel Prescott actually completed the ride to Concord) without staging a 'secretly false' reveal — state both as honest record per the no-TIL rule. NAMED THINGS and VIVID ACTIONS are the answers.

**5. Common Sense & Thomas Paine** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Pamphlet arguing for independence from monarchy · *src: AP US History Period 3; Hirsch*  
> Western-tradition pillar: ideas move history. Lead with the staggering reach — a ~50-page pamphlet selling perhaps 500,000 copies in a colonial population of 2.5 million, read aloud in taverns, turning fence-sitters toward independence. Engage Paine's actual argument (hereditary monarchy as absurd; a continent should not be governed by an island) as the serious case it was. The scale-of-influence number is the hook; the argument is the substance.

**6. Declaration of Independence (1776)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Jefferson drafts; July 4; unalienable rights · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US History; founding canon*  
> FLAGSHIP of the strand and a load-bearing carrier of the bank's natural-rights lineage (moral_vision §1, §3.6, §3.9). 'Unalienable rights' are NEGATIVE/natural rights — life, liberty, the pursuit of happiness are FREEDOMS, not entitlements to anyone's labor (§3.9 is explicit and names the bad case). Higher tiers: Jefferson borrowing from Locke; the deleted anti-slavery clause; the gap between 'all men are created equal' and the practice — which is exactly the standard Douglass/Lincoln/King later held America to (the bank's whole arc). Do NOT adopt the 1619 reframe as neutral (framework §3 forbidden); the founding principle was natural rights and self-government. The kid should own 'unalienable rights = life, liberty, pursuit of happiness' AND understand they are freedoms.

**7. Bunker Hill & Siege of Boston** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
'Don't fire till you see whites of eyes' · *src: Core Knowledge; military-history canon*  
> Military-history pillar; iconic-quote wonder hook ('Don't fire until you see the whites of their eyes' — Prescott/Putnam). The cool-fact reversal: it was a British victory that cost them so dearly (~1,000 casualties) it proved the colonials would stand and fight. Actually fought on Breed's Hill — state plainly as honest record, not as a gotcha. Outcome-and-cost is the answer.

**8. Washington Crossing the Delaware & Trenton** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Christmas-night surprise attack on Hessians · *src: Core Knowledge; famous painting; cultural canon*  
> Pillar-2 centerpiece, explicitly named in the framework (Trenton + 'Victory or Death'). Story-led at maximum: army nearly dissolving, enlistments expiring at year's end, the freezing night crossing through ice floes, the password 'Victory or Death,' the dawn surprise on the Hessians — 'the complete dawn victory saved the Revolution.' Washington's character under pressure is virtue-by-example. The painting (Leutze) is iconic but historically stylized — fine to note. NAMED detail ('Victory or Death') is the answer.

**9. Saratoga (1777) — the turning point** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
American victory brings France into war · *src: AP US History Period 3; military canon*  
> The strategic hinge — frame as cause-and-consequence: the American victory convinced France to commit openly, transforming a colonial rebellion into a global war Britain could lose. Western-tradition note: the alliance was secured by Franklin's diplomacy in Paris (ties to his topic). The 'what did Saratoga DO' (brought France in) is the answer, not just who won where.

**10. Valley Forge winter (1777-78)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Brutal winter; von Steuben drills army · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural canon*  
> Virtue-by-example through suffering and discipline (moral_vision §4, show-don't-preach). Concrete: ~2,000 dead from disease and exposure, no theatrical sentiment — state the cost plainly. The transformation is the wonder: the Prussian von Steuben turning a ragged army into a drilled fighting force, writing the drill manual by candlelight. The drillmaster-forges-an-army fact is the answer.

**11. Foreign allies: Lafayette, von Steuben, France** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
European officers and French alliance aid colonists · *src: AP US History; biographical canon*  
> Western-tradition / transatlantic-Enlightenment angle: the American cause drew idealists across the ocean. Lafayette the 19-year-old marquis who served without pay and named his son George Washington; von Steuben's drill; the French fleet and money (Rochambeau, de Grasse) that made Yorktown possible. Specific human details are the answers — the boy-marquis, the drill manual — not abstract 'France helped.'

**12. Benedict Arnold's treason** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Hero general defects to the British · *src: Hirsch cultural literacy; biographical canon*  
> Drama-of-character beat. Tell it honestly with NO 'secretly bad' staging (no-TIL rule) — Arnold was a genuine war hero (Saratoga) whose grievance, debt, and pride curdled into the West Point betrayal; Major André caught with the plans in his boot and hanged. The name became the American synonym for traitor. The specific betrayal (selling West Point) is the answer. A study in how virtue can be lost — by example, not sermon.

**13. Yorktown (1781) — Cornwallis surrenders** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Franco-American siege ends major fighting · *src: AP US History; military canon*  
> Decisive-moment wonder: the Franco-American trap (Washington's army + Rochambeau + de Grasse's fleet cutting off escape by sea), and the legend that British bands played 'The World Turned Upside Down' as they surrendered. The NAMED detail (the tune, or the sea-blockade that sprang the trap) is the answer. Honors the French alliance's payoff.

**14. Treaty of Paris (1783)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=low  
Britain recognizes American independence · *src: AP US History Period 3*  
> Consequence-framing: independence formally secured, with borders pushed to the Mississippi — a continent's worth of future. Negotiated by Franklin, Adams, and Jay. The 'what changed' (recognition + the vast western boundary) is the answer, not the date. Sets up the territorial questions to come.

**15. George Washington — general and first president** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Commander, Cincinnatus, two-term precedent · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US History; founding canon*  
> THE indispensable man and a flagship of virtue-by-example (moral_vision §4; framework Pillar 2 + §2). The Cincinnatus thread is the heart: he twice gave up power when he could have kept it — resigning his commission in 1783 (King George III reportedly said it made him 'the greatest man in the world') and stepping down after two terms. Refused anything like a crown. BUT no hagiography (framework §5 forbidden): he enslaved people at Mount Vernon and freed them only in his will — state plainly as part of the same honest record. The relinquish-power fact is the answer; the contradiction is taught straight.

**16. Benjamin Franklin — inventor, diplomat, founder** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Kite, bifocals, Poor Richard, French diplomacy · *src: Core Knowledge; Hirsch; founding canon*  
> Polymath and ordinary-ingenuity exemplar (moral_vision §2). Wonder hooks across tiers: the kite-and-key electricity experiment, the lightning rod, bifocals, the Franklin stove (all UN-patented on principle — he wanted them freely used), Poor Richard's aphorisms, and the Paris diplomacy that secured the French alliance. The specific invention/feat is the answer. Late note: he signed an anti-slavery petition near the end of his life — part of the honest founding record.

**17. Articles of Confederation & their failures** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Weak first government; no taxing power · *src: AP US History Period 3; Core Knowledge*  
> Frame as the deliberate experiment it was — a republic so afraid of central tyranny it created a government too weak to tax, regulate trade, or pay its soldiers, and watched it nearly fail. The specific structural defect (no power to tax / no executive) is the answer. Sets up WHY the Constitution was needed; honest that the first try faltered.

**18. Shays' Rebellion (1786-87)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Farmers revolt over debts and taxes · *src: AP US History Period 3*  
> Cause-and-consequence: indebted Massachusetts farmers (led by Revolutionary veteran Daniel Shays) shutting down courts, and the weak Confederation unable to respond — the scare that convinced the elites to call the Constitutional Convention. The 'what it triggered' is the answer. Tell the farmers' grievance honestly, not as mere disorder.

**19. Constitutional Convention & Great Compromise** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
1787 Philadelphia; framing the Constitution · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US History; founding canon*  
> Pillar-2 + Western-institutions centerpiece. Story-led: the closed windows and secrecy oath in the Philadelphia summer heat, 81-year-old Franklin carried in a sedan chair, the deadlock between large and small states resolved by the Great (Connecticut) Compromise — a bicameral legislature (House by population, Senate equal). The specific compromise mechanism is the answer. This is deliberative self-government in action — the inheritance the bank celebrates.

**20. The Constitution: separation of powers & checks** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Three branches; federalism; checks and balances · *src: Core Knowledge Civics; AP US History*  
> Core civic inheritance — the architecture that diffuses power so no one branch can dominate, drawing on Montesquieu and the hard lesson of monarchy. Teach a CONCRETE mechanism as the answer (the veto, the override, judicial review, the impeachment power) rather than the abstract phrase. The oldest still-standing written national constitution (moral_vision §3.6) — that durability is worth naming. This is the Western rule-of-law tradition made into a working machine.

**21. Three-Fifths Compromise & slavery in framing** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Counting enslaved people for representation · *src: AP US History Period 3*  
> Honest founding record, no sanitizing AND no anti-American distortion (framework §3, §4, §8). State plainly: slavery was the unresolved moral wound built into the founding bargain. Teach the counterintuitive truth carefully — the three-fifths clause was pushed by SLAVE states wanting MORE representation and resisted by antislavery delegates; it was a grim compromise to get a union, not an endorsement that the enslaved were 'three-fifths human.' This is the contradiction Lincoln and Douglass later forced America to confront against its own stated principles. Substance over slogan; the actual mechanics are the answer.

**22. Federalist Papers (Hamilton, Madison, Jay)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Essays arguing for ratifying the Constitution · *src: AP US History; Hirsch*  
> Western-tradition / great-ideas pillar: 85 essays under the pen name 'Publius,' written at speed to win New York's ratification, now the canonical explanation of the Constitution's design (Madison's Federalist 10 on factions; 51 on 'ambition must be made to counteract ambition'). Engage the actual argument — how a large republic guards against faction — as serious political philosophy. A specific idea (faction control, the auxiliary precautions) is the answer.

**23. Bill of Rights (first ten amendments)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Free speech, religion, arms, due process · *src: Core Knowledge Civics; AP US History; founding canon*  
> Core protected liberties — and these are NEGATIVE rights in the bank's framing (moral_vision §3.9): they tell the government what it may NOT do (Congress shall make no law...). Have the kid own which freedom lives in which amendment (1st speech/religion/press, 4th searches, 5th due process) as the answer. The Anti-Federalist demand that produced them is honest founding history. Religious-liberty clause ties to §3.4 and the bank's not-Christian-nationalist stance.

**24. Thomas Jefferson — author and third president** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Declaration author, Monticello, Louisiana Purchase · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US History; founding canon*  
> Author of the founding creed AND the founding's sharpest contradiction — taught together, no hagiography (framework §5). Penned 'all men are created equal' while enslaving over 600 people across his life including the Hemings family; doubled the nation with the Louisiana Purchase though he doubted its constitutionality. Also: architect of religious liberty (Virginia Statute), founder of the University of Virginia, the polymath of Monticello. The specific achievement OR the specific contradiction is the answer; both belong to the honest record.

**25. James Madison — 'Father of the Constitution'** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Architect of Constitution and Bill of Rights · *src: AP US History; founding canon*  
> The quiet scholar who out-prepared everyone — arrived at Philadelphia with a trunk of books and a drafted plan (the Virginia Plan), took the convention's most complete notes, then wrote much of the Bill of Rights. Western-tradition: ideas and preparation shaping institutions. A specific contribution (the Virginia Plan, the convention notes, drafting the Bill of Rights) is the answer, not the honorific label.

**26. Alexander Hamilton — Treasury & national bank** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Financial plan, national bank, fatal duel · *src: AP US History; biographical canon*  
> The immigrant orphan from the Caribbean who built American public finance from nothing — assumption of state debts, the national bank, the credit system. Present the Hamilton-vs-Jefferson clash over the bank as a genuine constitutional argument (loose vs strict construction), steel-manned both sides, not a hero/villain story. The drama: killed by Vice President Aaron Burr in the 1804 Weehawken duel. A specific policy or the duel is the answer. Economics note: keep it the historical finance story, not a modern-monetary-policy editorial.

**27. First political parties: Federalists vs Democratic-Republicans** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Hamilton vs Jefferson factions emerge · *src: AP US History Period 3*  
> Origin of the American two-party rivalry, born from the Hamilton-Jefferson split over the bank, the French Revolution, and how strong the central government should be. Present each side's actual vision (Federalist commerce-and-strong-center vs Republican agrarian-and-states) without taking a partisan side — steel-man both per §6. The defining disagreement is the answer.

**28. Washington's Farewell Address & precedents** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Warns against parties and foreign entanglements · *src: AP US History; Hirsch*  
> Virtue-and-wisdom capstone of the Washington thread. The voluntary surrender of power (setting the two-term precedent) plus the specific warnings — against the 'baneful effects' of party faction and against permanent foreign alliances. The specific counsel is the answer. Ties to the Cincinnatus theme; show the restraint, don't preach it.

**29. Whiskey Rebellion (1794)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Washington enforces federal tax by force · *src: AP US History Period 3*  
> The new government proving it could enforce its own law where the Confederation could not — Washington personally riding out at the head of ~13,000 militia, the only sitting president to lead troops in the field. The contrast with Shays' Rebellion (which the old government couldn't answer) is the teaching point. The specific show-of-force fact is the answer.

**30. Election of 1800 & peaceful transfer of power** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Jefferson defeats Adams; Burr tie; House decides · *src: AP US History; civics canon*  
> A genuine wonder of the founding — Jefferson called it the 'Revolution of 1800' because for the first time in modern history a government handed power to its bitter rivals WITHOUT bloodshed, by ballot. The Jefferson-Burr electoral tie thrown to the House (36 ballots), fixed afterward by the 12th Amendment. The peaceful-transfer fact is the answer; this is self-government working. Honors the rule-of-law inheritance.

**31. Marbury v. Madison & judicial review** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Supreme Court can strike down laws · *src: AP US History; civics canon*  
> Chief Justice John Marshall's quiet masterstroke — by declining a small power, the Court claimed the far greater one: judicial review, the authority to strike down laws that violate the Constitution. Western rule-of-law inheritance; checks-and-balances in action. The specific power established is the answer, not the case caption.

**32. Louisiana Purchase (1803)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Jefferson buys vast land from France · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US History*  
> Scale-wonder: ~828,000 square miles, roughly doubling the country, for about $15 million (~3 cents an acre) from a cash-strapped Napoleon. The number is the hook. Honest tension: Jefferson the strict-constructionist stretched his own constitutional scruples to do it — and the land was not empty; it was home to many Indigenous nations whose displacement followed (state plainly, no theater). The 'doubled the nation' fact is the answer.

**33. Lewis and Clark & Sacagawea** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Expedition explores West to Pacific · *src: Core Knowledge Grade 5; cultural canon*  
> Epic-journey wonder (the Corps of Discovery, ~8,000 miles to the Pacific and back, only one death the whole trip). Sacagawea — the Shoshone teenager carrying her infant, interpreting and guiding — presented as the substantive figure she was, on her own terms (moral_vision §4, no condescension toward any culture), not a footnote. York, Clark's enslaved man who made the journey, is part of the honest record. A specific feat or figure is the answer.

**34. War of 1812 & burning of Washington** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Second war with Britain; capital burned · *src: AP US History Period 4; Core Knowledge*  
> The 'second war of independence.' Vivid scene-set: British troops burning the Capitol and White House in 1814, Dolley Madison saving the Gilbert Stuart portrait of Washington as she fled. The specific dramatic image (the burning, Dolley's rescue) is the answer. Honest record — a near-disaster that ended in a draw but cemented American independence.

**35. Star-Spangled Banner & Fort McHenry** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Key writes anthem during bombardment · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural canon*  
> Origin-story wonder: Francis Scott Key watching the all-night British bombardment of Fort McHenry from a ship, seeing the enormous garrison flag still flying 'by the dawn's early light.' The specific image (the flag still there at dawn) is the answer. National-symbol heritage told as the concrete scene it was.

**36. Battle of New Orleans & Andrew Jackson** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Lopsided victory fought after peace signed · *src: AP US History; cultural canon*  
> Cool-fact reversal: the greatest American land victory of the war was fought two weeks AFTER the peace treaty was signed — news traveled slower than armies. Jackson's mixed force (regulars, militia, free Black soldiers, Lafitte's pirates, Choctaw) shattered the British assault. The fought-after-peace fact is the answer. Note Jackson honestly — the New Orleans hero who would later drive the Trail of Tears (framework keeps that for the next strand, but no hagiography here).

**37. Abigail Adams & 'Remember the Ladies'** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Early advocate for women in new republic · *src: Core Knowledge; women's-history canon*  
> Virtue-and-voice beat told through her actual words — her 1776 letter urging John to 'Remember the Ladies' and not put 'unlimited power into the hands of the Husbands,' warning of rebellion otherwise. Use the real quote as the hook; her specific argument is the answer. A natural-rights logic applied consistently — the same principle the founding rested on, pressed further. No anachronistic framing; let her own words carry it.

**38. John Adams — second president & XYZ Affair** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Defends soldiers; Alien and Sedition Acts · *src: AP US History; founding canon*  
> Independence champion and a study in principle vs power. The honor: defended the Boston Massacre soldiers; kept the nation out of a ruinous war with France. The black mark, stated plainly (no hagiography, framework §5): the Alien and Sedition Acts criminalized criticism of the government — a real assault on the free-speech principle the founding professed, and a cautionary tale the bank tells honestly. The specific act or stand is the answer.

**39. Betsy Ross & the first flag legend** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T2 · wt=low  
Stars and stripes origin story · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural folklore*  
> Treat as the cherished LEGEND it is, honestly — the story (Ross sewing the first flag, the five-pointed star she could cut in one snip) was popularized by her grandson decades later and isn't firmly documented. Tell it as a beloved national folk-tale, not a staged 'actually it's false' reveal (no-TIL rule). Light tier; the flag's design detail can be the answer.

**40. Locke, natural rights, and the philosophical roots of the founding** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Life, liberty, property; government by consent; the right of revolution · *src: vision-injected*  
> The IDEA the whole founding rests on — the consensus list jumps straight to grievances and battles but skips the philosophy that justified them, which is the literal first link in the bank's stated lineage (Locke -> the Founders -> Tocqueville -> Douglass/Lincoln/King). Teach John Locke's claim: people have natural rights to life, liberty, and property that exist BEFORE government; government is legitimate only by the consent of the governed; and when it turns destructive of those ends, the people may alter or abolish it. Jefferson lifted this almost directly into the Declaration. Engage it as the serious political philosophy it is. The specific Lockean move (rights precede government / consent / right of revolution) is the answer.

**41. Negative rights vs positive 'rights' — what 'unalienable' means** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Rights as freedoms others must merely respect, not claims on others' labor · *src: vision-injected*  
> moral_vision §3.9 is one of the most explicit, load-bearing rules in the vision and it lives RIGHT HERE — the Declaration's 'unalienable rights.' Teach the distinction the bank insists children learn: a NEGATIVE right (to your life, liberty, speech, conscience, property) asks only that others LEAVE YOU ALONE, so everyone can be honored at once at no cost to anyone; a POSITIVE 'right' to a good or service is a claim on someone else's labor and cannot be met without conscripting a provider. The founders' 'life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness' are freedoms, NOT entitlements to the baker's bread. Steel-man the opposing view (FDR's 1944 'Second Bill of Rights') at its strongest per §3.9 — present it fairly, never mock it — but the bank's line falls with Locke/Bastiat/Nozick. The specific freedom-vs-entitlement distinction is the answer. NEVER teach a child food or shelter is a natural right (§3.9 forbids exactly this).

**42. Washington resigns his commission — the American Cincinnatus** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
1783: victorious general voluntarily surrenders power to Congress · *src: vision-injected*  
> The single clearest virtue-by-example moment in the strand, called out implicitly by the framework's 'refused a crown' and moral_vision's show-don't-preach mandate — worth decomposing from the broad Washington ladder. Story-led: with the war won and an army that adored him, Washington could plausibly have made himself king (the Newburgh officers nearly mutinied; some floated a crown). Instead, in December 1783 he rode to Annapolis and handed his commission back to Congress, then went home to his farm — consciously modeling himself on the Roman Cincinnatus who returned to his plow. King George III, told Washington meant to resign, reportedly said it would make him 'the greatest character of the age.' The voluntary surrender of power is the answer. Show the virtue; never sermonize it (moral_vision §5).

**43. Religious liberty at the founding (Virginia Statute & the First Amendment)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
No established church; freedom of conscience for all faiths · *src: vision-injected*  
> Directly serves the vision's careful dual stance: Christian heritage is foundational (moral_vision §3.4) AND the bank is explicitly NOT Christian nationalist (§9) — religious liberty applies to every tradition. The founding got this right and it belongs in the strand. Teach Jefferson's 1786 Virginia Statute for Religious Freedom (which he ranked among his three proudest achievements, on his tombstone) and Madison's 'Memorial and Remonstrance,' which together disestablished the state church and made conscience free — then the First Amendment generalized it: no established national religion, free exercise for all. Present as the genuine innovation it was — a polity founded NOT on an established religion (§3.6). The specific principle (no establishment + free exercise / disestablishment) is the answer.

**44. The Northwest Ordinance (1787) bans slavery in the territories** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Antislavery and the rule of law written into the new western lands · *src: vision-injected*  
> The honest founding record runs BOTH directions — the same generation that wrote the three-fifths compromise also, in the same year (1787), barred slavery forever from the entire Northwest Territory (the future Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Michigan, Wisconsin) and guaranteed there public schools, freedom of religion, and trial by jury. This is the antislavery and rule-of-law thread the framework requires telling alongside slavery's wound (framework §3 forbids adopting the founding-was-only-about-slavery reframe; this is the counter-evidence stated as fact). Not triumphalism — it sat beside the contradiction. The specific provision (slavery barred from the Northwest Territory) is the answer.

**45. Phillis Wheatley — poet of the founding era** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Enslaved teenager becomes the first published African-American poet · *src: vision-injected*  
> Strengthens the honest, full-color American founding the vision wants — and does it through achievement, not grievance (moral_vision's show-don't-preach, no-condescension rules). Wheatley was kidnapped from West Africa as a child, enslaved in Boston, and by her late teens (1773) became the first African-American to publish a book of poetry — examined by a panel of skeptical Boston worthies (reportedly including John Hancock) who had to certify a Black woman had actually written it. She corresponded with Washington, who received her in 1776. The specific feat (first published African-American poet) is the answer. Tell it straight, on her own terms, as part of the founding generation.


## 19th century: Industrial Revolution, nationalism, US Civil War, imperialism (62)

**1. James Watt and the steam engine** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Steam power, separate condenser, the horsepower unit · *src: Core Knowledge; AP World Unit 6; Hirsch cultural literacy*  
> Pillar 1 keystone — the Western tradition as real human achievement. Celebrate ordinary ingenuity and the beauty of mechanism: WHY the separate condenser (1765, the Glasgow Green walk) made steam efficient enough to remake the world. Cool-fact-is-answer: the named insight (condense the steam in a separate vessel so the cylinder stays hot), not 'who invented X'. Watt the instrument-maker, Boulton the partner.

**2. The factory system and textile mills** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Spinning jenny, power loom, Arkwright's water frame, mill towns · *src: Core Knowledge IR unit; AP World Unit 6*  
> Pillar 1 — the leap from cottage craft to mechanized mass production told as ingenuity (Hargreaves' jenny, Arkwright's water frame, Cartwright's loom), with the human cost (child labor, mill discipline) stated plainly in the same honest record per moral_vision §4 'honest not preachy'. No 'modern audiences find this troubling' framing — state the 14-hour day as fact alongside the productivity miracle.

**3. The railway revolution and Stephenson's Rocket** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Steam locomotives, the Rocket 1829, railway mania · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1; AP World Unit 6*  
> Pillar 1 wonder — railways collapsed distance. Cool-fact-is-answer: the Rainhill Trials 1829 where the Rocket beat its rivals, the named multitube boiler innovation, the speed that astonished. Lead with the strange/dramatic specific (a machine outrunning a horse) per moral_vision §2 wonder-first.

**4. Bessemer process and the age of steel** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Cheap mass steel, 1856 converter, rails and bridges · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1; cultural literacy*  
> Pillar 1 + Pillar 6 mechanism wonder — blasting air through molten iron to burn off carbon (the shower of sparks). Cheap steel as the substrate of skyscrapers, bridges, rails. Beauty-of-mechanism framing: WHY blowing cold air made the iron hotter (the carbon itself burns). Threads into the Gilded Age industrialists (Carnegie).

**5. Child labor and industrial working conditions** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Mines, mills, 14-hour days, Factory Acts reform · *src: Core Knowledge IR unit; AP World Unit 6 ideologies*  
> Honest record per moral_vision §4 — state the conditions plainly (small children pulling coal carts, the breaker boys, the trapper in the dark), then the named reform that ended it: the Factory Acts, the 1842 Mines Act, Lord Shaftesbury's campaign (a Christian reformer in the Wilberforce line). Honest, not cynical; reform credited to named people who acted at cost. NOT a Marxist-inevitability frame — the abuses were ended by liberal reform and a wealthier society, not revolution.

**6. Labor unions and the early reform movements** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Trade unions, strikes, shorter hours, suffrage agitation · *src: AP World Unit 6; Core Knowledge*  
> Present on its own terms — workers organizing freely to bargain (the Tolpuddle Martyrs 1834, the Chartists' six points, the ten-hour movement). Steel-man honestly without adopting the labor-theory-of-value frame as fact. Note the classical-liberal counterpoint where natural: rising real wages and falling hours came chiefly from capital accumulation and productivity, not only from agitation (moral_vision tradition, §3.3). Avoid framing the union as the sole engine of progress.

**7. Marx, Engels, and the Communist Manifesto (1848)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Class struggle, 1848 manifesto, Das Kapital 1867 · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1; AP World Unit 6 ideologies*  
> CRITICAL dual-commitment per moral_vision §3.1 + §1.7. Marxism-AS-PHILOSOPHY gets serious treatment — alienation, historical materialism, the critique of ideology are real intellectual contributions; attribute the framing to Marx ('Marx argued...') per the §6 advocacy-framing rule, never adopt 'the proletariat'/'false consciousness' as neutral fact. Sharp distinction held: this is the idea, written in the British Museum reading room, that the 20th century would run as an experiment costing 65-100M lives (the bank counts that record elsewhere). Steel-man the philosophy; the body count is cited where Marxism-in-practice appears (off-strand, 20th c). NO strawman, NO mockery.

**8. Eli Whitney's cotton gin and its consequence** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
1793 gin, unintended mass expansion of slavery · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 6 technology; APUSH*  
> Surprise-reversal done honestly (moral_vision §2) — an invention meant to ease labor instead entrenched and expanded slavery across the cotton South. State the consequence plainly as fact, no theatrical 'secretly bad' reveal (the §6 TIL ban). Cool-fact-is-answer: the gin's effect (slavery exploded rather than withered) is the knowable payoff. Sets up the Civil War strand.

**9. The telegraph and Morse code** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
Morse 1844, 'What hath God wrought', instant messages · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 6 technology*  
> Pillar 6 wonder — first time information beat the messenger. Cool-fact-is-answer: the first message 'What hath God wrought' (Numbers 23:23, chosen by Annie Ellsworth), 1844 Baltimore-Washington line. Lead with the wonder of distance annihilated. Western-ingenuity celebration.

**10. Bell, Edison, and the late-century invention boom** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Telephone 1876, light bulb 1879, war of currents · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 1 + Pillar 6*  
> Pillar 1/6 — American ingenuity building daily modern life. War of the Currents drama (Edison's DC vs Tesla/Westinghouse AC) is great cool-fact-is-answer material. Honest: Edison the relentless tinkerer ('1% inspiration, 99% perspiration'), Tesla the visionary. Celebrate the achievement; avoid hagiography (Edison's hardball tactics are fair game as fact).

**11. Urbanization and the growth of industrial cities** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Slums, sanitation, cholera, public-health reform · *src: AP World Unit 6; Core Knowledge*  
> Honest record + the heroic fix. Cool-fact-is-answer: John Snow's 1854 Broad Street pump handle (removing it ended the cholera outbreak and founded epidemiology) — discovery-recognition, how-we-know-what-we-know. Bazalgette's London sewers. State the squalor plainly, then credit the named reformers who solved it. Mechanism + virtue-by-example.

**12. Great Famine in Ireland and the age of migration** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Potato blight 1845-52, mass emigration to America · *src: AP World Unit 6 migration; Core Knowledge*  
> Honest, not cynical (moral_vision §4). State the catastrophe plainly: ~1M dead, ~1M emigrated, the blight, the coffin ships, and the contested record of British policy (food exports continued, the doctrinaire laissez-faire response of Trevelyan). Present the policy-failure facts without anti-Western inherent-condemnation framing (§9) — state what happened. The migration reshaped America (Boston, New York).

**13. Abolition of the British slave trade (Wilberforce)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
1807 Slave Trade Act, 1833 emancipation, Royal Navy squadron · *src: HISTORY_FRAMEWORK Pillar 1 + §5; cultural literacy*  
> FLAGSHIP vision topic — abolition as a Christian-led moral crusade the West produced and led (moral_vision §3.4, framework §5). Wilberforce, Clarkson, the Quakers, the evangelical revival; the 1807 Act, 1833 emancipation, and the Royal Navy West Africa Squadron 1808-1860s that hunted slavers at sea. Cool-fact-is-answer: Clarkson's evidence chest, the Brookes diagram, '20 years' of persistence. This is the imago-Dei principle in action. Pair naturally with the honest record of the internal African slave trade the Squadron also suppressed. HEAVY weight, vision-mandated.

**14. Haitian Revolution and Toussaint Louverture** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Only successful slave revolt, 1804 independence · *src: Core Knowledge Latin America unit; APUSH Unit 5*  
> Present on its own terms with full drama — enslaved people defeating Napoleon's army to found a free nation (1804). Cool-fact-is-answer: Toussaint Louverture the self-taught general, the only successful large-scale slave revolt in history. Natural-rights story — the Declaration's principles claimed by the enslaved themselves. Honest about the aftermath's difficulty without editorializing. Virtue-by-example (courage at cost).

**15. Latin American independence: Bolivar and San Martin** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Spanish colonies freed, the Liberator, Andes crossing · *src: Core Knowledge Unit 6 Latin America; AP World*  
> Story-led drama — San Martin's army crossing the Andes (a feat to rival Hannibal), Bolivar 'the Liberator' freeing six nations, their meeting at Guayaquil. Cool-fact-is-answer: the named impossible crossing, the named liberator. The natural-rights/self-government wave spreading from the American and French models across a continent. Present on its own terms.

**16. Mexican independence and Father Hidalgo** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Grito de Dolores 1810, priest-led revolt · *src: Core Knowledge Latin America unit*  
> Story-led — the village priest Miguel Hidalgo ringing his church bell at Dolores, Sept 16 1810, the Grito that launched a nation. Cool-fact-is-answer: the named church-bell cry. Christianity as a real historical agent (a priest leading the revolt). Present on its own terms.

**17. Greek War of Independence** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
1821-29 revolt against Ottomans, Byron at Missolonghi · *src: AP World nationalism; cultural literacy (Byron)*  
> Romantic nationalism that stirred all Europe — Western philhellenism (Europe's debt to Athens drawing volunteers). Cool-fact-is-answer: Lord Byron dying of fever at Missolonghi 1824 having given his fortune to the cause; the naval victory at Navarino 1827. Drama-of-the-great-figure (Byron). The Christian Greeks throwing off four centuries of Ottoman rule, stated as fact.

**18. The Revolutions of 1848** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Springtime of Peoples, liberal-nationalist uprisings across Europe · *src: AP World Unit 6 + Euro; Core Knowledge*  
> The liberal-nationalist 'Springtime of Peoples' — the demand for constitutions, free press, and self-government sweeping nearly every European capital. Present the classical-liberal aspirations seriously (moral_vision tradition). Note the honest outcome: most were crushed within a year, yet they planted the constitutional seed. Same year as the Manifesto — the liberal and the socialist roads diverging.

**19. Italian unification (Risorgimento): Garibaldi and Cavour** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Redshirts, Expedition of the Thousand, 1861 kingdom · *src: AP World/Euro nationalism; Core Knowledge*  
> Story-led drama — Garibaldi's thousand Redshirts sailing to conquer Sicily and Naples, then handing it all to the king (the romantic hero who took no crown). Cool-fact-is-answer: the Expedition of the Thousand, the named handover at Teano. Cavour the diplomat-architect. Present on its own terms; the patchwork forged into one Italy 1861.

**20. German unification: Bismarck and blood and iron** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=medium  
Realpolitik, three wars, 1871 empire at Versailles · *src: AP World/Euro nationalism; cultural literacy*  
> Present on its own terms — Bismarck's Realpolitik and 'blood and iron' speech, three engineered wars (Denmark, Austria, France), the Empire proclaimed in the Hall of Mirrors at Versailles 1871. Cool-fact-is-answer: the named doctrine ('blood and iron'), the calculated diplomacy. Honest portrait of statecraft without moralizing; this is the power-recognition (how a balance of power was built and would later shatter into WWI).

**21. Franco-Prussian War and the capture at Sedan** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
1870 war, Napoleon III captured, German Empire proclaimed · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3 19th-c battles*  
> Pillar 3 decisive moment — the war that birthed Germany and humiliated France. Cool-fact-is-answer: the encirclement at Sedan 1870 where an emperor (Napoleon III) was captured with his whole army, and the German Empire proclaimed on French soil. Story-led generals + consequence. Sets the grievance that would feed 1914 and 1919.

**22. Queen Victoria and the British Empire at its height** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Victorian era, empire on which sun never set · *src: Cultural literacy; AP World/Euro*  
> Pillar 1 era-defining — the empire 'on which the sun never set'. Cool-fact-is-answer: vivid specifics (Victoria crowned at 18, her 63-year reign, Empress of India 1877, the Great Exhibition's Crystal Palace 1851). Celebrate the achievements (rule of law spread, abolition enforced, engineering) honestly alongside imperial costs stated plainly per moral_vision §3.4 — neither romanticized nor apologized for.

**23. Crimean War and the Charge of the Light Brigade** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
1854 suicidal cavalry charge, 'into the valley of Death' · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3 19th-c battles*  
> Pillar 3 iconic moment — a blundered order made immortal by Tennyson. Cool-fact-is-answer: the named charge 'into the valley of Death' at Balaclava 1854, the miscommunicated order sending light cavalry against the wrong guns ('Theirs not to reason why'). Drama + the poem that fixed it in memory. Story-led.

**24. Florence Nightingale and modern nursing** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
Crimea, the Lady with the Lamp, hospital sanitation · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural literacy*  
> Virtue-by-example + discovery-recognition. Cool-fact-is-answer: the Lady with the Lamp who cut death rates by attacking filth, AND her pioneering use of the polar-area 'rose' diagram to prove sanitation saved lives (data persuading the establishment). A woman earning the establishment's respect through rigor — moral_vision celebrates exactly this (cf. Mary Anning). Show virtue, don't preach it.

**25. Solferino and the founding of the Red Cross** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
1859 battle, Henri Dunant, Geneva Conventions · *src: HISTORY_TEMPLATES Pillar 3*
