CRITICAL per §6 dated-references + §framework current-politics line: keep it HISTORICAL — the 1948 founding and 1967/1973 wars are in scope; the present-day conflict and active politicians are OUT. No verdict on the contested present (§no-verdict-on-contested). State the founding and the conflict's origin as facts.

**33. Marshall-era Economic Miracles (Japan & West Germany)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Defeated nations become economic powerhouses · *src: AP World; AP Euro*  
> A natural §3.2/§3.3 economics-lens topic: how the LOSERS of WWII grew rich and FREE under market economies + the rule of law, while command economies stagnated. The German Wirtschaftswunder (Erhard freeing prices overnight 1948), Japan's rise. Frame as the seen-vs-unseen verdict made visible — free markets + sound institutions produced the miracle. The named reform/figure is the answer.

**34. Globalization & the Internet Age** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Connected world economy, trade, and technology · *src: AP World 8.8; modern canon*  
> §3.6 names the Internet as part of the technological substrate the American open society built. Frame the post-Cold-War connected world with the §3.2 trade lens (specialization and exchange lift billions out of poverty — a real human good). Keep it timeless and substantive (ARPANET origins, the container ship, the fall of trade barriers); steer clear of post-2010 current-politics flashpoints (§6).

**35. September 11, 2001** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Terror attacks reshape the modern era · *src: AP US Since 1945; modern canon*  
> In scope (the framework permits settled events through ~2010; 9/11 is historical fact, not active partisan debate). Present soberly and concretely — the towers, the hijacked planes, Flight 93's passengers fighting back ('Let's roll') as virtue-by-example under the §4 honest-not-cynical rule. Keep STRICTLY to the 2001 event and its immediate human story; do NOT drift into post-2010 war-on-terror politics (§6). The named act of courage is the answer; glib distractors banned (§viral 'glib about real suffering').

**36. European Union & the Euro** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low  
Former enemies unite into one economic bloc · *src: AP Euro; modern canon*  
> Present on its own terms — old rivals (France and Germany, three wars in 70 years) building a single market and currency, a genuine achievement of postwar peace. Honest at T5 with the §3.2 lens available: monetary union without fiscal union has real tensions worth naming as economics, not partisanship. Keep timeless; no post-2010 current-politics (§6). No verdict — present the structure and the aspiration.

**37. The Holodomor & Walter Duranty's Cover-Up** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Stalin's engineered Ukrainian famine 1932-33 and the NYT reporter who hid it · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED in the heaviest detail the bank possesses. 3.5-7M Ukrainians; collectivization tripled grain quotas; borders sealed; villagers caught eating dropped grain shot. Walter Duranty's NYT cover-up EXPLICITLY NAMED — won the 1932 Pulitzer for whitewash reporting while privately conceding to the British embassy 'perhaps 10 million' had died; Pulitzer never revoked. Gareth Jones broke the story honestly and was murdered in Mongolia 1935 (virtue-at-cost). Steelman distractors include the establishment-soft-pedaled 'about half a million' so the kid picks the honest number. The consensus list folds the entire USSR into 'glasnost' and 'collapse' — the atrocity record MUST stand on its own.

**38. Mao's Great Leap Forward & Cultural Revolution** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The largest famine in history and the decade of chaos that followed · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED. Great Leap Forward 1958-62: 30-45M dead (the largest famine in human history); backyard furnaces melting farm tools for useless pig-iron; the four-pests campaign (killing sparrows → locust plagues); grain exported during famine. Cultural Revolution 1966-76: Red Guards, the 'four olds,' intellectuals sent to the countryside, struggle sessions. Chang & Halliday's Mao cited. The consensus 'Chinese Revolution 1949' entry stops at the takeover; the atrocities — the actual human cost of Maoism — are required content the consensus list omits entirely.

**39. The Khmer Rouge & the Killing Fields** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Pol Pot's Year Zero and the Cambodian genocide 1975-79 · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED. 2M dead (a quarter of Cambodia's population); Year Zero; the forced evacuation of Phnom Penh at gunpoint; people killed for wearing glasses (a mark of the educated); Tuol Sleng / S-21 prison; the Killing Fields. State the evil plainly (§4). CRITICAL linkage the bank should make: this followed the US withdrawal from Indochina — the consequence-of-withdrawal thread noted on the Vietnam topic. Honest, unflinching, no euphemism.

**40. The Gulag & Solzhenitsyn's Witness** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The Soviet camp system and the man who exposed it from inside · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED at the highest billing — Solzhenitsyn is a named exemplar in moral_vision §2, §4, §5, AND §8 (one of only four 'great question' models). The Gulag Archipelago built from 227 prisoner testimonies, ~18M passed through the camps; Magadan, Kolyma, Vorkuta. The VIRTUE story is the heart: he memorized fragments and smuggled them out chapter by chapter, never holding the full manuscript, because the KGB would find it. Arrested 1945, One Day in the Life of Ivan Denisovich 1962, exiled 1974. Show virtue by example (§5), never preach it. The book proved the camps were a CORE FEATURE of the Soviet state from 1918, not a Stalin aberration.

**41. Sakharov, Havel & the Dissidents of Conscience** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The scientists and writers who defied the Soviet bloc from within · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED — moral_vision names Sakharov and Havel directly (§1 lineage 'the moral witness of Solzhenitsyn, Havel, and Sakharov'; §2 virtue exemplars). Andrei Sakharov: built the Soviet H-bomb, then lost everything (the 1975 Nobel Peace Prize he couldn't collect, internal exile in Gorky) opposing the regime's weapons and lies. Vaclav Havel: the playwright jailed for samizdat, typing under occupation, who walked from prison cell to the presidency of a free Czechoslovakia (the Velvet Revolution 1989). Courage-at-cost (§2, §5) — show it, don't preach it. These are the human faces of the moral conflict's victory.

**42. North Korea: The Prison State** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
The totalitarian regime and camps that still operate · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED. moral_vision §3.1 names 'North Korean prison camps that still operate' as part of the established atrocity record; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §3 names the kwan-li-so camps and the Arduous March famine (hundreds of thousands to millions dead, 1990s). The night-time satellite photo (South ablaze with light, North a black void) is the wonder-legible hook and the answer. Frame as the still-living proof of what the Korean War's northern outcome produced — the consensus 'Korean War' entry stops at 1953; the regime's nature is the required completion. Keep historical (the famine, the camp system as documented) without drifting into active current-politics (§6).

**43. Castro's Cuba: La Cabaña & the Exiles** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
The executions and exodus that followed the Cuban Revolution · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED. HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §3 names 'Castro's La Cabaña executions; Che killed Bolivia 1967.' The consensus 'Cuban Revolution & Castro' entry presents the takeover but not what the regime DID: Che Guevara presiding over the La Cabaña firing squads, the political prisoners, the mass exodus (the 1980 Mariel boatlift, the rafters). Present honestly (§4) — this punctures the romantic-revolutionary myth with the documented record. The named place/figure is the answer.

**44. Reagan's 'Evil Empire' & the Coalition That Won the Cold War** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The Reagan-Thatcher-John Paul II partnership and the moral case against the USSR · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED to CELEBRATE — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §4 names 'the Reagan-Thatcher-John Paul II coalition celebrated' and 'Reagan's 1983 Evil Empire speech' specifically. Reagan named the USSR what it was when détente-minded elites called it gauche; the arms buildup (and SDI/'Star Wars') that the §3.2 economic-exhaustion thread says helped bankrupt the Soviets; Thatcher the 'Iron Lady'; JP2 the Polish pope. Frame as the moral-clarity-plus-strategy that ended the conflict. This is the Cold War's moral spine and the consensus list reduces Reagan to one wall speech.

**45. John Paul II's Warsaw Homily: 'Be Not Afraid'** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
The 1979 Mass that sparked Solidarity and shook the Soviet bloc · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §4 names 'JP2's June 1979 Warsaw homily (Be not afraid) as the spark of Solidarity' specifically. The Polish pope returning home June 1979; a million Poles in Victory Square chanting 'We want God'; the regime watching its monopoly on public life evaporate in nine days. Treat Christianity as the substantive historical force it was (moral_vision §3.4, framework 'stance preserved') — JP2 as a real Cold War agent, not decoration. The named phrase is the answer. Pairs directly with the Solidarity topic.

**46. The Civil Rights Movement as Moral Victory** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Douglass-to-King and the natural-rights case that ended Jim Crow · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED at the bank's core. moral_vision §3.6 and §8 name the natural-rights line 'Frederick Douglass at Rochester (1852), Abraham Lincoln at Cooper Union (1860), Martin Luther King in Birmingham (1963)' — and the Douglass July-4th question is one of only FOUR 'great question' exemplars in §8. The KEY framing: the movement WON by appealing to the natural-rights tradition of the founding documents (King's 'promissory note,' the Letter from Birmingham Jail), not by rejecting them — America's failure measured against America's own creed. State Jim Crow plainly (§3.6 named failure). Virtue-by-example. The home-front moral story of the modern American era; the foreign-policy-weighted consensus list has no purely-American civil-rights entry.

**47. The Economic Verdict: How the West Bankrupted the USSR** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Why the command economy lost — the calculation problem made visible · *src: vision-injected*  
> MANDATED by the bank's economics spine. moral_vision §3.2-§3.3 treat the Mises-Hayek calculation problem as established fact — and the Cold War was the largest real-world test of it. Frame the Soviet collapse not as bad luck but as the PREDICTED result: without market prices for capital goods, the planners could not rationally allocate; chronic shortages, the bread queues, the empty shelves beside a bristling arsenal. The seen-vs-unseen (§3.2 Bastiat lens) made continental. Steelman the planned-economy case at its strongest, then let the historical result answer it (moral_vision steelmanning rule). This connects the Cold War's end to WHY it ended on the terms it did.


## Science, invention & exploration (cross-era keystones) (49)

**1. Archimedes of Syracuse** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Ancient Greek inventor and mathematician · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Lead with the wonder + the courage-unto-death drama, not the formula. 'Don't disturb my circles' as his last words at the Roman sack of Syracuse 212 BC (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK names this as a T1 drama-of-minds hook); the bath-tub Eureka; the lever ('give me a place to stand'); the screw still lifting water; the war machines that held off Rome. Founding genius of the Western mechanical tradition (moral_vision §3.3 Athens lineage). The cool specific is the answer, not Archimedes' name.

**2. Aristotle as natural philosopher** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Classical observation, classification, geocentric model · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge; Western tradition canon*  
> NO condescension toward the past (moral_vision §4): 'Aristotle was not stupid for thinking the heart was the seat of thought.' Present him as the man who taught the West to OBSERVE and CLASSIFY — the marine-biology dissections, the systematic study of animals — whose authority then shaped 2000 years. The reversal hook is that his very greatness made his errors load-bearing (the thing Galileo/Copernicus had to overturn was Aristotle-via-the-Church-authority). Athens node of the Athens-to-Philadelphia inheritance (§3.3).

**3. Ptolemy and the geocentric universe** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Earth-centered model dominant until Copernicus · *src: AP Euro; history-of-astronomy canon*  
> Present on its own terms — the geocentric model was a genuine predictive achievement (epicycles forecast planetary positions for 1400 years), not a dumb superstition. Frames the Copernican revolution: you can't feel the drama of Copernicus/Galileo without respecting what they had to dethrone. Beauty-of-mechanism angle (§2): WHY the model worked well enough to last.

**4. Eratosthenes measures the Earth** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Calculated Earth's circumference with shadows · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Pure wonder + ordinary ingenuity (moral_vision §2): a librarian in Alexandria measured the whole planet with two sticks, two shadows, and the distance between Syene and Alexandria — and got it nearly right ~240 BC. Surprise-reversal payload: educated ancients KNEW the Earth was round and roughly how big (kills the flat-earth-myth misconception). The decidable cool fact (how he did it / what shadow angle) is the answer.

**5. Roman engineering: aqueducts, roads, concrete** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Aqueducts, roads, arches, Roman concrete · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge Rome unit*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism (§2: 'WHY the Roman concrete still stands') + the Rome node of the Western inheritance (§3.3). The self-healing seawater concrete, the Pont du Gard, the gravity-fed aqueduct gradient, roads still walkable 2000 years on. Western art + architecture as inheritance worth celebrating (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK). The specific marvel (Pantheon's unreinforced dome, the concrete chemistry) is the answer.

**6. Chinese Four Great Inventions** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Paper, printing, gunpowder, the compass · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge China units*  
> Non-Western civilization on its OWN terms, NOT an exotic foil (moral_vision §4, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK pillar 5). Song-dynasty printing, the lodestone compass, gunpowder from alchemists seeking immortality. Honest comparison without condescension AND without false equivalence: China invented these first, yet the institutional take-off happened in the West (§4 'some traditions developed institutions others did not... the bank says so when relevant'). The specific origin/use is the answer.

**7. Islamic Golden Age: algebra, optics, medicine** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Al-Khwarizmi, Ibn al-Haytham, Avicenna, House of Wisdom · *src: AP World; encyclopedias; Core Knowledge*  
> Covered substantively but NOT over-weighted (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK pillar 5 explicit). Ibn al-Haytham inventing the controlled experiment in optics; al-Khwarizmi's algebra; Ibn Sina's Canon as the medical text for 600 years; the Baghdad House of Wisdom translation movement. Courage-in-pursuit-of-truth roster names Ibn Sina (§2). The honest DECLINE thread is REQUIRED here and I inject it separately (al-Ghazali ~1100, Mongol sack of Baghdad 1258) — stated as historical fact, not editorialized.

**8. Hindu-Arabic numerals and zero** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Place-value system and the concept of zero · *src: AP World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Wonder + non-Western achievement on its own terms: zero as a number (Brahmagupta's rules ~628), place value, the system that traveled India to Baghdad to Fibonacci's Pisa. The reversal: Roman numerals make arithmetic almost impossible — the 'nothing' symbol was a revolution. Concrete-over-abstract (§5): why you can't multiply MMXLVIII easily. The specific innovation is the answer.

**9. Isaac Newton: gravity and motion** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Gravity, three laws, Principia, calculus, prism · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro; cultural-literacy canon*  
> The supreme Scientific-Revolution figure and explicitly a BELIEVER (moral_vision §3.4 names Newton; HISTORY_FRAMEWORK 'Newton, Galileo, Kepler — all believers per the historical record'). The plague-year 1666 apple at Woolsthorpe is a T1 drama hook. Unified heavens and Earth under one physics. The prism splitting white light, the inverse-square law, the three laws — the specific marvel is the answer. The Newton-Leibniz priority dispute is a T3 drama hook (note: calculus belongs to the math strand).

**10. Leeuwenhoek, Hooke and the microscope** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Microscope reveals cells and microbes · *src: Core Knowledge science-history; encyclopedias*  
> Pure wonder — opening the unseen world (§2 'Octopuses see with their skin'-style strangeness). A Delft cloth-merchant grinding his own lenses saw 'animalcules' swimming in pond water and his own tooth scrapings; Hooke's Micrographia drew the flea and coined 'cell' from monastery cells. Ordinary ingenuity (§2). The specific first-sighting is the answer.

**11. Columbus and the voyages of 1492** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Atlantic crossing, Spain, the Americas · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US/World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Honest, not cynical, not preachy (moral_vision §4 — uses THIS exact case): 'The Columbian Exchange caused devastating Indigenous mortality from disease — state it. Don't editorialize.' Tell the navigation/seamanship marvel AND the demographic catastrophe plainly, no theatrical reveal, no 'we now know better.' Kill the flat-earth myth (educated people knew the Earth was round; the real dispute was its SIZE — Columbus was wrong, he thought it small). The specific is the answer.

**12. Vasco da Gama and the sea route to India** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Rounded Africa to reach India, 1498 · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge Age of Exploration*  
> Age of Exploration on its own terms — the ocean spice route that broke the Venetian/overland monopoly and reoriented world trade. Honest about the violence da Gama brought to the Indian Ocean (not theatricalized, just stated). The specific navigational/geographic feat (rounding the Cape, the monsoon-wind crossing) is the answer.

**13. Prince Henry, the caravel and navigation tools** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Caravel, astrolabe, compass, Portuguese exploration · *src: AP World; Core Knowledge Age of Exploration*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism + ordinary ingenuity (§2): the ships and instruments that made open-ocean voyaging possible. The lateen sail letting a caravel tack against the wind, the astrolabe/quadrant fixing latitude by the sun and Polaris, dead reckoning. The specific tool-and-what-it-solved is the answer. (Note: 'Henry the Navigator' himself rarely sailed — a teach-the-real-story angle, no hagiography.)

**14. Lewis and Clark expedition** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Crossed America to the Pacific, Sacagawea · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> American story (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK pillar 2) + exploration wonder. Jefferson's Corps of Discovery mapping the Louisiana Purchase; Sacagawea as guide and interpreter (present her substantively, on her own terms, not as a token). The specific (the keelboat, the grizzly encounters, reaching the Pacific 'Ocian in view! O! the joy', the species catalogued) is the answer.

**15. Industrial Revolution: textiles and the factory** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Spinning jenny, power loom, factory system · *src: Core Knowledge Unit 5; AP World/Euro*  
> Real human progress stated honestly INCLUDING the human cost — but the economic substance is an Austrian/seen-unseen matter that lives in economics; here keep it to the inventions + their world-changing scale (§3.3 lens noted, not warped in). The reversal the bank can teach: industrialization, for all its grim early factories, is what ENDED mass subsistence poverty (Pinker/Enlightenment-Now data, §3.5). The specific machine-and-what-it-did is the answer.

**16. The steam locomotive and railroads** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Stephenson's Rocket, transcontinental railroad · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US/World; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Wonder + American story: rail shrank a continent. Stephenson's Rocket winning the Rainhill Trials 1829; the Golden Spike at Promontory 1869 joining the transcontinental line. Honest about who built it (Chinese and Irish labor at brutal cost — stated plainly, §4). The specific (the Rocket's speed, the Spike, the gauge) is the answer.

**17. Eli Whitney: cotton gin and interchangeable parts** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Cotton gin and interchangeable-parts manufacturing · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Honest history without theatrical reveal (§4, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK forbids sanitizing slavery): the cotton gin is the dark-irony case — a labor-saving invention that made short-staple cotton profitable and thereby ENTRENCHED and EXPANDED slavery rather than ending it. State the consequence plainly, no editorializing. The interchangeable-parts idea seeded mass production. The specific mechanism/consequence is the answer.

**18. Samuel Morse and the telegraph** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Telegraph and Morse code, instant messages · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> First instant long-distance communication — annihilating distance is the wonder. 'What hath God wrought' as the first message 1844 (a real quote, biblical, fits the bank's matter-of-fact treatment of the era's faith, §3.4). The specific (the dot-dash code, the first-message content, the 1858 transatlantic cable) is the answer.

**19. Alexander Graham Bell and the telephone** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium  
Invented the telephone, 1876 · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder of voice across wires. Honest teach-the-real-story: Bell beat Elisha Gray to the patent office by hours, and the work grew from teaching the deaf (his mother and wife were deaf). 'Mr. Watson, come here, I want to see you' as the first call. The specific is the answer. No hagiography — the priority race is part of the honest record.

**20. Thomas Edison and the light bulb** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Light bulb, phonograph, Menlo Park lab · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> American ingenuity (§2 ordinary ingenuity, §3.6 American technological substrate). Teach the REAL story, not the myth: Edison didn't invent the bulb, he found a carbon filament that lasted and built the whole electrical SYSTEM (generation, wiring, meters) around it — the 'invention factory' at Menlo Park was itself the innovation. The specific (the 1300 filament trials, the phonograph as his favorite, the system not the bulb) is the answer.

**21. The electricity pioneers: Faraday, Tesla, Franklin** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Franklin's kite, Faraday's induction, Tesla's AC · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Faraday is NAMED in moral_vision §3.4 as one of the believer-scientists ('Newton, Boyle, Faraday, Maxwell') — frame his self-taught bookbinder's-apprentice rise + deep Christian faith as virtue-by-example, NOT as incidental. Franklin's kite + the American-Founder-as-scientist angle (§3.6). Tesla's AC vs Edison's DC 'War of the Currents' drama. The specific discovery (induction = moving magnet makes current; Franklin proving lightning is electricity) is the answer.

**22. Louis Pasteur: germ theory and vaccines** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Germ theory, pasteurization, rabies vaccine · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro; cultural-literacy canon*  
> HISTORY-of-science achievement told STRAIGHT as the conquest of disease — proved germs (not 'bad air') cause illness; saved countless lives. IMPORTANT scope line: the institutional-capture/'trust the science' vaccine SCRUTINY (moral_vision §3.8, MEMORY vaccine-stance) is a SCIENCE-SUBJECT overlay, NOT this history topic — do not import that frame here. Here: the swan-neck flask killing spontaneous generation, the rabies-vaccine save of Joseph Meister 1885. The specific experiment is the answer.

**23. Darwin and evolution by natural selection** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Beagle voyage, Galapagos, Origin of Species · *src: AP World/Bio; Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Present the science straight + the discovery DRAMA (§2): the 5-year Beagle voyage, the Galapagos finch beaks, the 20-year delay until Wallace's letter forced his hand 1858, Origin 1859. NO science-vs-religion strawman either direction (§4 'no smug atheist voice'). Frame as the mechanism that reframed all living things; keep human-application/eugenics OUT (that scrutiny is a science-subject overlay per §3.7 and the science-bank eugenics-history line). The specific mechanism is the answer.

**24. Mendel and the foundations of genetics** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Pea-plant experiments reveal heredity · *src: Core Knowledge science-history; encyclopedias*  
> Mendel is NAMED in moral_vision §3.4 as a believer-scientist ('Mendel, Lemaitre') — frame as the AUGUSTINIAN FRIAR in his monastery garden whose 8 years of pea-counting were IGNORED until rediscovered in 1900, 16 years after he died. Virtue by example (patient honest work unrewarded in his lifetime) + science-and-faith-not-opponents (§3.4). The specific (dominant/recessive ratios, the 3:1, why peas) is the answer.

**25. Joseph Lister and antiseptic surgery** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
Antiseptics make surgery survivable · *src: Core Knowledge; encyclopedias; medicine-history canon*  
> Real human progress + courage against an establishment that mocked him (virtue by example, §2). Carbolic-acid antisepsis after he read Pasteur; surgeons resisted hand-washing for years (the Semmelweis-adjacent tragedy). The reversal: before this, surgery itself killed you via infection. The specific (carbolic spray, the germ-link, the death-rate collapse) is the answer.

**26. Fleming and the discovery of penicillin** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Accidental mold discovery, first antibiotic · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder + ordinary ingenuity + surprise (§2): a contaminated petri dish in 1928 because Fleming was a messy housekeeper. Teach the REAL fuller story (the bank prizes precision): Fleming discovered it but Florey and Chain at Oxford a decade later turned it into a usable drug that saved WWII soldiers — the discovery alone wasn't enough. The specific (the mold-killing-bacteria observation; who made it a drug) is the answer.

**27. Roentgen and the X-ray** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Discovered X-rays; first medical imaging · *src: Encyclopedias; history-of-science canon*  
> Pure wonder + surprise-reversal (§2): an accidental glow in a darkened lab 1895, then the eerie image of his wife's hand-bones-and-wedding-ring — the first time anyone saw inside a living body. He called them 'X' for unknown and refused to patent them (virtue: gave the gift freely). First Nobel in Physics. The specific (what he saw, why 'X', the hand image) is the answer.

**28. Marie Curie and radioactivity** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Radium, polonium, two Nobel Prizes · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Courage-in-pursuit-of-truth + virtue by example (§2, §4): a woman barred from French universities who processed TONS of pitchblende in a leaky shed to isolate radium, won TWO Nobels in different sciences, and ultimately died of the radiation she discovered. Frame her achievement on the merits (not as identity-tokenism — the bank rejects that, §3.5). The specific (polonium named for Poland, the glowing test tubes, two-Nobel feat) is the answer.

**29. The Wright brothers and powered flight** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
First airplane flight, Kitty Hawk 1903 · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> NAMED THREE TIMES in moral_vision (§2 wonder, §2 courage, §2 virtue-by-example: 'the Wright brothers risking everything on a calculation,' 'staking everything on flight'). Two bicycle mechanics who built their own wind tunnel, solved 3-axis control when funded experts failed, and flew 12 seconds at Kitty Hawk 1903 — American ingenuity (§3.6 'the airplane' as American technological substrate). The specific (wing-warping/3-axis control, the wind tunnel, 12 seconds/120 feet) is the answer.

**30. Henry Ford and the assembly line** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Model T, assembly line, mass production · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> American story + real human progress (§3.6): the moving assembly line cut a car from 12 hours to 90 minutes and the $5 day let workers buy what they built. Honest record (the bank doesn't hagiograph): Ford's later antisemitism is part of the factual record, stated plainly if relevant, never theatricalized (§4, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK no-hagiography rule). The specific (moving line, Model T, $5 day, 'any color so long as black') is the answer.

**31. The automobile and the internal combustion engine** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Benz, Daimler invent the gasoline car · *src: Encyclopedias; history-of-technology canon*  
> Beauty-of-mechanism (§2): the engine that put the world on wheels. Karl Benz's 1885 Patent-Motorwagen; Bertha Benz's unauthorized 1888 long-distance drive that proved the car worked (a great real-person wonder hook, told on her own terms). The specific (four-stroke cycle, who built the first practical car, Bertha's drive) is the answer.

**32. Einstein and relativity** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Relativity, E=mc2, the famous physicist · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder at its purest (§2: 'Light bends around stars' is a moral_vision exemplar line — that IS the 1919 eclipse confirmation of general relativity). The patent-clerk who reimagined space and time in his 1905 'miracle year'; the bending-starlight test that made him world-famous overnight. Concrete handles over jargon (§5): the twin paradox, the train thought experiment. The specific (what bends, E=mc2 meaning, the 1919 eclipse) is the answer.

**33. The atomic bomb and the Manhattan Project** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Splitting the atom, Oppenheimer, Trinity test · *src: AP US/World; Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Hard topic handled HONESTLY, not preachy (§4): state the science and the stakes plainly — no theatrical 'was it a war crime' reveal, no glibness about the suffering (viral test, §7). Oppenheimer's 'Now I am become Death' (a real quote), the Trinity flash, Fermi's Chicago pile. American-led, ended the war. The specific (fission chain reaction, Trinity, the Oppenheimer quote) is the answer. The moral weight is carried by facts, not editorializing.

**34. Marconi, radio and broadcasting** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
Wireless radio, then television · *src: Encyclopedias; history-of-technology canon*  
> Wonder of signals through empty air into every home. Teach the real contested story precisely (the bank prizes accuracy): Marconi sent the first transatlantic signal 1901, but Tesla's prior patents were later recognized — a genuine priority dispute, stated as fact. The specific (transatlantic 'S' in Morse, the priority question, the leap to broadcasting) is the answer.

**35. Polar exploration: Amundsen, Peary, Shackleton** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium  
Race to the South and North Poles · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Courage + endurance as virtue-by-example (§2). Shackleton's Endurance crushed by ice, the 800-mile open-boat journey to South Georgia, and EVERY man brought home alive — leadership at the breaking point. Amundsen beating Scott to the South Pole 1911 (and Scott's whole party dying on the return). The specific (Shackleton's boat journey, Amundsen's dogs-vs-Scott's-ponies decision) is the answer.

**36. Everest: Hillary and Tenzing** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
First to summit Mount Everest, 1953 · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder + the partnership: a New Zealand beekeeper and a Sherpa mountaineer reached the highest point on Earth 1953. Tell it on EQUAL terms — Tenzing as a full partner, not a porter (no condescension, §4); they agreed never to say who stepped first. The specific (the summit, the gracious shared-credit detail, the South Col route) is the answer.

**37. Sputnik and the start of the Space Age** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
First satellite, 1957, Space Race begins · *src: AP US/World; Core Knowledge; cultural-literacy canon*  
> Cold War as moral conflict frame available (HISTORY_FRAMEWORK pillar 4 / moral_vision §3.4): the beeping Soviet satellite that shocked America into the Space Race — the contest between a free society and a totalitarian one (which the free society won by landing on the Moon). State plainly that the Soviet program ran on prison-camp/coerced labor where relevant (§3.1, honest record). The specific (first artificial satellite, the beep, what it triggered) is the answer.

**38. Yuri Gagarin: first human in space** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=low  
First person to orbit Earth, 1961 · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Wonder of the first human beyond the atmosphere 1961 — credit the achievement straight, on its own terms (no condescension toward the Soviet feat; honest history credits real accomplishment, §4). 'Poyekhali!' ('Let's go!'). The specific (first to orbit, single orbit ~108 min, his exclamation) is the answer. Pair-able with Sputnik/Apollo as the Space-Race arc.

**39. Apollo 11 and the Moon landing** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Armstrong, Aldrin, 'one small step,' 1969 · *src: Core Knowledge; AP US; cultural-literacy canon*  
> NAMED in moral_vision §3.6 ('put humans on the Moon') as a peak of the American experiment AND §2 American technological achievement. The peak of exploration — celebrate it fully and honestly. The 1202 alarm, Armstrong taking manual control with seconds of fuel left, 'the Eagle has landed,' 'one small step.' The specific (the landing drama, the quote, 400,000 people who built it) is the answer. Free society reaching the Moon = the Space-Race payoff.

**40. Watson, Crick, Franklin and the DNA double helix** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Discovery of DNA's structure, 1953 · *src: AP Bio/World; cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Rosalind Franklin is NAMED in moral_vision §2 virtue-by-example — the recognition-justice angle is VISION-MANDATED, not optional. Her Photo 51 X-ray crystallography was the key evidence Watson and Crick used (shown to them without her knowledge); she died at 37 before the 1962 Nobel, which can't be awarded posthumously. Frame as the discovery of life's code AND honest about the credit (stated as fact, no theatrical reveal, §4). The specific (the double helix, base-pairing, Photo 51's role) is the answer.

**41. The computer pioneers: Babbage, Lovelace, Turing** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Analytical engine, first programmer, Turing machine · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> Ideas behind modern computing + drama-of-minds (§2, HISTORY_FRAMEWORK 'Turing's apple' is a T4 hook). Babbage's never-built Analytical Engine; Ada Lovelace writing the first algorithm a century early (credit on the merits); Turing's universal machine, his Bletchley codebreaking that shortened WWII, and his tragic persecution and death — stated plainly, not theatricalized (§4). The specific (Lovelace's algorithm, the Turing machine concept, Enigma) is the answer.

**42. The transistor and the digital revolution** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Transistor, microchip, personal computer · *src: Encyclopedias; history-of-technology canon*  
> American technological substrate (moral_vision §3.6 names 'the integrated circuit'). The 1947 Bell Labs transistor (Bardeen, Brattain, Shockley) → Kilby/Noyce's integrated circuit → the chip that built the modern world. Beauty-of-mechanism: the tiny switch replacing the hot fragile vacuum tube. The specific (what the transistor does, where it was invented, the IC leap) is the answer.

**43. The Internet and the World Wide Web** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
ARPANET, Berners-Lee invents the Web · *src: Cultural-literacy canon; encyclopedias*  
> American technological substrate (moral_vision §3.6 names 'the Internet'). Teach the real precision the bank demands: the INTERNET (ARPANET 1969, packet-switching, TCP/IP) and the WEB (Berners-Lee at CERN 1989-91, who gave it away free, no patent — virtue) are DIFFERENT things kids conflate. The specific (packet-switching vs the Web, the first message 'LO', Berners-Lee's free gift) is the answer.

**44. Mary Anning, the fossil hunter of Lyme Regis** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Self-taught girl who found the first ichthyosaur and plesiosaur skeletons; supplied the science men published under their own names · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED INJECTION — moral_vision §2 names her TWICE as the controlling virtue-by-example: 'Mary Anning earning a man's recognition from a girl's beach' and among 'people who told the truth at cost.' A poor working-class girl who survived a lightning strike as a baby, hunted fossils in the crumbling Lyme Regis cliffs to feed her family, found the first complete ichthyosaur at 12 and the first plesiosaur — and watched gentleman geologists publish her finds without crediting her, barred from the Geological Society because she was a woman. Wonder + courage + virtue + honest record of who got credit. The specific find/fact is the answer.

**45. Lemaitre, the priest who proposed the Big Bang** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Belgian Catholic priest and physicist who first proposed the expanding universe and the 'primeval atom' · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED INJECTION — moral_vision §3.4 names Lemaitre explicitly as the capstone of the believer-scientists list ('Lemaitre, who proposed what became the Big Bang') and as proof the bank 'does not treat science and Christianity as opponents.' Georges Lemaitre, a Catholic PRIEST, derived the expanding universe from Einstein's equations in 1927 and proposed the 'primeval atom' — and Einstein first told him 'your math is correct but your physics is abominable,' then recanted. Hubble's data confirmed the expansion. The reversal hook: the Big Bang's father wore a cassock. The specific (expanding universe / primeval atom / Einstein's reversal) is the answer.

**46. Tycho Brahe and the war on the data** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
The naked-eye astronomer whose two decades of precise observations made Kepler's laws possible · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED INJECTION — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §7 'Drama of the great minds' names 'Tycho's silver nose' as a canonical drama hook, and the strand currently runs the astronomy-revolution arc (Copernicus-Kepler-Newton) WITHOUT the man whose 20 years of pre-telescope naked-eye data were the empirical bedrock Kepler stood on. The Danish nobleman with a metal nose (lost in a duel over a math dispute), his island observatory Uraniborg, his pet elk, his strange death — and the priceless Mars data Kepler inherited. The specific (the silver/brass nose, the precision data, what Kepler did with it) is the answer. Wonder + drama-of-minds.

**47. The decline of the Islamic Golden Age: al-Ghazali and the sack of Baghdad 1258** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
How the medieval Islamic scientific flourishing ended — al-Ghazali's anti-rationalist turn and the Mongol destruction of Baghdad's House of Wisdom · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED INJECTION — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK pillar 5 states the decline thread is REQUIRED honest history: 'al-Ghazali's Incoherence of the Philosophers (~1100) helped close the golden age's rationalist tradition; Mongol destruction of Baghdad 1258 ended the House of Wisdom... The pattern (religious orthodoxy stifling inquiry) is stated as historical fact, not editorialized.' The consensus 'Islamic Golden Age' topic covers the rise but NOT the fall. State plainly, no condescension, no triumphalism (§4): the Tigris ran black with ink from books thrown in the river 1258; why the recovery the West achieved didn't happen here. The specific cause/event is the answer.

**48. Lise Meitner and the discovery of nuclear fission** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
The physicist who explained fission and was passed over for the 1944 Nobel that went to her collaborator · *src: vision-injected*  
> VISION-MANDATED INJECTION — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §7 names 'Lise Meitner overlooked at the 1944 Nobel' as a canonical T5 drama-of-minds hook, and she is the missing scientific link AHEAD of the Manhattan Project topic: it was Meitner (a Jewish physicist who had just fled Nazi Germany) and her nephew Frisch who first explained and NAMED nuclear fission in 1938, while her collaborator Otto Hahn alone got the 1944 Chemistry Nobel. Frame the recognition-justice as fact, not theatrical reveal (§4). The specific (she explained/named fission; the Nobel that omitted her; element 109 meitnerium named for her) is the answer.

**49. Boltzmann, entropy, and the equation on the tombstone** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=low ⭐  
The physicist who founded statistical mechanics, fought for the reality of atoms, and whose entropy formula is carved on his grave · *src: vision-injected*  
> LOWER-PRIORITY VISION INJECTION — HISTORY_FRAMEWORK §7 names 'Boltzmann's tombstone S = k log W' as a canonical drama-of-minds hook. Ludwig Boltzmann statistically explained why heat flows one way (entropy), was ridiculed by colleagues who denied atoms even existed, and the formula they fought over is engraved above his name. Beauty-of-mechanism (§2) + drama-of-minds at the breaking point. The specific (entropy = why time has a direction; the tombstone equation) is the answer. Lower weight than the women-in-science injections — include if the strand wants a physics-foundations drama beat.


## Art, music & ideas (cross-era keystones) (57)

**1. Parthenon and the Greek architectural orders** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Athens Acropolis temple; Doric/Ionic/Corinthian · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Art History anchor work; cultural literacy*  
> Top-billing Pillar-1 Athens keystone — the inheritance, not a museum label. Per the Wonder Pattern, ask for the cool specific (Phidias' colossal Athena, the entasis curve that has NO straight lines so it reads straight, the marbles painted in bright color not white), never 'name the order' rote. Frame as the template every later Western column descends from. Drama-available: if the stem invokes the Persian sack of the Acropolis, the answer is the rebuilt temple's trick, not a date.

**2. Roman Pantheon and the concrete dome** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Hadrian's domed temple; oculus; lost concrete · *src: AP Art History; Core Knowledge ancient Rome*  
> Pillar-1 Rome keystone explicitly named in framework ('Pantheon's still-standing concrete dome'). Celebrate the beauty of mechanism (moral_vision §2): WHY it still stands — the recipe (volcanic pozzolana, lighter aggregate up top), the lost formula that left it unmatched for 1,300 years until Brunelleschi. The oculus + rain-drain holes is the kid hook. This is the same lost-knowledge thread that sets up Brunelleschi.

**3. Gothic cathedrals (Chartres / Notre-Dame)** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
pointed arch, flying buttress, rose windows, stained glass · *src: Core Knowledge medieval; AP Euro; AP Art History*  
> CORE of moral_vision §3.4 — medieval cathedrals as the monastic/ecclesiastical achievement that built the modern world, faith and engineering reaching toward light. NO smug 'people used to believe' voice (§4). Framework names Chartres rose windows + the Madonna's Veil relic, Sainte-Chapelle. Cool-fact answers: the flying buttress that lets walls become glass; Chartres' surviving medieval blue. Notre-Dame's 2019 fire + restoration is in scope (settled, not partisan) and a vivid hook.

**4. Brunelleschi's dome of Florence** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
1436 Santa Maria del Fiore; herringbone brick; no scaffold · *src: Core Knowledge Renaissance; HISTORY_TEMPLATES exemplar*  
> THE canonical worked exemplar of the whole bank (HISTORY_TEMPLATES §1) — one figure, five tier questions. Highest weight. Each cool fact gets its own answer: lost-Roman-formula (T1), herringbone (T2), the egg demonstration + 'you could have built my dome too' (T3), goldsmith-by-training (T4), still the largest masonry dome (T5). Engineering-feat-that-launched-the-Renaissance framing. NEVER stuff all five into one stem.

**5. St. Peter's Basilica and the Renaissance/Baroque papal Rome** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Bramante/Michelangelo dome; Bernini's colonnade · *src: AP Euro; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Pillar-1 Renaissance-into-Baroque keystone. Celebrate the ambition straight; Christian heritage as historical foundation, not advertised (§4, no smug-believer voice). Cool-fact answers: Michelangelo taking over the dome in his 70s for free 'for the love of God'; Bernini's colonnade as 'the arms of the Church embracing the faithful' (his own image). Bramante nicknamed 'Maestro Ruinante' for demolishing old St. Peter's is a vivid hook.

**6. Leonardo da Vinci — Mona Lisa and Last Supper** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
sfumato; vanishing-point Supper; the Renaissance polymath · *src: Core Knowledge grade 5 Renaissance; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Archetypal Renaissance genius — Pillar-1 high weight + 'drama of the great minds' (Pillar 6). Cool-fact answers over rote: the mirror-writing notebooks; the Last Supper painted in failing experimental tempera so it began flaking in his lifetime; sfumato = 'smoke,' no visible lines. The polymath wonder (flying machines, anatomy dissections) is the §2 'ordinary ingenuity' lead. Avoid 'who painted' rote.

**7. Michelangelo — Sistine ceiling and David** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
4-year ceiling; Creation of Adam; 17-foot David · *src: Core Knowledge grade 5; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Framework explicitly names 'Michelangelo's Sistine ceiling + David.' High weight. Drama-of-the-mind hooks: he insisted he was a sculptor not a painter and resented the ceiling commission; the David carved from a 'ruined' block other sculptors had abandoned; the near-touching fingers of Adam and God. Ask for the specific, not 'who sculpted David.'

**8. Raphael — School of Athens** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Vatican fresco gathering all Greek philosophers · *src: AP Art History; AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> Perfect Pillar-1 synthesis — the Vatican itself honoring the Athens-to-Rome philosophical inheritance the bank champions (Athens→Jerusalem→Rome lineage). Cool-fact answer: Plato points up (the Forms) while Aristotle gestures down (the world) — the whole argument of Western philosophy in two hands; Raphael painted Leonardo's face onto Plato and slipped his own face into the crowd.

**9. Donatello and Ghiberti — early Renaissance sculpture** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
first freestanding nude David; Gates of Paradise · *src: AP Art History; Core Knowledge Renaissance*  
> Framework names both ('Ghiberti's Gates of Paradise, Donatello's David — first freestanding nude since antiquity'). The revival-of-classical-antiquity thread of Pillar 1. Cool-fact answer: Donatello's bronze David was the first free-standing nude statue cast since the Romans, a thousand-year gap; Michelangelo reportedly named Ghiberti's doors the 'Gates of Paradise.'

**10. Botticelli — Birth of Venus and Primavera** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium  
Medici Florence; mythological subjects revived · *src: AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Pillar-1 Medici-Florence keystone — the rebirth of classical mythology as subject matter. Present on its own terms. Cool-fact hook: Venus standing on a scallop shell as the first large mythological nude of the Renaissance; the Medici patronage that funded it. Avoid 'who painted' rote — ask the wondrous specific.

**11. Jan van Eyck and the Northern Renaissance** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Arnolfini Portrait; oil-paint innovation; minute detail · *src: AP Art History; AP Euro*  
> Pillar-1 Northern thread — ordinary ingenuity (moral_vision §2): the oil-paint technique that let the North out-detail Italian fresco. Cool-fact answer: the convex mirror in the Arnolfini Portrait reflecting the whole room AND two figures in the doorway, with 'Jan van Eyck was here' signed above it like a witness. Mechanism-of-the-medium wonder, not attribution.

**12. Albrecht Durer — Northern Renaissance printmaking** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
engravings; self-portraits; Praying Hands · *src: AP Euro; AP Art History*  
> Where the print revolution (Gutenberg thread) meets Renaissance draftsmanship — ideas-strand crossover. Cool-fact hook: Durer's 1500 self-portrait deliberately composed to look like the traditional image of Christ (a statement about the artist as creator); his rhino woodcut drawn from a description he never saw in person, yet it shaped how Europe pictured rhinos for centuries. Present straight, no theatrical reveal.

**13. Caravaggio and the Baroque (tenebrism)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
dramatic light-dark; violent realism; the brawling painter · *src: AP Art History; AP Euro*  
> Pillar-1 Baroque + drama-of-the-mind (the brawling painter who killed a man and fled with a death sentence, painting his own guilt into a severed head). Cool-fact answer: tenebrism = the single shaft of light out of black that he invented and that defined Baroque drama. The violent biography is a real-fact hook, not a 'secretly bad' reveal (anti-pattern guard).

**14. Bernini — Baroque sculpture** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Ecstasy of St. Teresa; Apollo and Daphne; marble made flesh · *src: AP Art History; AP Euro*  
> Framework names 'Bernini's Ecstasy of St. Teresa + Apollo and Daphne marble miracle.' The beauty-of-mechanism lead (§2): HOW marble was carved to look like fingers pressing into soft flesh, or Daphne's fingertips turning to laurel leaves mid-run. Treat the religious subject (St. Teresa's vision) on its own terms, no smug voice.

**15. Rembrandt — Dutch Golden Age** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Night Watch; Anatomy Lesson; self-portraits; light · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Rembrandt's Night Watch + Anatomy Lesson.' Pillar-1 Dutch keystone. Cool-fact answers: The Night Watch isn't a night scene — centuries of darkened varnish fooled everyone; it was trimmed on all sides to fit a wall, cropping figures out. The self-portrait series tracking one face from cocky youth to bankrupt old age = the human-at-the-breaking-point wonder.

**16. Vermeer — Girl with a Pearl Earring** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Dutch domestic light; tiny output; camera obscura · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names Vermeer explicitly. Ordinary-ingenuity + beauty-of-mechanism: the possible camera-obscura use; barely ~34 surviving paintings from a whole career; the 'Dutch Mona Lisa' that is a tronie (a type-study), not a portrait of a known person. Quiet luminous interiors as the hook — ask the specific, not attribution.

**17. Velazquez — Las Meninas** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Spanish court; mirror; painter in the frame · *src: AP Art History anchor work; AP Euro*  
> Pillar-1 Spanish-Golden-Age peak. Cool-fact answer: the painting-about-painting — Velazquez paints HIMSELF at his easel, and the king and queen appear only as a small reflection in the back mirror, putting the royal couple (and the viewer) in the same spot. The puzzle of who is looking at whom is the wonder. Present on its own terms.

**18. Jacques-Louis David and Neoclassicism** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
Oath of the Horatii; Death of Marat; Revolution's painter · *src: AP Art History anchor work; AP Euro*  
> Art as revolutionary propaganda — the ideas-strand crossover into the French Revolution (Pillar 1 Enlightenment/Terror thread, where Lavoisier was guillotined). Honest, not preachy (§4): David was the Revolution's image-maker AND voted for the king's death AND later painted Napoleon's coronation — state plainly, no editorial. Cool-fact: Death of Marat as a deliberate secular pietà, idealizing a real political murder.

**19. Goya — The Third of May 1808** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium  
firing-squad painting; the dark late works · *src: AP Art History; AP Euro*  
> The first modern protest painting of war's horror — fits the bank's honest-about-violence stance (§4, state what happened, don't twist it). Cool-fact answer: the central victim's pose echoes the crucifixion, with a wound in the hand, turning a Napoleonic firing-squad atrocity into something timeless. The 'Black Paintings' Goya painted straight onto his own house walls in deaf isolation = drama-of-the-mind hook.

**20. Romanticism — Delacroix and Turner** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Liberty Leading the People; Turner's storms of light · *src: AP Euro; AP Art History*  
> Framework names 'Turner, Delacroix's Liberty Leading the People.' Pillar-1 19th-c thread — emotion and color over Neoclassical order. Cool-fact hooks: Liberty as a bare-breasted allegory striding over the barricade dead (often miscalled a French Revolution scene — it's 1830); Turner reportedly lashing himself to a ship's mast to paint a real snowstorm. Ask the specific.

**21. Impressionism — Monet and the movement** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Impression Sunrise; plein air; water lilies; broken color · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> The break into modern art — Pillar-1 keystone. Surprise-reversal hook (§2): 'Impressionism' began as an INSULT from a hostile critic mocking Monet's 'Impression, Sunrise'; the painters kept the slur as their name. Plein-air = made possible by the new portable paint tube (ordinary-ingenuity mechanism). Cool-fact answer over 'who painted.'

**22. Van Gogh — Starry Night and the ear** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Post-Impressionist; sold one painting; cut his ear; suicide · *src: Core Knowledge modern American/European; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Van Gogh (Starry Night, ear).' Tortured-genius archetype = drama-of-the-mind at a kid-accessible level. Honest, not theatricalized (§4): the ear and the suicide are stated as fact, not staged as a reveal; sold essentially one painting alive yet now among the most famous painters ever = surprise-reversal. Starry Night painted from a sanatorium window. Mind the strict drama-available rule — ask about the act, not a venue/date.

**23. Picasso and Cubism** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Les Demoiselles; Guernica; fractured multiple viewpoints · *src: Core Knowledge modern art; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Picasso's Guernica 1937 (response to the German bombing).' High weight — modern-art revolution AND a moral-witness painting against the bombing of civilians (fits the bank's honest-about-atrocity stance). Cubism = showing multiple viewpoints at once is the mechanism hook. The legend of Picasso telling a Nazi officer 'No, YOU did' about Guernica is the cool-fact answer. Distinguish classic Picasso from any modern-slop framing.

**24. Rodin and modern sculpture — The Thinker** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low  
Rodin's Thinker and Burghers of Calais · *src: Core Knowledge; cultural literacy*  
> Gateway to modern sculpture. Cool-fact answer: The Thinker was originally a small figure of the poet Dante perched atop the Gates of Hell, brooding over the damned, before being enlarged into a standalone icon. The Burghers of Calais set at ground level (no heroic pedestal) so the viewer meets the condemned men eye to eye = the deliberate anti-monument wonder. (Note: consensus title mis-tags 'Monet'; topic is Rodin.)

**25. Surrealism — Dali's melting clocks** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low  
Persistence of Memory; dream imagery · *src: Core Knowledge modern; cultural literacy*  
> Modern-movement keystone kids recognize. Cool-fact hook: Dali said the soft melting watches came from staring at a runny Camembert cheese; the dreamlike imagery is the draw. Keep it on the art/mechanism level; avoid any current-politics or edgy-biography drift. Ask the specific, not attribution.

**26. American modern art — Hopper, Pollock, Warhol** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium  
Nighthawks; drip painting; pop-art soup cans · *src: Core Knowledge American art; cultural literacy*  
> 20th-c American contribution — fits the celebrate-the-American-experiment thread (moral_vision §2/§3.6) at the cultural level. Cool-fact answers: Pollock's drip technique = painting with the canvas on the FLOOR, walking around it, never touching brush to cloth; Hopper's Nighthawks diner with no visible door; Warhol's exactly-32 soup-can canvases for the 32 varieties. Present straight; no ideology.

**27. Lascaux / Altamira cave paintings** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
Paleolithic animal paintings; discovered by accident · *src: AP Art History (Global Prehistory); Core Knowledge*  
> The origin of human art — pure wonder (§2): humans 17,000+ years ago made deliberate beautiful images. Cool-fact answer: Lascaux found in 1940 by four teenagers and a dog chasing a rabbit down a hole; Altamira's bison so sophisticated that scholars first dismissed them as a hoax — too good to be 'primitive.' That dismissal-then-vindication is the surprise-reversal. NO condescension to the past (§4).

**28. Egyptian art and the pyramids of Giza** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
profile convention; Great Pyramid; Sphinx; King Tut's mask · *src: Core Knowledge ancient Egypt; AP Art History; cultural literacy*  
> Monumental ancient art every kid knows — present on its own terms (§4, no exotic-foil framing). Framework names 'Tutankhamun's tomb discovered by Carter 1922 (yes, wonderful things).' That quote is the Tier-1 Named-Things answer. Cool-fact: the strict profile-convention (head and legs sideways, eye and shoulders front) held for 3,000 years; the Great Pyramid was the tallest human structure for ~3,800 years.

**29. Illuminated manuscripts (Book of Kells / Lindisfarne)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
hand-painted gospels; monastic gold-leaf labor · *src: Core Knowledge medieval; AP Art History*  
> How books were art before printing — DIRECTLY the moral_vision §3.4 thread (the unnamed monks who copied through plague years, §2 'courage in pursuit of truth'). Framework names Book of Kells + Lindisfarne Gospels. Cool-fact answer: a single carpet-page could take a monk months, with pigment ground from lapis lazuli traded all the way from Afghanistan. Sets up the Gutenberg contrast. Christian heritage on its own terms.

**30. Gutenberg's printing press and the printed book** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
movable type ~1450; 42-line Bible; idea revolution · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> THE ideas-revolution keystone of the strand — mass literacy, the information revolution of its age, the engine of the Reformation that follows. High weight. Framework names 'Gutenberg's 42-line Bible 1450 (the first printed Bible).' Cool-fact answers: movable metal type let one press do the work of dozens of scribes; he went bankrupt and lost the business to his investor Fust before the Bibles even sold. Celebrate the real human progress.

**31. Islamic art and architecture (Alhambra / Dome of the Rock)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
arabesque; geometric tile; calligraphy; no figures · *src: Core Knowledge Islamic art (explicit); AP World; AP Art History*  
> Non-Western tradition ON ITS OWN TERMS (§4, NOT exotic foil) — the aniconic tradition of pattern and calligraphy. Cool-fact answer: because figural images were avoided in sacred art, Islamic artists pushed geometry and calligraphy to dazzling heights — Alhambra tilework contains nearly all the mathematically possible symmetry patterns centuries before mathematicians catalogued them. Framework's honest decline-thread (al-Ghazali) belongs in context, not as the art's framing. Honest comparison is not condescension.

**32. Great Mosque of Cordoba** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=low  
horseshoe arches; Moorish Spain; mosque-cathedral · *src: AP Art History anchor work*  
> Iconic Islamic-Spain architecture, on its own terms. Cool-fact answer: the forest of double-tier red-and-white horseshoe arches (over 850 columns) creating an endless-hall illusion; a Renaissance cathedral was later built right into its center, and Charles V reportedly regretted it ('you have destroyed something unique'). The mosque-then-cathedral layering is stated as historical fact, no editorial.

**33. Taj Mahal** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Mughal marble tomb 1632-53; Shah Jahan for Mumtaz · *src: AP World; AP Art History anchor work; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Taj Mahal 1632-53 (Shah Jahan for Mumtaz).' Mughal achievement on its own terms — the love-story monument. Cool-fact answers: built as a tomb for the wife who died bearing their 14th child; the perfect symmetry broken only by Shah Jahan's own later-added tomb beside hers; inlaid with semi-precious stones that glow at dawn. Present straight as a world masterpiece.

**34. Chinese art — landscape painting, calligraphy, porcelain** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
ink landscapes; the brush; blue-and-white porcelain · *src: Core Knowledge Chinese art (explicit); AP World*  
> Major non-Western tradition on its own terms (§4) — the brush and the ceramic. Cool-fact answers: a master ink landscape leaves vast areas of blank silk as mist and water, where what's NOT painted carries the scene; blue-and-white porcelain was so prized that 'china' became the English word for all fine ceramic. Substantive civilization, not exotic foil.

**35. Terracotta Army of Qin Shi Huang** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
8,000 unique clay soldiers buried; discovered 1974 · *src: AP World; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Qin Shi Huang + terracotta army.' Staggering ancient sculpture program — vivid kid hook. Cool-fact answer: every one of the ~8,000 soldiers has a DIFFERENT face, as if modeled on real men; found in 1974 by farmers digging a well; they were originally painted in bright colors that flaked away within minutes of hitting the air. On its own terms, no exotic framing.

**36. Japanese art — Hokusai's Great Wave and woodblock prints** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
ukiyo-e; the Great Wave; influence on Impressionists · *src: Core Knowledge Japanese art (explicit); AP Art History*  
> Non-Western tradition on its own terms PLUS the East-to-West influence thread the framework prizes (these prints shaped Monet/Van Gogh — connects to the Impressionism cell). Cool-fact answer: the tiny Mount Fuji sitting calm in the distance UNDER the towering claw of the wave; mass-produced cheap woodblock prints, not elite paintings, that ended up remaking European art. Ask the specific.

**37. African art — Benin bronzes and masks** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
lost-wax bronze plaques; ritual masks; looted 1897 · *src: Core Knowledge African art (explicit); AP Art History (Africa)*  
> Sophisticated metalwork tradition on its own terms — celebrate the achievement (the lost-wax casting rivaled anything in Europe, shattering the 'primitive Africa' myth). The honest record (framework Pillar 5): the bronzes were looted in the 1897 British Punitive Expedition, whose altars were 'piled with skulls' — state BOTH the artistic mastery AND the brutal context plainly, no editorial. Repatriation is a fact, not an editorial verdict (avoid current-politics drift).

**38. Pre-Columbian monumental art (Maya / Aztec / Moai)** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Mayan temples; Aztec calendar stone; Easter Island heads · *src: AP Art History (Indigenous Americas, Pacific); Core Knowledge*  
> New-World + Pacific stone traditions on their own terms (§4). Cool-fact answers: the Aztec Sun Stone packing a whole cosmology into carved rings; the Moai 'walked' upright into place (rocked side to side with ropes, as modern experiments showed). The Aztec honest-brutality record (Templo Mayor sacrifice) belongs to the monuments/civilization cells, not forced into every art question — keep THIS cell on the artistry, state context plainly where relevant.

**39. Gregorian chant and medieval sacred music** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
monophonic plainchant; Hildegard of Bingen; notation born · *src: Core Knowledge music; AP Euro*  
> Where Western WRITTEN music begins — moral_vision §3.4 Christian-heritage thread + framework's named 'Hildegard of Bingen, Gregorian chant, Notre-Dame school polyphony.' Cool-fact answer: monks needed to remember hundreds of melodies, so they invented the staff and notation — the reason we can still play 1,000-year-old music. Hildegard = a 12th-c abbess composing visionary music + the drama-of-the-mind hook (a woman whose authority the Church recognized). On its own terms, no smug voice.

**40. Bach — Baroque counterpoint** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
St. Matthew Passion; fugues; revived by Mendelssohn 1829 · *src: Core Knowledge music (composers); cultural literacy*  
> Framework names the full arc: 'Bach (St. Matthew Passion forgotten 1727 then revived by 20-year-old Mendelssohn 1829).' High weight, foundational composer. Beauty-of-mechanism (§2): WHY the fugues feel inevitable — a single theme chased through every voice. Surprise-reversal: Bach was half-forgotten as old-fashioned until the teenage Mendelssohn resurrected the St. Matthew Passion. Cool-fact answers over 'who composed.'

**41. Handel — Messiah and the Hallelujah Chorus** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=medium ⭐  
oratorio; standing tradition; written in 24 days · *src: Core Knowledge music; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Handel's Hallelujah Chorus.' Most-performed choral work, instantly familiar. Cool-fact answers: the whole Messiah composed in about 24 days in a near-trance; the audience-standing tradition said to start when King George II rose during the Hallelujah Chorus. Present straight. Number ('24 days') only works if the stem CONSTRUCTS it as the shattered-expectation payoff (Tier-4 rule).

**42. Mozart — the prodigy composer** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
child genius; Don Giovanni; unfinished Requiem; died at 35 · *src: Core Knowledge music; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names 'Mozart (prodigy, Don Giovanni, Requiem unfinished dead at 35).' Archetypal prodigy = drama-of-the-mind, high weight. Cool-fact answers: composing full symphonies before age 10 and touring Europe's courts as a child; the Requiem left unfinished at his death at 35, commissioned anonymously by a stranger he came to believe was Death itself. Mind the strict drama-available rule — ask the act/object, not a date.

**43. Beethoven — the deaf composer and the Ninth** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
going deaf; Fifth's four notes; Ode to Joy premiere 1824 · *src: Core Knowledge music; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names it with the cool detail: 'Beethoven's deaf 9th-symphony premiere 1824 (contralto turned him to see the applause).' THAT is the Tier-1 Named-Things/Vivid-Action answer — he couldn't hear the ovation and had to be turned around to see it. High weight, deaf-genius drama-of-the-mind. The four-note 'fate' opening of the Fifth is the kid hook. Bridge to Romantic music.

**44. Romantic-era composers — Chopin, Tchaikovsky, Wagner** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
Chopin's Polish exile; 1812 Overture cannons; Wagner's Ring · *src: Core Knowledge music; AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> Framework names all three with cool details: 'Chopin (Polish exile, dying TB, buried with Polish soil), Wagner's Ring + Bayreuth, Tchaikovsky's 1812 Overture with real cannons.' Each detail is the answer: real cannons scored into the 1812; Chopin's heart removed and smuggled back to Poland in a jar; Wagner building his own opera house for one 16-hour cycle. Drama-of-the-mind + Named-Things. Ask the specific, not attribution.

**45. Stravinsky — Rite of Spring riot 1913** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
audience riot at the premiere; modernist rhythm · *src: AP Euro; cultural literacy; HISTORY_TEMPLATES*  
> Framework names 'Stravinsky's Rite of Spring riot 1913' in BOTH Pillar 1 and Pillar 6 — the moment modern music broke from tradition. The riot IS the cool-fact answer (the audience came to BLOWS over the pounding dissonant rhythm and primitive choreography). Drama-available: the violence in the stem yields the riot, not the venue/date. Surprise-reversal: a ballet that caused a literal brawl.

**46. Birth of jazz — Louis Armstrong and New Orleans** — standard (~5q) · `ladder` · T2-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
blues roots; improvisation; Armstrong; the American art form · *src: Core Knowledge music (jazz explicit); cultural literacy*  
> America's original art form — fits the celebrate-the-American-experiment thread (moral_vision §2/§3.6) at the cultural level; an achievement born of Black American musicians. Cool-fact answer: improvisation = inventing the melody live, never the same twice; Armstrong's trumpet and gravel voice making the soloist the star. Present the achievement straight, no ideology. Ask the specific.

**47. American vocal traditions — spirituals, folk, patriotic song** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T3 · wt=medium  
spirituals; folk songs; Star-Spangled Banner · *src: Core Knowledge music (explicit: spirituals/folk/patriotic)*  
> The shared American songbook — celebrate-the-American-experiment thread. Cool-fact answers: spirituals like 'Wade in the Water' carrying coded escape directions for the Underground Railroad; the Star-Spangled Banner's tune borrowed from an old British drinking song; written by Francis Scott Key watching Fort McHenry survive the night (connects to War of 1812 cell). Honest record (the spirituals' slavery origin) stated plainly.

**48. Families of instruments and the orchestra** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T1-T2 · wt=low  
strings/brass/woodwind/percussion; Peter and the Wolf · *src: Core Knowledge music (instrument families explicit)*  
> Basic musical literacy — how orchestral music is built. Lowest weight in the strand (foundational, not a wonder keystone), but legitimate Core Knowledge. Cool-fact hook to avoid dry definition: Prokofiev's 'Peter and the Wolf' assigns each character a specific instrument (the duck = oboe, the wolf = French horns) so kids learn the families by story. Keep it kid-accessible; one concrete handle.

**49. Homer's epics — Iliad and Odyssey** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T4 · wt=high ⭐  
Trojan War; Odysseus' journey; oral epic; Western canon's start · *src: Core Knowledge grade 6 (Greece); cultural literacy*  
> Where Western literature BEGINS — top-billing ideas-strand keystone (Athens inheritance). High weight. Cool-fact answers: the Trojan Horse trick; Odysseus blinding the Cyclops by calling himself 'Nobody'; composed and memorized orally for centuries before being written down. The oral-memory feat = §2 ordinary-ingenuity wonder. Present straight.

**50. Greek drama — tragedy and comedy** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
Sophocles; Oedipus; theater of Dionysus; masks · *src: Core Knowledge; AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> Origin of Western theater — Athens-inheritance ideas keystone. Cool-fact answers: every role played by masked men, with giant masks that doubled as megaphones to carry the voice up a stone hillside seating 17,000; the same Oedipus story that gave us 'tragic flaw.' Present on its own terms, no condescension to the ancient world (§4).

**51. Dante — The Divine Comedy** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Inferno's nine circles; Italian vernacular; the afterlife journey · *src: Core Knowledge medieval; cultural literacy*  
> Medieval Christian imagination's masterpiece that also SHAPED the Italian language — moral_vision §3.4 thread (Christian heritage as intellectual foundation) treated on its own terms, no smug voice (§4). Cool-fact answers: Dante wrote it in everyday Italian instead of scholarly Latin, helping fix the language; he placed his own political enemies (and a pope) into specific circles of Hell by name. The nine descending circles is the kid hook.

**52. Shakespeare — the plays and the language** — **DEEP** (~12q) · `ladder` · T1-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
Globe Theatre; Hamlet/Macbeth/Romeo; coined words · *src: Core Knowledge (multiple grades); cultural literacy*  
> Greatest writer in English — top-billing ideas keystone, high weight. Cool-fact answers: he coined or first-recorded hundreds of words still used daily ('eyeball,' 'lonely,' 'swagger') and phrases ('wild-goose chase,' 'break the ice'); boys played all the women's roles at the Globe; the open-air theatre's 'groundlings' stood in the pit for a penny. Ask the wondrous specific, never 'who wrote' rote.

**53. Cervantes — Don Quixote** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium  
tilting at windmills; first modern novel; Spanish Golden Age · *src: AP Euro; cultural literacy*  
> The first modern novel + an idiom kids still use ('tilting at windmills,' 'quixotic'). Spanish Golden Age. Cool-fact answer: the old knight charging windmills he believes are giants; Cervantes himself lost the use of his left hand at the Battle of Lepanto (connects to the military strand) and later wrote the book partly while imprisoned. Present straight; ask the specific.

**54. Mozart vs. Salieri — the poisoning myth vs. the record** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=medium ⭐  
the real Salieri (respected court composer who taught Beethoven/Schubert/Liszt, NOT a murderer); myth vs. history · *src: vision-injected*  
> Behind-the-scenes wonder REQUIRED by HISTORY_TEMPLATES §1 (popular myth vs. historical reality), applied to music. The 'Salieri poisoned Mozart' story (from the play/film Amadeus) is FALSE — Salieri was a successful, respected Vienna court composer. Tell the truer story without tearing down: the professional rivalry was real and ordinary; the murder is legend. Teaches kids that cultural narratives are constructed. Honest, not cynical (§4). Cool-fact answer = the real students he taught, or that the poisoning never happened.

**55. Shostakovich's Fifth — music coded under Stalin** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T4-T5 · wt=high ⭐  
the 1937 'forced rejoicing' finale; composing under threat of the Gulag after official denunciation · *src: vision-injected*  
> The communist-atrocity-truth-telling pillar (moral_vision §3.1; framework Pillar 4, HEAVY by design) applied to the ARTS — courage in pursuit of truth (§2). Shostakovich wrote his Fifth after Stalin's regime denounced him in Pravda and friends were vanishing into the Gulag; the 'triumphant' finale is widely heard as hollow, forced rejoicing 'under threat.' Show the artist surviving under terror — the human at the breaking point. State the Stalinist context plainly, no false equivalence. Cool-fact answer = the hidden meaning of the finale, or the denunciation that preceded it.

**56. Monteverdi and the birth of opera (Palestrina to Orfeo)** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T3-T4 · wt=medium ⭐  
Palestrina saving polyphony at Trent; Monteverdi's 'Orfeo' 1607 as the first enduring opera · *src: vision-injected*  
> Fills the Renaissance/early-Baroque MUSIC gap between Gregorian chant and Bach. Cool-fact answers: the legend that Palestrina's Mass single-handedly persuaded the Council of Trent NOT to ban complex polyphony from church (saving a whole musical tradition); Monteverdi's Orfeo turning the Greek Orpheus myth into the first opera still staged today. Christian-heritage music thread (§3.4) + Greek-myth-revival thread, both on their own terms.

**57. Eiffel Tower — the structure Paris planned to tear down** — standalone (~2q) · `standalone` · T2-T3 · wt=low ⭐  
1889 World's Fair iron tower; the artists' petition against it; built to be temporary · *src: vision-injected*  
> Framework explicitly names 'Eiffel Tower 1889 (planned for demolition).' Surprise-reversal wonder (§2): the most beloved monument in France was built as a temporary fair exhibit, denounced by leading artists and writers as a hideous iron eyesore, and slated for demolition after 20 years — saved only because its height made it useful as a radio/wireless antenna. Engineering-as-art crossover. Cool-fact answer = the 'planned to be torn down, saved by radio' reveal.
